## PREFACE.

---

Even a brief observation of the opinions held by educated Chinese people, regarding how their government and customs compare to those of other countries, reveals a widespread belief that China far surpasses all other nations in historical age. Consequently, they believe they possess a more ancient and proven civilization—both political and social—which they claim has given them the accumulated wisdom of many ages. Therefore, they feel entitled to be considered superior to other peoples whose national experience they say covers a much shorter period of time.

Such an impression, even if it were based on truth, would naturally create an excessive feeling of superiority over those who are believed to lack such advantages. This is similar to how the Egyptian priests of Sais felt toward the Greeks; they told the statesman Solon <note>Solon (c. 630 – c. 560 BC) was a famous Athenian lawmaker; according to Plato, Egyptian priests told him that the Greeks had no ancient history and were "but children" in knowledge.</note> that the Greeks were merely children in their knowledge of the past. However, as will be shown, this Chinese assumption of superiority is a purely imaginary feeling of self-esteem without any real foundation. Furthermore, due to their isolation from the rest

<summary>The author argues that the Chinese belief in their nation's superior antiquity and wisdom is a baseless sentiment used to justify feelings of cultural superiority over other nations.</summary>
<keywords>China, historical antiquity, civilization, national identity, Solon, Sais, cultural superiority, isolationism</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that China's isolation and untested claims of ancient superiority lead to diplomatic arrogance and conflict with foreign powers.</meta>

of the world. Because of this imaginary and doubtful sense of superiority—which the Chinese have never tested against other cultures—their attitude tends to turn into arrogance, with all its unfortunate results. This arrogance becomes even more obvious because their claim to such superiority is either accepted through the apathy of others or is not firmly denied by the nations who deal with them. These foreign nations have too readily accepted and believed in the authenticity of Chinese history, science, and timelines <note>original: "chronology"</note>. 

Lord Elgin, in his letter to Lord Clarendon <note>George Villiers, 4th Earl of Clarendon (1800–1870), served as the British Foreign Secretary during the mid-19th century.</note> on July 9, 1857, expressed his opinion on this subject:

> "I believe it must be obvious to everyone who has carefully studied our relations with China that the primary cause of our difficulties with that country is the Peking <note>original: "Pekin," the older spelling for Beijing</note> court's stubborn refusal to treat other nations as *equals*."

Sir Frederick Bruce <note>Sir Frederick Bruce (1814–1867) was a British diplomat and the first British Minister to China following the Second Opium War.</note>, in a letter to Lord Malmesbury on July 13, 1859, wrote:

> "Foreign citizens residing in China are portrayed as members of barbarian tribes who are *lacking in civilization*."

Many of the political conflicts that have emerged between China and other nations have grown from this very sense of superiority on the part of the Chinese. Furthermore, much of the difficulty experienced in resolving these conflicts has been caused by their unwillingness or inability to reasonably

<summary>The author argues that China's refusal to treat other nations as equals, fueled by an untested belief in their own superior civilization, is the root cause of diplomatic conflict.</summary>
<keywords>China, Lord Elgin, Lord Clarendon, Sir Frederick Bruce, diplomacy, cultural superiority, Beijing, international relations, arrogance</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation for writing the book, focusing on how certain Western writers have enabled Chinese claims of antiquity and how the author aims to debunk these claims by making foreign research accessible in English.</meta>

challenge <note>original: "contest"</note> this sentiment. This issue also arises because these ideas have found some encouragement and support from various writers who, having lived closely among the Chinese, were incautiously led to accept their claims of superior antiquity and civilization too easily. This resulted from an excessive admiration for Chinese institutions and a readiness to believe <note>original: "credulousness"</note> in the alleged ancient origins of their timeline <note>original: "chronology"</note>.

This false assumption of superiority, based on an alleged antiquity, has been—and remains—a major obstacle <note>original: "bar"</note> to the Chinese people's acceptance of the truths of Christianity. Consequently, the civilizing power of the Christian view of humanity and its mutual social relationships is lost to the Chinese.

Therefore, with the goal of correcting this unreasonable view of Chinese history, the following research has been undertaken. It investigates the questions surrounding Chinese assumptions and attempts to show that their claims are groundless.

Since most works dealing with Chinese timelines and history are written in French or other foreign languages—which are difficult to obtain nowadays—and because it is desirable to make them more accessible to English readers through translations of relevant sections, this research has been compiled with those needs in mind. The goal is to provide an English-language reference for information and study regarding...

<summary>The author argues that Westerners have mistakenly supported Chinese claims of ancient superiority, which hinders the spread of Christianity; he intends to debunk these claims using translated foreign research.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, detailing the intended audience for the work—specifically officials and missionaries—and his goal of providing a consolidated English resource to counter exaggerated claims about Chinese history.</meta>

<page-num>vi.</page-num>

...for all people who, in an official or private capacity, come into contact with the educated class of Chinese people. It is also intended for Christian missionaries, for whom a true understanding of the history of the people they seek to convert will undoubtedly be useful. Furthermore, since many English-language works are not easily accessible to everyone, I have included extracts from them here. This ensures that everything scholars of Chinese literature and history have presented <note>original: "adduced"</note> regarding the timeline <note>original: "chronology"</note> may be gathered into a single volume and made easily available.

If this work proves useful in any way by encouraging a critical and accurate understanding of Chinese antiquity and civilization, and by countering the excessive sympathy created by the Chinese people's skillful and artificial portrayal of their own history, then I will be fully rewarded for the extensive reading and thought this research required. Throughout the entire course of my investigation, I have kept in mind the words of Sir William Drummond:

> "The writer who attempts to distinguish truth from fiction in ancient history takes on a task that is more likely to be exhausting <note>original: "laborious"</note> for himself than pleasing to others."

*Origins* <note>original: "Origines"</note> Volume 1, Preface, page 8.

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

---

## CHAPTER I.

### *General View of the Question.*

To begin with a clear understanding of what Chinese chronology actually is, and to clarify what is meant in the following investigation, it is necessary to state at the very beginning <note>original: "in limine"</note> that there is no fundamental <note>original: "generic"</note> difference between the system the Chinese use and the systems used by other nations to record the sequence of events over time throughout their history.

The specific aspect of Chinese chronology that we will examine here is its unsupported claim <note>original: "pretension"</note> to be an authentic record of events in China. However, a distinction must be made between two categories: first, the clearly mythical and legendary ages which the Chinese—like other Eastern nations—try to calculate using improbable lengths of time; and second, the more plausible sequence of events which, when compared to the established timelines of other ancient nations, does not appear to be impossible or contradictory to their history.

<summary>This chapter introduces the study of Chinese chronology, distinguishing between the legendary, mythical eras of the past and the more plausible historical records that align with other ancient civilizations.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese chronology, historical authenticity, mythical ages, Eastern nations, temporal cycles, historical methodology</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the general concept of Chinese chronology, distinguishing between mythical legends and plausible historical records. It established that the author intends to critically evaluate Chinese claims of antiquity by comparing them with other ancient civilizations.</meta>

->CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

---

Therefore, the eras preceding the reign of the legendary Chinese monarch Fu-hi, as well as those of his immediate predecessors and successors, are not considered worthy of critical examination here. 
<margin>✓</margin>
Even learned <note>original: "erudite"</note> Chinese scholars exclude these periods from any reliable and accurate chronology of China. This research focuses solely on the chronology that begins with the Yellow Emperor <note>original: "Hwangti"</note> in 2704 <term>BCE</term>. This is the earliest period that Chinese scholars agree can be reasonably discussed, and it may be treated as the starting point of the Chinese era, beginning with his reign.

Strictly speaking, the Chinese do not have a precise era comparable to the Era of Nabonassar <note>A chronological era starting in 747 BCE, used by ancient Greek and Babylonian astronomers</note>, or those used by the Jews, Muslims <note>original: "Mahometans"</note>, or Christians. This is because the era of the Yellow Emperor is neither certain nor universally accepted by the Chinese people, even though the government has officially adopted it—along with Chinese history in general—to support its own claims of great antiquity. The lack of such a standardized era is sufficient on its own to raise doubts about the accuracy of Chinese chronology.

<margin>†</margin>
The reason for challenging <note>original: "impugning"</note> Chinese chronology is not because it is historically impossible for a civilization to be that old. Recent discoveries of Egyptian, Babylonian, and Assyrian historical monuments have clearly proven the ancient existence of these...

<summary>The author argues that Chinese history prior to the Yellow Emperor (Hwangti) is mythical and that the lack of a standardized, universally accepted chronological era undermines the accuracy of Chinese historical claims.</summary>
<keywords>Fu-hi, Yellow Emperor (Hwangti), BCE, Era of Nabonassar, historical authenticity, Chinese government antiquity claims, Egyptian monuments, Babylonian history, Assyrian history</keywords>

<meta>The previous page introduced the debate surrounding Chinese chronology, contrasting it with the established histories of Egypt, Babylon, and Assyria. ### GENERAL VIEW OF THE QUESTION.

...two nations thousands of years ago, everyone is quite willing to allow any other nation, such as the Chinese, to share in a similarly respected <note>original: "venerable"</note> ancient existence, provided their claim can be proven. It is partly because the records <note>original: "annals"</note> of those undoubtedly ancient nations make no mention of a contemporary <note>original: "coeval"</note> Chinese empire that reasonable doubt arises regarding the accuracy of the chronology on which China's antiquity is based. If China had been a center of science and civilization at that time, possessing the size and power attributed to it, it would have been so prominent in the world that it could not have gone unnoticed or unrecorded. This absence of any mention of China in the records of known ancient nations justifies treating its ancient history with hesitation. It also makes a careful investigation into its truth necessary.

To anyone approaching the question of whether Chinese chronology is authentic, the issue seems to turn on one point: is the historical compilation presented by the Chinese supported by contemporary <note>original: "synchronous"</note> monuments and trustworthy records? Or is it a narrative written in a more recent period, based on weak evidence, and skillfully fitted to fake ancient dates to make it look detailed and accurate?

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Therefore, it is impossible in this current research to completely separate the study of chronology from that of history. However, the examination of history will only be secondary <note>original: "subservient"</note> to the study of chronology and will only occupy a minor position in this part of the investigation. The specific question of Chinese history will be discussed in Part II of this research.

The Roman Catholic missionaries who first examined Chinese historical literature in the 16th, 17th, and 18th centuries were so struck by its consistency and logic <note>original: "regularity and cohesion"</note> that several of them—such as Du Halde, Gaubil, Couplet, Visdelou <note>OCR original: "Viseldou"</note>, Parrenin, Martini, Amiot, and De Mailla—unhesitatingly accepted the chronology it contained. In his "preliminary discourse" to De Mailla's *History of China* (Volume 1, page 22), the Abbé Grosier praises the *Tong-kien-kang-mou* <note>original: "Tongjian Gangmu," a famous 12th-century historical work often translated as the "General Mirror for the Aid of Government"</note>, of which De Mailla’s work is a translation:

> “The authority of these annals is unquestionable <note>original: "irrefragable"</note> in China, and 
> “the learned people of that empire show an esteem 
> “for this collection that is close to veneration.”

This sentence may be taken to express the opinion of all the missionaries mentioned above on the subject. However, De Guignes, in his preface to the *Shoo-king* <note>original: "Shujing" or the "Classic of History," one of the Five Classics of Chinese literature</note> (page 25), 

> “says that the long series <note>original: "suite"</note> of Chinese historical works 
> “has overwhelmed <note>original: "imposed on"</note> the missionaries and the scholars 
> “of Europe. Most of them have believed 
> “that Chinese chronology deserves special attention 
> “and that it is even preferable to any other similar 
> “system.”

<summary>This page discusses how early Catholic missionaries were so impressed by the internal consistency of Chinese historical records that they initially accepted their ancient timelines as unquestionable fact.</summary>
<keywords>Jesuit missionaries, Chinese chronology, Tongjian Gangmu, Shujing, De Mailla, Abbé Grosier, De Guignes, historical methodology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Chinese historical records were received in Europe, shifting from the initial acceptance by certain missionaries to a growing debate among French scholars and skeptical missionaries regarding the reliability of Chinese dates.</meta>

->GENERAL VIEW OF THE QUESTION.<-

***

Consequently, they sent translations of the primary Chinese historical books to Europe. There, French scholars <note>original: "savants"</note>—such as Deshauterayes in his "observations" introducing De Mailla's *General History of China* <note>original: "Histoire Générale de Chine"</note>, Fourmont, Rémusat, Pauthier, and Biot—immediately accepted their authenticity. They did so because the records came from authoritative and respected Roman Catholic missionaries who had recommended and adopted them as trustworthy. Fréret extensively examined the specific subject of Chinese chronology in the 13th and 14th volumes of his works (Paris, 1796), as did Gaubil in his *Treatise on Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "traite de la Chronologie Chinoise"</note>. Both of these authors presented nearly every argument that can be made in its favor.

Other missionaries, however, such as Cibot and Prémare (in his "preliminary discourse" to Gaubil’s French translation of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note>), took the opposite view. By carefully examining what even some Chinese writers themselves had written about their own chronology, they concluded that they were justified in doubting the credibility of the Chinese historical system. Naturally, this skepticism was also shared by scholars in Europe, such as De Guignes (in his preface to Gaubil's French translation of the *Shujing*), Klaproth in his *Memoirs on Asia* <note>original: "Memoires sur L'Asie"</note>, the Reverend George Costard in Volume 44 of the *Philosophical Transactions* (page 476), the Abbé Renaudot, and M. Dortous de Mairan <note>OCR original: "Dourtoux de"</note>...

<summary>This page outlines the intellectual divide in 18th-century Europe regarding Chinese history, contrasting scholars who accepted missionary-provided chronologies with those who began to doubt their accuracy based on internal Chinese critiques.</summary>

***

Mairan. Fréret, too, in the first part of the 13th volume of his works, exposes the unreliability of Chinese chronology, although in a later dissertation, he takes a more favorable view of it. His statements and arguments will be examined in a later chapter of this research.

Today, Dr. Legge in the "prolegomena" <note>original: "prolegomena," meaning introductory remarks or a formal preface</note> to his English translation of the *Chinese Classics*, Dr. Chalmers in his *Origin of the Chinese*, and W. F. Mayers in his *Chinese Reader's Manual* have not hesitated to declare their conviction, after careful study of the question, that Chinese chronology is entirely unreliable. However, Dr. Legge, in his introduction to the 3rd volume of *The Sacred Books of the East* (Oxford, 1879), somewhat revised his previously published opinions about Chinese history. Canon M'Clatchie has also contributed some valuable papers in the *North China Herald* of 1872 on the antiquity of the Chinese, in which he also contests the accuracy of their chronology.

<margin><unclear>to the pre-condition the more correct</unclear></margin>

Therefore, since the time Chinese chronology first came to the attention of scholars, there have been two opposing opinions on the subject. Now, since the discovery of undisputed ancient monuments in Egypt and Assyria has justified the attribution of great historical antiquity to those countries and has overcome previous doubts about them, a similarly favorable feeling has been revived in the minds of some people: that Chinese chronology should also be considered and maintained as equally—

credible as that of Assyria and Egypt. Unfortunately for such an analogy, no authentic ancient monuments have yet been discovered in China that corroborate the country's chronological system through their testimony, as has been done in Egypt.

<margin><unclear>And even then, if contested, it would not be sufficient to persuade that China is more than...?</unclear></margin>

The only monument that claims any antiquity is the so-called "Tablet of Yu," a description of which was published by Hager <note>Joseph Hager (1757–1819) was an Austro-German philologist who wrote on Chinese inscriptions</note>. Dr. Legge <note>James Legge (1815–1897) was a famous Scottish sinologist and missionary known for his translations of Chinese texts</note>, in his introduction <note>original: "prolegomena"</note> to the *Chinese Classics* (Volume III, part 1, page 67 and following), reviews the history of its discovery in the 13th century AD <note>original: "of our era"</note> and concludes: "The review I have given of the history of the stone sufficiently shows, in my own opinion, that it is not entitled to the least credit; and I am supported in this view by the great majority of Chinese archaeologists" (page 71).

Mr. C. T. Gardner of Her Majesty’s Consular Service in China has also provided an account of this Tablet of Yu in the *China Review* (March and April 1874, page 293). Although on the whole he argues for its "probable antiquity," he admits that no Chinese scholar claims <note>original: "pretends"</note> "that this inscription found in 1212 AD was the original tablet engraved by Yu." He suggests <note>original: "supposes"</note> that when "all the books and ancient records were destroyed by the order of the Emperor Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> in <term>212 BC</term>," the "Yu monument perished among them for a time." However, there is no proof that the tablet existed then, and the edict of Qin Shi Huang so clearly specifies the books that were destroyed that Mr. Gardner's hypothesis is entirely

This claim is unsupported by evidence. He argues that “nothing could be more probable than that certain scholars should have learned the Yu inscription by heart and then carved a new tablet containing it,” but even this theory <note>original: "supposition"</note> is based on the initial assumption that an original tablet had been destroyed. The Chinese authors whom Mr. Gardner quotes to support his views lived no earlier than the present dynasty <note>The Qing Dynasty (1644–1912), the final imperial dynasty of China</note>, and they offer nothing that was not already noted by Dr. Legge in his review of the history of the tablet. Legge concludes that “although the ancient inscription itself is in all probability genuine, the modern Chinese transcripts must be received with great caution, as Chinese scholars range from complete skepticism to total belief on the subject.” Therefore, as an authority on chronology, the Tablet of Yu is entirely valueless.

In his *Treatise on Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "Traité de la chronologie Chinoise"</note> (page 66), Antoine Gaubil states that Emperor Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> “erected a stone tablet on which was engraved a eulogy of himself.” Because this remark appears immediately after the description of that emperor’s journey “to the tomb of Yu on Mount Kuaiji <note>original: "Hoey Ki"</note> in the district of Shaoxing <note>original: "Chao-hang"</note> in Zhejiang <note>original: "Chekiang," a province on the eastern coast of China</note>,” it may be that this tablet of Qin Shi Huang has been mistaken for one erected by Yu himself. Gaubil mentions in a note to this passage that “the remains of this monument are still to be seen.”

In an article titled “The Deluge” in the *Contemporary Review* (November 1879, page 466), François Lenormant <note>François Lenormant (1837–1883) was a prominent French archaeologist and historian</note> makes the following remarks on the Tablet of Yu:

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

In *The Chinese Repository* <note>original: "C. R. M."; likely referring to the scholarly journal or Medhurst's "China: Its State and Prospects"</note>, on page 281, Mr. Medhurst comments on the *Shen-Yu-pai*, or the Tablet of Yu: “To this day, it remains a matter of doubt as to how far such an inscription ever actually existed. At the same time, it is a mystery what common origin, if any, can be attributed to the various reputed facsimiles, copies, or tracings of the inscription which are undoubtedly to be found in various parts of the empire.” Mr. Medhurst provides a brief critical analysis of the inscription by Tang Xunfang <note>original: "Tang Siün-fang"</note>, “a native of the area in which the original inscription is said to be found, and an individual of both rank and education.” Tang states that the commonly held opinion—that the tablet had been inscribed by Yu to commemorate his miraculous deeds—was entirely groundless.

Another ancient monument, or series of monuments, exists in the so-called "stone drums" of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty. An accurate account of these has been given by Dr. S. W. Bushell, a physician at the British Legation in Beijing <note>original: "Peking"</note>, in the *Journal of the North China Branch of the Royal Asiatic Society* (New Series, No. 8, page 133). They are believed to date back to the time of the ruler King Xuan <note>original: "Hsuan-wang"</note>, who reigned from 827 to 782 BC. They were first discovered in Shaanxi <note>original: "Shensi"</note> province between 627 and 649 AD, within the ancestral territory of the Zhou dynasty, during the early part of the Tang dynasty. Dr. Bushell, with great fairness, presents the arguments for and against <note>original: "pro and con"</note> their authenticity used by Chinese archaeologists, and concludes

Whatever may be thought of these monuments, they are of slight importance, as they only record a hunting expedition of the sovereign King Xuan <note>original: "Hsuan-wang"; King Xuan of Zhou, reigned 827–782 BC</note>, an event which was not even recorded in the annals of his reign. According to Chinese critics, this lack of record prevents them from being thoroughly identified with this specific epoch in chronology.

Another supposed ancient inscription in Chinese, which is said to exist on a terra cotta vase discovered by Dr. Heinrich Schliemann at Hissarlik <note>original: "Hassarlik"; the archaeological site of ancient Troy in modern-day Turkey</note>, has been lately brought forward in support of Chinese antiquity. However, although the Chinese ambassador in London in 1879 claimed to discern Chinese characters in the inscription, Professor Archibald Sayce, in a letter to the *Times* on June 11, 1879, effectively dismissed the idea. He showed that they were not Chinese, but belonged to the Cypriot syllabary <note>A writing system used on the island of Cyprus from the 11th to the 4th century BC</note>. See also the *China Review* for July–August 1879, containing remarks by Dr. Bushell, who declares that the claim cannot be accepted as certain. This syllabary will be considered in the fourth part of these researches.

Among the supposed ancient monuments of China are also the seventy-two tablets engraved by order of seventy-two ancient Chinese sovereigns who ruled before Fuxi <note>original: "Fu-hi"; a legendary culture hero and the first of the Three Sovereigns of ancient China</note>, located on Mount Tai <note>original: "Tay-shan"</note> in Shandong <note>original: "Shantung"</note>. François Chabas, in his *Studies on Antiquity* <note>original: "Etudes sur l'antiquitè"</note>, chapter 4, page 97, "On the nations known to the ancient Egyptians" <note>original: "sur les nations connues des anciens Egyptiens"</note>, shows that in the monuments of that country, there is no mention of any nation known to the Egyptians that corresponds to China, even though they knew all the other established nations existing alongside their own early history.

There are old traditions that the Egyptians had colonies as far east in Asia as Bactria <note>An ancient region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern-day Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note> at a very ancient date. Diodorus Siculus says that in times of remote antiquity, the Egyptians had sent out colonies over the whole known world (see Sir George Cornewall Lewis, *An Historical Survey of the Astronomy of the Ancients*, page 261). This has been recently explained by Alexandre Moreau de Jonnès, who cites the ancient authorities on the subject in *The Ocean of the Ancients and Prehistoric Peoples* <note>original: "L'ocean des anciens et les peuples antehistoriques"</note>, in the chapter "The Cushite Colonies" <note>original: "Les colonies coushites"</note>, Paris 1873. He shows that Bamiyan <note>original: "Bamian"; a valley in modern-day Afghanistan known for its ancient giant Buddha statues</note> was the capital of the Egyptian...

...colony. He also states that the Ethiopians—whom he identifies with the Libyans of Africa—had built seven cities in Sogdiana <note>An ancient Iranian civilization in Central Asia, located in modern-day Uzbekistan and Tajikistan</note>. He adds that even today, villages entirely populated by Black men—doubtless descendants of these Africans—can be found in Tibet. He cites the geographer Ritter <note>Carl Ritter (1779–1859), a co-founder of modern geography</note> as evidence of this and associates them with the Chorasmians and Bactrians <note>Ancient Central Asian peoples from regions near the Oxus River</note>.

Therefore, because China is near that region and likely had connections with it during Egyptian rule, it must have been known to the Egyptians if it had truly been an empire at that time, as the Chinese claim. It is evident that there was a very thorough knowledge of Egypt in Eastern Asia, as seen in descriptions of that country in Sanskrit literature.

Lieutenant F. Wilford, in Volume II of the supplement to Sir William Jones's works (London, 1801), has shown this clearly. Additionally, Baldwin <note>John Denison Baldwin, author of *Prehistoric Nations* (1869)</note>, in *Prehistoric Nations* (page 53), writes about the ancient Sanskrit geographical system found in the Puranas <note>A vast genre of Indian literature containing mythology, history, and cosmology</note> and other ancient books. He says that Africa was known as *Cusha-dwipa* <note>In Hindu cosmology, one of the seven "island-continents" (dvipas) that make up the world</note>. 

Therefore, if Egypt was well known in India—and if the "India" of the Sanskrit books referred to *Jambu-dwipa* <note>The central continent of the terrestrial world in Hindu/Buddhist cosmology, often identified with Central Asia</note> (the center of the world and the ancient home of the whole Aryan race, located far to the east and north of modern Hindustan, and comprising the old Iranian Empire with its capital at Balkh)—it can be inferred that the Egyptians also knew the East of Asia, just as they were known by it. Baldwin, in the previously cited work (page 64), states that *Auga-dwipa* was the country of the Manchu or Mongol people, and *Yama-dwipa*...

<summary>This page argues that if ancient China had been a major empire, it would have been recorded by the Egyptians, given the documented geographic links between Egypt, Central Asia, and India in Sanskrit texts.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding whether ancient China was a great empire contemporaneous with Egypt, using geographical evidence and ancient texts like the Vendidad to challenge Chinese chronological claims.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

<header>CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.</header>

...was the ancient Chinese empire. However, one might doubt whether this identification is supported by facts or other historical records. Even if we take the strongest view—that "Yama" refers to China—it does not necessarily follow that the country known by that name was then the seat of a great empire like Egypt, which is claimed to have existed at the same time. 

The geography described in the *Vendidad* <note>original: "Vendidad"; a collection of religious texts from the *Avesta*, the primary scripture of Zoroastrianism</note>, which mentions the fourteen Aryan settlements, certainly does not include China among the Aryan civilizations, though it does mention the *Haptu Hindu* <note>original: "Haptu Hindu"; Avestan for "Seven Rivers," referring to the Punjab region of Northwest India and Pakistan</note>, or India. 

Communication between Bactria <note>An ancient region in Central Asia, located in parts of modern Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note> and China must have been much easier then than it is today. There appears to have been a continuous chain of cities stretching from Kashgar to Lobnor <note>A former salt lake in China, now largely dried up, once a key stop on the Silk Road</note> and toward China on the southern edge of the Gobi Desert, from Khotan to Lobnor. Similarly, on the northern and eastern sides between Hami and Gansu <note>original: "Kansuh"</note>, there were also cities and large populations. 

Sir Douglas Forsyth, in his 1877 article for the *Journal of the Royal Geographical Society* regarding the buried cities in the Great Gobi Desert, mentions the ruins of several ancient cities. The most significant of these is Kok-noor (the "Blue Lake," also known as *Tsing Hai* <note>original: "Tsing Hai"; modern Qinghai Lake</note>). He notes that the actual buried cities are located many days' travel east of Khotan. 

In his work *An Account of an Embassy to the Court of the Teshoo Lama in Tibet* <note>original: "Turner in his Embassy to Thibet"</note> (specifically referencing the French translation, Volume II, page 50), Samuel Turner states that a knowledge of Egypt existed in ancient Tibet. Since it is probable that this knowledge was mutual, it serves as even stronger evidence against the existence of a great Chinese empire in ancient times, given that Tibet is so close to China.

<summary>The author argues that ancient geographical texts and archaeological ruins in the Gobi Desert suggest China was not a major empire during the time of ancient Egypt, noting that while Tibet knew of Egypt, it lacked similar records of a neighboring Chinese power.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese Empire, Egypt, Vendidad, Gobi Desert, Silk Road, Bactria, Tibet, Sir Douglas Forsyth, Samuel Turner, ancient geography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient geographical connections between China and Central Asia, transitioning from the mention of ruins in the Gobi Desert to evidence of established trade routes between Sichuan, Tibet, and Bactria.</meta>

It is evident that the Chinese in ancient times knew of Bactria <note>An ancient region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note>, and would consequently have been known to the Bactrians, because an ancient trade route existed between China and that country.

Chinese records of this trade seem to indicate a route from Sichuan <note>original: "Sze-chuen"</note> to Bactria. According to Colonel Yule in his introductory essay to Captain Gill’s *River of the Golden Sand* (page 40), this route probably passed through Tibet <note>original: "Thibet"</note>. He contrasts these records with a remarkable passage from the *Periplus* <note>original: "Periplus"; specifically the *Periplus of the Erythraean Sea*, a 1st-century AD Greek travel guide for sailors</note> of the 1st century AD. This text speaks of "Thin" and of its great city, "Thinae, from which raw silk, silk thread, and silk fabrics were brought overland through Bactria to Bharuch <note>original: "Bary-gaza (Bhroch)"; an ancient port city in modern-day Gujarat, India</note>." This is further supported by the statement of Ptolemy a century later, who says there was a road from the countries of the Seres and Sinae <note>Ancient Greco-Roman names for the people of China</note> to Bactria by the "stone tower" (that is, by way of Kashgar and the Pamir Mountains).

In the records of Sosnovsky’s <note>original: "Sossnoffsky"</note> expedition to China in 1874–1875 (see the *Journal of the Royal Geographical Society*, as cited above), he states:

> "The Gobi Desert is far from being a wretched
> desert where nothing but hardship and misery 
> await us. Water lies close to the surface. Near such 
> springs, and in the mountain valleys, grass is 
> found underfoot, suitable not only for camels but 
> for horses. In some places, a mantle of vegetation 
> extends for a considerable distance, providing 
> browsing ground for herds of wild animals, 
> including camels and asses. After an eight-day 
> march, we reach the fertile oasis of Hami <note>original: "Khamil"</note>." It

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient trade routes between China and Central Asia, arguing that communication was easier in antiquity before the expansion of the Gobi Desert sands.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

<header>CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.</header>

It thus appears that communication between Eastern Bactria and China is even now far from difficult. Considering the impact of the shifting sands in the Gobi Desert, which destroyed the cities mentioned in the 6th century <term>AD</term>, these communications must have been much easier in the centuries before Christ (<term>BC</term>).

Gaubil, in his *Treatise on Chinese Chronology* <note>original: *Traité sur la chronologie Chinoise*</note>, page 8, writes in confirmation of this:
> "When Alexander the Great was in India and Bactria, he could have easily had some knowledge of China. In his time, there were merchants in Khorassan <note>A historical region in Greater Iran, covering parts of modern Iran, Afghanistan, and Turkmenistan</note> and in the regions of Samarkand and Bukhara <note>Major trade cities in modern-day Uzbekistan</note> who traded in several Chinese items; at least, this is very probable." On page 37 of the same work, Gaubil writes: "Abdallah (a Persian author), in his translation of the summary of Chinese history, speaks 
<margin>+</margin>
> of Tsao-fu <note>A famous legendary charioteer; historical records usually associate him with King Mu rather than King Wu</note> from the time of King Wu <note>original: "Wu-wang"</note>, around 1001 <term>BC</term>, and says that he traveled as far as Persia." 

Therefore, since the Chinese themselves acknowledge communication between Eastern Asia and China, the fact that China is never mentioned in the ancient histories of Asia and Egypt is even more remarkable.

Some of the writers mentioned, who support traditional Chinese chronology, have also placed great emphasis on the timing of certain eclipses recorded in Chinese books. They argue that these dates coincide with the verified occurrence of the same eclipses in other historical timelines, as proven by astronomical calculations. This issue will also be examined later.

<summary>This page argues that ancient trade between China, Bactria, and Persia was well-established, citing evidence from historians like Gaubil and Persian authors. It highlights the mystery of why ancient Egypt and Western Asia do not mention China despite these early connections.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a critical evaluation of ancient Chinese chronology, specifically addressing the lack of external historical records and the reliance on internal Chinese sources and astronomical data.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

# GENERAL VIEW OF THE QUESTION.

...in the present research. This is done more by quoting and comparing the opinions of learned writers on the subject than through a professional astronomical analysis of the events involved. My goal is simply to point out that the conclusions drawn by these writers from eclipses mentioned in Chinese books—which they use to support this ancient chronology—are incorrect and unsustainable.

Due to the total absence of external testimony, we must look only to Chinese literature for internal evidence regarding the truth of its chronology. This internal evidence, which is the only information available on the matter, has been recently re-examined. This re-examination relies primarily on earlier research by the Jesuits <note>Catholic missionaries who were among the first Europeans to study Chinese history and science in depth during the 17th and 18th centuries</note> and other writers published in the last century <note>Referring to the 1700s</note>. The validity of Chinese chronology has been zealously defended by modern historians, such as Léon Carré in *The Ancient Orient* <note>original: *L’ancient Orient*</note>, Maspero in *The History of the Peoples of the Orient* <note>original: *Histoire des peuples de l’orient*</note>, and Barton in *The Ancient World*. These authors all maintain its authenticity and even praise its usefulness in completing our knowledge of the early history of the human race. 

Dr. Schlegel, in his *Chinese Star Mapping* <note>original: *Uranographie Chinoise*</note>, also persistently defends the truth of Chinese chronology. He asserts that it extends even further back than the most enthusiastic Chinese historians claim. He places the Emperor Yao <margin>|</margin> at 16,916 <term>BC</term> <note>in the work cited, page 30; original: *op: cit:*</note>. Baron Richthofen, in his work *China* (Berlin, 1877), also strongly advocates for the great antiquity of Chinese chronology.

<summary>This page discusses the debate over the accuracy of ancient Chinese chronology, noting that because no external historical records exist, scholars must rely on internal Chinese texts and astronomical records of eclipses. While the author is skeptical, he notes that many prominent 19th-century scholars—including Carré, Maspero, and Schlegel—vigorously defend the extreme antiquity of Chinese history.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of ancient Chinese chronology, specifically distancing this work from previous attempts by missionaries to align Chinese history with biblical timelines.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

In these researches, there is no intention to engage in any comparative discussion regarding Chinese chronology and its supposed relationship to the Holy Scriptures, whether following the Vulgate, the Septuagint, or the Samaritan calculations <note>These are different ancient versions of the Bible used by scholars to calculate the age of the earth and human history</note>. As noted by Sylvestre de Sacy <note>A famous French linguist and orientalist (1758–1838)</note>—quoted in Volume 1, page 53, of Lenormant’s *The First Civilizations* <note>original: "Prémiéres Civilisations"</note>—there is no such thing as a biblical chronology that carries any dogmatic authority. 

Although Catholic missionaries in China felt it was necessary to try to reconcile the Chinese chronological system with the Christian history of the world's duration so as not to offend the Chinese people they converted to the faith, there is no need to follow that path today. I do not intend to enter into a discussion on the merits of that question, nor even to mention it, except to briefly introduce various opinions for the sake of reference and to make this treatise complete.

I also do not intend to focus on the clearly mythical and fictitious timelines of the long eras before the time of figures like Fu-hi <note>A legendary first emperor of China, traditionally credited with inventing writing, fishing, and trapping</note> and others who are generally recognized as belonging to the earliest possible periods of Chinese national existence. I will only mention these earlier periods where they help illustrate the main topic. Furthermore, I do not intend to attempt to develop or establish a new system of chronology to explain Chinese history.

->**GENERAL VIEW OF THE QUESTION.**<-

This section of the research has the sole objective of examining Chinese chronology and the Chinese cycles used for its calculation. However, the essential connection between the planetary basis for calculating time and its application to recording the history of nations has also required an examination of the historical aspects of the general and astronomical questions involved in the same.

In the second part of the research, the individuals and events mentioned in Chinese history will be the specific subject of inquiry.

The third part will contain research on the civilization, <term>cultus</term> <note>original: "cultus"; the system of religious worship and ritual practices</note>, and religion of the Chinese. The fourth part will be devoted to Chinese literature, the written character, and the language.

It is obvious that the object of this research is not being approached for the first time. Naturally, the opinions of the authors already mentioned—both for and against the credibility of Chinese chronology—will be noted and will form a prominent part of the analysis of its merits. Therefore, much of the work that appears now will have been published previously, though it is presented here in a different form.

<meta>This page begins Chapter II, shifting the focus to the historical origins of Chinese chronological systems, specifically the foundational work of the historian Sima Qian during the Han Dynasty.</meta>

## Chapter II.

->*The origin, source, and method of the*<-
->*Chinese system of chronology.*<-

It is generally accepted that the first appearance of any system of chronology in China dates from the time of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts’ien"</note>. In his work called the *Shiji* <note>original: "Sse-ki"</note>, or *Historical Records*, composed around the <margin>Year 91 B.C.</margin> year 91 B.C. during the reign of Emperor Wudi <note>original: "Wu-ti"</note>, the fourth sovereign of the Han Dynasty, he compiled an elaborate scheme of ancient Chinese history. He arranged the eras of the most celebrated figures and the principal events of history, assigning chronological dates to each of them. In William Frederick Mayers' *Chinese Reader’s Manual* <note>original: "Mayers C. R. M."</note>, page 258, under the heading of Wudi, he states: “In 104 B.C., a change of calendar was introduced in accordance with the calculations of Sima Qian; this forms the epoch with which the modern period of Chinese chronology begins.”

There are various opinions regarding the merits of Sima Qian as a historian and chronologist. Jean-Pierre Abel-Rémusat <note>original: "Remusat"; a famous French sinologist</note> relates that several of the Jesuit missionaries in

<summary>This page identifies the historian Sima Qian (c. 145–86 B.C.) as the father of Chinese chronology, noting that his "Historical Records" established the framework for dating ancient Chinese history. It highlights the calendar reform of 104 B.C. as the starting point for modern Chinese chronological systems.</summary>

<keywords>Sima Qian, Shiji, Emperor Wudi, Han Dynasty, Chinese chronology, calendar reform, William Frederick Mayers, Jean-Pierre Abel-Rémusat</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sima Qian’s reputation among Western scholars and begins to explore the historical sources he used to construct his chronology of ancient China.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

---

In China, he was titled the "Herodotus of that country." Joseph-Anna-Maria de Moyriac de Mailla, in Volume I of his *General History of China* <note>original: "l’histoire générale de la Chine"</note>, Preface, page xviii, calls him "the restorer of history." Father Prémare, in the "preliminary discourse" <note>original: "discours preliminaire"</note> attached to the French translation of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king"; the Classic of History</note>, calls Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-tsien"</note> "an ingenious and polished author, but one who is not as reliable as is thought." On page lvi of the same work, <margin>x</margin> he writes, "This writer is considered a liar among the best Chinese critics." Father Prémare, in his *Vestiges* <note>original: "Vestiges des principaux dogmes chrétiens tirés des anciens livres chinois"</note>, page 23, says: "Under the Han Dynasty—that is, after two hundred years of brutal and constant warfare, the burning of the books, and the total loss <note>original: "shipwreck"</note> of the teachings of the ancients—there finally appeared Sima Qian, Zuo Qiuming <note>original: "Tso-ki'u-ming"</note>, Kong Anguo <note>original: "K'ung-ngan-kwok"</note>, Mao Chang <note>original: "Mao-chang"</note>, and other similar innovators. These men took it upon themselves to forge systems that suited their own personal interests <note>original: "passions"</note> and wrongly forced the *Shujing* and the *Shijing* <note>original: "Shi-king"; the Classic of Poetry</note> to appear consistent with their own historical accounts. It is true that they were refuted by the writers of the following century, but those later authors do not prove to be any more faithful to the truth than their predecessors."

Leaving aside the personal and literary character of Sima Qian for the moment, the question that naturally arises is: what were the existing sources available to him at that time for the composition of his history and chronology?

Before Sima Qian, there are said to have been the records of the different feudal or tribal states of China, and also certain books called the "Books of Xia" <note>original: "Hia"</note>, or...

<summary>This page presents conflicting scholarly views on the reliability of Sima Qian, the "restorer of history," with some critics accusing him of forging historical systems. It concludes by beginning to investigate the original sources Sima Qian used to compile his records, such as state documents and the Books of Xia.</summary>

<keywords>Sima Qian, De Mailla, Father Prémare, Shujing, Shijing, Han Dynasty, Zuo Qiuming, Kong Anguo, Mao Chang, Xia Dynasty, Chinese historiography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical sources available to early Chinese historians, specifically focusing on the lost records and legendary chronicles that predated the Han Dynasty.</meta>

-># CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

...those of the first Chinese dynasty known as the Xia <note>original: "Hia"</note>. These were probably a calendar of the Xia dynasty, an account of which has been described by Dai De <note>original: "Tae-ti"</note> in his collection known as the *Da Dai Liji* <note>original: "Ta-tae-ti"; the Records of Ritual by the Elder Dai</note>. (See Wylie, under this name.) There are also the *Sanfen* <note>original: "San-fen"</note> or the history of the *Sanhuang* <note>original: "San Hwang"</note>, the "Three Sovereigns": Fu Xi <note>original: "Fuh-hi"</note>, Shennong <note>original: "Shen-Nung"</note>, and Huangdi <note>original: "Hwangti"</note>. They are said to have reigned in China long before the Xia dynasty, and this book is supposed to have contained their instructions and the rules and methods of their government. There was also the *Wudian* <note>original: "Wu tien"</note>, which is said to have contained the history of the five sovereigns who came after the aforementioned Huangdi—namely, Shaohao <note>original: "Shao-hao"</note>, Zhuanxu <note>original: "Chwan-Hü"</note>, Di Ku <note>original: "Chih"</note>, Yao, and Shun, who immediately preceded the Xia dynasty. However, the *Sanfen* no longer exists, nor does any authenticated portion of the *Wudian* exist today.

There were also the *Basuo* <note>original: "Pa So"</note>, or the "Eight Records," which discuss the eight *Gua* <note>original: "Kwa"</note> or diagrams <term>diagrams: refers to the Eight Trigrams used for divination and representing the fundamental principles of reality</term> of Fu Xi, and the *Jiuzhou* <note>original: "Kiu-Keou"</note>, or the "Nine Categories," which treat the nine departments of the empire.

These are all known because they were mentioned by Zuo Qiuming <note>original: "Tso K'iu-ming"</note> in his commentary on the *Chunqiu* <note>original: "Ch'un Ts'iu"; the Spring and Autumn Annals</note> of Confucius. In that work, he says that the *Sanfen* was the history of the three *Huangs* (Sovereigns), and that the *Wudian* was the history of the five *Ti* (Emperors).

Dr. Legge <note>James Legge, a famous 19th-century Scottish sinologist</note> is of the opinion that the "Books of Xia" existed at the time of Confucius; however, it does not appear that either they or the other books mentioned above were still in existence <note>original: "extant"</note> at the time of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"</note>, nor are...

<summary>This page lists several ancient, mostly lost, Chinese historical texts—such as the Sanfen and Wudian—that purportedly recorded the era of the Three Sovereigns and Five Emperors before the Xia dynasty. It discusses the scholarly debate regarding whether these records were still available to early historians like Sima Qian.</summary>

<keywords>Xia dynasty, Sanhuang, Wudian, Fu Xi, Huangdi, Sima Qian, James Legge, Zuo Qiuming, Eight Trigrams, ancient Chinese records</keywords>

->## THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.<-

...they were reputed to contain any chronological data regarding the passages of history they were supposed to narrate.

<margin><unclear>Yue</unclear></margin>
There was also the work of Yue-zi <note>original: "Yo-tze"</note> from around the time of King Wen <note>original: "Wen-wang"</note> and King Wu <note>original: "Wu-wang"</note>, but the only chronological statement he makes is that the Shang dynasty lasted 576 years (see Gaubil, *Chinese Chronology*, page 65). Only a fragment of Yue-zi's work has been preserved and published by the Taoists.

Besides these books of uncertain existence, there were the works of Confucius, who lived from 551 to 479 BC, such as the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king"; the Book of Documents</note>, the *Shijing* <note>original: "Shi-king"; the Classic of Poetry</note>, and the *Yijing* <note>original: "Y-king"; the Book of Changes</note>—which Confucius either explained or preserved—and the *Chunqiu* <note>original: "Ch'un T'siu"; the Spring and Autumn Annals</note>. Dr. James Legge, in the work cited <note>original: "op: cit:"</note> on page 20, writes: "The *Shujing* itself does not supply the means of establishing any scheme of chronology for the long period of time which it covers."

Antoine Gaubil <note>a French Jesuit missionary and astronomer noted for his studies of Chinese history</note> in his *Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "Chronologie Chinoise"</note> on page 52, writes that "the *Shujing* neither gives the number of the emperors of the Xia <note>original: "Hia"</note> dynasty, nor the length of their reigns, nor the total duration of that dynasty. It also does not provide the number of the emperors of the Shang dynasty, nor the total number of the years of their reigns; it only gives the years of the reigns of some of the emperors of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty." On page 85, Gaubil further states that according to what the *Shijing* says, "neither the era of Houji <note>original: "Hiou-tsi"</note>, the ancestor <note>original: "chief"</note> of the Zhou dynasty, nor that of Xie <note>original: "Sie"</note>, the ancestor of the Shang dynasty, can be determined." On page 91, when discussing the other works of Confucius—the *Daxue* <note>original: "Ta-hio"; the Great Learning</note>, and the *Analects* <note>original: "Lun-"</note>...

<summary>This page discusses the limitations of ancient Chinese texts, such as the works of Confucius and Yue-zi, noting that they fail to provide a definitive chronological framework for the Xia and Shang dynasties. Scholars like James Legge and Antoine Gaubil are cited to show that these classic texts lack specific reign lengths and dates for early Chinese history.</summary>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

...Yu, and the *Zhongyong* <note>original: "Tchung Yung"; The Doctrine of the Mean</note>, he says, "these books do not give any fixed epoch of chronology, but they assume the history from Confucius as far back as Yao."

Father Prémare <note>Joseph-Henri Marie de Prémare, a Jesuit missionary and sinologist</note>, in his *Traces of the Principal Christian Dogmas Taken from Ancient Books* <note>original: "vestiges des principaux dogmes Chretiens tirés des anciens livres"</note>, Paris 1878, page 25, writes:

> "The philosopher Mozi <note>original: "Meh Tsze"</note>, who lived in the 5th century and a short time after Confucius, says this: 'I have read the annals of all the reigns, and these books are not what is now called the *Chunqiu*' <note>original: "Ch'un T'siu"; the Spring and Autumn Annals</note>.
> He adds, 'it is certain that this book no longer exists unless it be in the pages of the three commentators, who reproduce—I do not know which text, and each in a different manner—and which they each explain in their own fashion.' Thus Wenzhongzi <note>original: "Ven tchong see"</note>, (who lived 179–163 <term>B.C.</term> under the early Han dynasty), with several others, states with reason: 'The three glosses have made the books of the *Chunqiu* entirely disappear,' and Liu Zhiji <note>original: "Lieou-tchi-ki"</note>, who lived under the Tang dynasty (618–907 <term>A.D.</term>) and who is the author of the *Shitong* <note>original: "Chi-toung-soui-ouay"; Generalities on History</note>, does not hesitate in affirming that the *Chunqiu* and the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo king"; the Book of Documents</note> first appeared at the same time as the commentaries."

Gaubil, in his *Treatise on Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "traite de la chronologie Chinoise"</note> page 81, writes of the *Yijing* <note>original: "Y-king"; the Book of Changes</note> that "the different parts which compose this book do not give any fixed chronology. It is not that there have not been Chinese who pretended that they found a chronology in the *Yijing*, and even in the eight trigrams <note>original: "eight kwa"; the Bagua</note>, but there is no foundation to be found..."

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

> "...made in these Chinese systems of chronology which are based on the *I-Ching* <note>original: "Y-king"; also known as the Book of Changes</note>, for those persons have made an *I-Ching* according to their own fashion."

There were also the works of Mencius <note>a Chinese philosopher often described as the "Second Sage," following Confucius</note> who lived from 372 to 289 <term>BC.</term>. However, the works of these two celebrated authors contained no general chronology of <margin>✓</margin>China. Although Confucius sketches the successive events of ancient times without assigning dates to them, Mencius merely gives a general sketch of the duration of the Chinese empire in his *Book II*, chapter XIII, part 4 (see *Life and Works of Mencius* by Dr. Legge <note>James Legge, a prominent Scottish sinologist and missionary</note>, London, 1875, page 194). He states that from <margin>✓</margin>his own time to the era in which King Wen <note>original: "Wên-wang"</note> and King Wu <note>original: "Wu-wang"</note> (the reputed founders of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty) flourished, there were more than 700 years. In *Book VII*, part II, chapter XXXVIII, verse 4 (of the same work, page 38), he says that from King Wu to the time of Cheng Tang <note>original: "Ching Tang"</note>, the <margin>✓</margin>traditional founder of the Shang dynasty, there were about 500 years; and from the time of Cheng Tang to <margin>✓</margin>that of Yao and Shun <note>legendary sage-kings of ancient China</note>, there was the same space of about 500 years.

It would therefore appear that at the time of Mencius, the Chinese did not have a chronological era for the designation of years. Mencius' method of calculating historical epochs was similar to that of Herodotus and Thucydides <note>original: "Thucidides"; Greek historians known for their early narrative styles</note>, 

> "who both denote a series of years by stating the interval between the event in question and their own lifetime, or by stating the interval between the event in question and some other previous event."

<summary>This page argues that ancient Chinese historians like Confucius and Mencius lacked a fixed chronological era, instead dating events by calculating intervals between historical figures and their own lives.</summary>
<keywords>Mencius, Confucius, I-Ching, King Wen, King Wu, Zhou dynasty, Cheng Tang, Shang dynasty, Yao, Shun, Herodotus, Thucydides, James Legge, Chinese Chronology</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the lack of a fixed chronological era in early Chinese history, comparing the Chinese methods of dating to those of other ancient civilizations mentioned in the previous page.</meta>

->### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

"...designating an event, the time of which is assumed to be known" (see Sir G. C. Lewis, *An Historical View of the Astronomy of the Ancients*, London 1862, page 25). This method is similar to that of the Egyptian priests, who reckoned time by stating that 900 years had passed between the death of King Moeris <note>original: "Meoeris"; an Egyptian pharaoh, likely Amenemhat III</note> and their own era. It also resembles the records of the Syrian priests, who stated that 2300 years had elapsed between the foundation of Tyre and their own day.

In the same book, Mencius also provides details regarding the time between King Wen <note>original: "Wên-wang," the founder of the Zhou Dynasty</note> and himself. He relates this to the time of Confucius, stating that about 500 years intervened between those two figures, and about 100 years or more passed from the time of Confucius to his own day. This presents a slight discrepancy from his other account of how time was distributed.

Between the eras of Mencius and Confucius and the beginning of the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> dynasty (259–210 <term>B.C.</term>), there were also two notable authors: Zhuangzi <note>original: "Chwang-tsze"</note>, a native of the state of Liang also known as Zhuang Zhou, who lived around <note>original: "circa"</note> 330 <term>B.C.</term> (see Mayers, *Chinese Reader’s Manual*, page 30); and Liezi <note>original: "Lieh-tsze"</note> or Lie Yukou <note>original: "Lieh-yü Kow"</note>, who lived in "the period immediately following that of Confucius" (see Mayers, page 126). The work of Liezi is referenced by Sir John V. Day in *The Prehistoric Use of Steel and Iron* (London 1877, page 182):

> "In the *Kangxi Zidian* <note>original: "Kwang-hi tsi-tien"</note> or *Kangxi’s Dictionary*, published around the year <term>A.D.</term> 1710, the author quotes from the writings of Liezi. He reports him as saying that a red blade will cut jade as easily as it would cut..."

Shortly after that, and at the time of the founder of the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> dynasty, Qin Shi Huang, who lived from 259 to 210 B.C., there occurred what is termed in Chinese history the "burning of the books." This took place by that sovereign’s orders; and if there were any chronological works extant <note>original: "extent"</note> at the time of that event—works which had been composed since the death of Confucius and Mencius—they are all supposed to have perished, with the exception of a book known as the *Bamboo Annals*. This book, however, did not come to light until long after Sima Qian’s time (in 265 A.D.), when it was said to have been discovered in the ruins of an ancient tomb of one of the Wei princes. > "The original work is considered to have been long-lost, and there is good ground for believing that the one now known by that name is a fabrication."

To understand the resources the Chinese possessed for determining the foundations of their chronology—from the "burning of the books" to the time of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"</note>—it is necessary to return to the era surrounding these people and events. We must try to understand not only what Chinese records and writings survived at that time, but also the circumstances surrounding them.

The sovereign Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note>, who was previously one of the princes of the State of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note>, had already been mentioned. Through boldness and great intelligence, he managed to overturn China's Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty, under whose dominion he had been a feudal ruler. In 221 B.C., he replaced the deposed sovereign and established himself as emperor of the whole of China as it was then known. Far from being the populous empire it is today, it is thought to have contained about ten million inhabitants. This is according to T. Sacharoff in *The Rise and Fall of the Chinese Population* <note>original: "The Rise and fall of the Chinese population," by T. Sacharoff, member of the imperial Russian embassy at Peking; translated into English by Rev. Wm Lobscheid, Hong Kong, 1862, page 9</note>. Sacharoff says:

> "From the foundation of the empire to the accession of the Han dynasty, later historians quote four censuses, providing an average number for the various eras of..."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Chinese population statistics and their impact on historical legitimacy, following the citation of T. Sacharoff's 19th-century demographic study.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

"...hardly more than ten million individuals." This is not surprising, for the same author also states on the same page: "During the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty, when the empire had already attained some stability, <margin>X</margin> only half—that is, one-third—of modern China was inhabited; and even this sparse population was very unequally distributed."

This statement about the population, which is so small compared to the current number of inhabitants in China, is not quoted to prove that it was unlikely there was an important ancient Chinese empire. It is debatable whether any of the great empires of antiquity exceeded a native population of ten million, excluding subject peoples. However, the size of their territories was small compared to China. To argue that the China of the Zhou, <margin>X</margin> Shang, or Xia <note>original: "Hias"</note> dynasties was a great empire, it would require a much larger population than ten million to meet the standard of importance that Chinese historians attribute to it.

Without examining too closely the ethnic <note>original: "ethnological"</note> origin of the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> state—of which Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> had been the ruler under the name Zheng <note>original: "Cheng"</note>—it is at least <margin>-</margin> clear that this sovereign had ideas and habits entirely opposed to those supposed to have been common among the Chinese at that time. Since he could not have learned these from the Chinese, he must have acquired them from another, and probably foreign, source. His luxury and grandeur, it is said, caused great...

...discontent, as it was unlike the behavior of the ancient sovereigns of China; and it would almost appear from this that he was not of Chinese origin, but that he came from one of the western nations whose manners and civilization were more advanced than the simple ways of the Chinese, who, it is said, were scandalized by his extravagance in thought and action.

The state of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note>, from which Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> came, is described by Pauthier in *Memoirs on the Antiquity, History, and Civilization of China* <note>original: "Memoires Sur l'antiquité, de l'histoire et de la civilisation Chinoise"</note> (Paris 1868, page 10):

> "This state of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> is also the one which was the nearest to the other states of Western Asia, and with which it had the earliest and most ancient relations;"

and this relationship between the Qin state and Western Asia sheds some light on the means that had been available to Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "Shehwang-ti"</note> for obtaining an extensive knowledge of affairs and customs outside of those of China. De Guignes, in *History of the Huns* <note>original: "Histoire des Huns"</note> (Vol. I, pages 18–19), gives the following graphic description of this celebrated sovereign:

> "He was so successful in his wars that he became the absolute sovereign of nearly all China. This prince, by his vast genius, which embraced all that was grand and magnificent, placed himself above all the overly-meticulous <note>original: "minutious"</note> laws of the Chinese, and he despised their prejudices.
> 
> "He changed the face of the whole empire—his taste for public works led him to perform extraordinary things which can be favorably compared with the great works of Egypt. Many objects which were in bronze <note>original: "brouze"</note>, and others..."

"[ary statues] made of gold, were of such weight that some of his successors found it a major task to move them from one city to another. These statues and other monuments were intended to decorate the magnificent palace that he had built at Xi'an <note>original: "Si-gan-fu"</note>, his capital. The Chinese, astonished by such extravagance, unanimously spoke out and reminded him of the simple ways of their ancestors. On all sides, they spoke only of the 
<margin>X</margin>
precepts left in the ancient books, and a multitude of examples were brought forward of princes who had conducted themselves differently and who did not oppress the people with such heavy burdens as he did during his reign. The monarch, in a fit of irritation, resolved to burn all the books in order to erase the memory of these ancient rulers who were constantly cited by scholars to criticize his splendor."

<margin><unclear>that again from custom now unp...</unclear></margin>
It is worth noting from the excerpt above that De Guignes identifies a specific motive for why Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> ordered the ancient books of China to be burned. He argues it was caused by the contrast drawn by certain people among his new subjects between his own conduct and that of the supposed ancient rulers of China recorded in the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note> and other Chinese texts. This comparison fueled his dislike of these books because they condemned his ways. To remove this source of criticism regarding his lifestyle in the eyes of the Chinese people, he issued the edict for their destruction.

Father Premare <note>original: "Pére Premare"</note>, in a letter from 1724 found in Volume 33 of the *Edifying and Curious Letters* <note>original: "Lettres édifiantes et curieuses," a collection of reports from Jesuit missionaries</note>, provides another reason for Qin Shi Huang’s actions...

<summary>This page explores the motivations behind Qin Shi Huang's infamous order to burn ancient Chinese texts, suggesting he sought to silence scholars who used historical precedents to criticize his extravagant lifestyle and heavy taxation.</summary>

-># THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.<-

[...continued from previous page regarding] Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> destroying the books. “The learned men of that time could not endure a king who desired to be absolute, and they made a bad use of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note> and had unceasingly on their lips the example of Cheng Tang <note>original: "Ching Tang," the virtuous founder of the Shang dynasty</note>, who overthrew the infamous Jie <note>original: "Kie," the last, wicked ruler of the Xia dynasty</note>, and of King Wu <note>original: "Wu Wang"</note> who dethroned the tyrant, Zhou Xin <note>original: "Chow sin"</note>. They thus fanned the flames of revolt on all sides, and it was this which made the new monarch deprive the learned Chinese of those books which, in their hands, caused disturbance. Besides, Qin Shi Huang, in ordering the burning of the books, had only the goal of maintaining himself in possession of the throne which he had obtained.”

Pauthier, in the work cited on page 32, gives another motive for Qin Shi Huang’s destruction of the ancient Chinese books, which he quotes from the Chinese historian of the Han dynasty, Ban Gu <note>original: "Pan Ku"</note> (A.D. 92).

> “The Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> dynasty ordered the destruction of the literary monuments by fire, in order to render the *black heads*—that is, all the Chinese people—ignorant and stupid.”

De Mailla, in his preface to the *General History of China* <note>original: "Histoire générale de la Chine"</note>, page VIII, gives another reason for the burning of the books. “*Qin Shi Huang, who afterwards ordered the books to be burned, had no intention of sparing the Shujing, the morality of which blamed his conduct so strongly; and he considered that the severity of the maxims contained in the books were only fit to perpetuate trouble, and that this had been the principal cause of the bloody <note>original: "sanguinary"</note> wars by which the empire had been so long torn <note>original: "wrent"</note>.*”

<summary>This page explores different historical explanations for why Qin Shi Huang ordered the burning of Chinese books, suggesting he sought to suppress criticism, keep the populace ignorant, and end the civil wars fueled by ancient political philosophies.</summary>

<keywords>Qin Shi Huang, Shujing, Book Burning, Cheng Tang, King Wu, Ban Gu, De Mailla, Chinese History, Qin Dynasty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the various historical reasons given for Qin Shi Huang’s destruction of ancient Chinese texts, specifically focusing on the research of the French scholar Nicolas Freret and the Jesuit missionary De Mailla.</meta>

-># CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

Freret <note>original: "Freret," Nicolas Freret (1688–1749), a French historian and scholar</note> gives another reason for the burning of the books in Volume XIII, page 223, although he writes on page 126 of the same volume that "the motive for it is unknown."

> "Under the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty, the empire was divided among a great number of small sovereigns who all sought to be independent. Each of these princes, in order to distinguish himself from the others, not only refused to adopt the written characters invented in other kingdoms, but even wished to make changes in the generally accepted characters so as to make them unique to his own kingdom.
> 
> "The 540 characters of Cangjie <note>original: "Ts'ang Hieh," the legendary official credited with inventing Chinese characters</note>, the minister of Huangdi <note>original: "Hwang-ti," the Yellow Emperor</note>, were not sufficient, so Emperor Shun ordered others to be created. It happened that several characters of very different forms were intended to express the same thing. Because of this, the system of writing became a type of code <note>original: "cypher"</note> that was all the more difficult to understand because the rules of analogy had not been followed; the meaning of one character could not lead to the meaning of another."

On page 217 of the same volume, he had previously stated: "I derive this history of Chinese writing from a letter from De Mailla <note>original: "De Mailla," Joseph-Marie de Mailla, a Jesuit missionary and historian</note> to Étienne Souciet <note>original: "Etienne Sonciet," a Jesuit scholar and librarian</note> dated June 1, 1735, which was shared with me by the author's request." After having thus described the confusion existing among the states of China before the time of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note>, caused by each state having different modes of writing, he states in Volume XIII, page 228: —

<summary>This page explores Nicolas Freret’s theory that the destruction of books was linked to the chaotic state of Chinese writing during the Zhou dynasty, where regional princes created their own inconsistent characters. This lack of standardization made the written language as difficult to decode as a secret cipher.</summary>

-># THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.<-

> "Qin Shi Huang <note>original: 'She hwang-ti,' the first emperor of a unified China (r. 221–210 BCE)</note> thought that it would be proper to abolish all these different types of writing and to establish the use of characters common to all the provinces. This way, there would no longer be a need to produce multiple copies of the same edict to make it understandable to everyone who was required to follow it. He therefore charged his minister Li Si <note>original: 'Li-sze'</note> with this important task."
>
> "While Li Si was working on his new characters, Meng Tian <note>original: 'Mêng T'ien'</note>, a general of the imperial army, was occupied with discovering a material more convenient for writing than the bamboo tablets. Up to that time, written characters were traced onto these tablets using a stick dipped in lacquer <note>original: 'varnish'</note>."
>
> "The attempts of Meng Tian were not long without result: after several trials, he at last succeeded in making a rudimentary sort of paper, and all public documents were copied onto it using the *Xiaozhuan* <note>original: 'Tsin-chuan'</note>, or the writing style adopted by Qin Shi Huang. These characters created by Li Si (9,353 in number), which are called the Small Seal script <note>original: 'lesser seal character'</note>, are preserved in the *Shuowen Jiezi* <note>original: 'Shwoh-wen,' an early 2nd-century Chinese dictionary</note> by Xu Shen <note>original: 'Hü-shên'</note>."
>
> "Li Si ordered translations—or rather, copies—to be made in his new script of books on medicine, divination, and agriculture, as well as the specific history of the state of Qin <note>original: 'Tsin'</note>, which recorded the ancestors of the reigning family. He then obtained an edict for the suppression of all ancient books of 
<margin>X</margin> > history and moral philosophy."

<summary>This page describes Emperor Qin Shi Huang's efforts to standardize the Chinese writing system into the Small Seal script and the invention of an early form of paper by General Meng Tian. It also details the targeted preservation of practical texts alongside the state-ordered destruction of historical and philosophical works.</summary>
