<meta>This page continues the discussion on the standardization of the Chinese writing system, moving from the actions of Li Si under the first emperor Qin Shi Huang to earlier attempts at script unification during the Zhou dynasty.</meta>

## CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

De Mailla, in the work previously cited <note>original: 'op: cit.'; referring to Joseph-Anna-Marie de Moyriac de Mailla’s *Histoire générale de la Chine* (General History of China)</note>, Volume 2, page 401, confirms this in his account of Li Si’s <note>original: 'Li-sze'</note> famous speech before his sovereign:

> "My advice would be that all written characters be reduced to a single system, and that everyone should be required, under the most severe penalties, to use only those used at your Majesty's court. What confusion it is to see at least seventy different ways of writing in a single state! Is it not a certain way to keep the spirit of rebellion alive?"

This reason for the destruction of the ancient books of China is quite curious, for it reveals a state of diversity in writing that would naturally be a serious barrier to any kind of national literature or chronology. At the same time, it made it difficult for regional literature to circulate among neighboring states, thereby hindering—if not making impossible—the preservation of a uniform imperial history that could be respected by a universal body of scholars across the nation. Fréret <note>original: 'Frérêt'; referring to the French scholar Nicolas Fréret</note> (Volume 13, page 225) attributes a similar attempt to unify the Chinese written character to the eleventh ruler of the Zhou <note>original: 'Chao'</note> dynasty; interestingly, this was done for the same reasons that motivated Qin Shi Huang <note>original: 'She hwang-ti'</note>.

<margin>X</margin>

> "King Xuan <note>original: 'Süan wang'</note>, who ascended the throne in 827 BCE and who wished to restore order and authority in the empire, undertook to fix the disorder that existed in written characters. He ordered the creation of a writing style which he called the *Dazhuan* <note>original: 'Ta chuan'; also known as the Great Seal script</note>, and he commanded that it alone be used."

<summary>This page explores the political motivations behind the standardization of Chinese writing, linking the suppression of ancient texts to the need for administrative unity and the prevention of rebellion. It notes that both Qin Shi Huang and the earlier King Xuan of Zhou attempted to consolidate the many regional scripts into a single official system.</summary>

<keywords>Li Si, Qin Shi Huang, King Xuan of Zhou, Dazhuan, Great Seal script, De Mailla, Fréret, Chinese writing systems, censorship, Zhou dynasty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical attempts to standardize the Chinese writing system, specifically focusing on the challenges faced by rulers like King Xuan and later Qin Shi Huang due to regional resistance.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

> “...throughout the empire, but his orders were not obeyed, and the tributary kings made it a point of their own dignity to preserve the writing system that was special to their own country.”

This version of the event now under consideration shows that instead of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: 'She hwang-ti'; the first emperor of a unified China</note> being an enemy of literature, as is generally supposed, he must have been disciplined in literary studies. It suggests he was of an elevated mind to have been able to devise a plan for the unification of the Chinese written language. On the other hand, the learned class of Chinese, who have been so highly praised <note>original: 'vaunted'</note>, had never even attempted to remedy the confusion which existed through the diversity of methods of writing. Nicolas Fréret <note>original: 'Freret'; an 18th-century French scholar</note> aptly remarks on this state of things (Volume 13, page 251):

> “One can easily imagine that amidst the wars that had devastated <note>original: 'desolated'</note> all the provinces of the empire, learning had been but little cultivated. The diversity of written characters—which were no longer the same as soon as any one passed from one kingdom to another—was a hindrance to copies of the same book being largely distributed, as each copy could only be read in a small area of the country.”

All this would, moreover, lead to the inference that the different states of China had different national origins, as they possessed different forms of writing. This question, however, will be specially considered in Part II, although it may serve to mention here what Georg Horn <note>original: 'Hornius'</note> writes on this subject in *Noah's Ark* <note>original: 'Arca Noe', published 1666</note> (page 440):

<margin>X</margin>
> “It is sufficiently evident from the diversity of physical forms, of manners, and of language, that the Chinese are not all of the...”

<summary>This page argues that Emperor Qin Shi Huang's efforts to unify the Chinese script were an intellectual achievement rather than a simple act of censorship, suggesting that the diverse regional scripts reflected distinct national origins within the empire.</summary>

The variety of opinions they present regarding this strange event also suggests that a certain amount of guesswork <note>original: 'conjecture'</note> was used in forming them. Consequently, it may be permissible to offer some other reflections on another probable cause of this celebrated occurrence. Rightly or wrongly, this event has been used to explain the lack <note>original: 'paucity'</note> of literary and chronological works from the early periods of China, by stating that a tyrant had destroyed them.

<margin>x</margin> It is curious to observe that this same event—the burning of the books—has also been assigned as the cause for the Chinese losing their knowledge of astronomy and other subjects. In a civilized empire such as ancient China is presumed to have been, these things could hardly have failed to be preserved by oral tradition, even if the written records of them had disappeared.

<margin>✓</margin> It is said that books on astrology were exempt <note>original: 'excepted'</note> from the edict for destroying the books. However, considering the small practical differences between these two sciences <note>original: 'astronomy and astrology'</note>, it is hard to believe that the astronomical books were not also destroyed, unless one argues that no astronomical books existed at that time. The Reverend George Costard, in his *History of Astronomy* (London, 1767), remarks on this subject:

> "I am afraid that astronomy originally owes its birth and progress to

> "Chinese considered and treated the two sciences as

“The Chinese believe there is a mutual relationship between the actions of princes and their subjects and celestial phenomena. It is in order to discover that relation that their astronomers have devoted all their efforts (Gaubil, *Abridged History of Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: 'Histoire abregée de l’astronomie Chinoise'</note>, in Vol. II, page 31, of Souciet’s *Mathematical Observations* <note>original: 'observations, mathematiques'</note>, Paris 1729, Vol. II, 31). Is it not essentially saying that these two sciences <note>astronomy and astrology</note> were, in China as everywhere else, <margin>x</margin> blended together and viewed as one and the same? The princes, therefore, who burned astronomical books, also burned astrological books.”

Gaubil, in his *Treatise on Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: 'Traité sur l’astronomie Chinoise'</note>, page 50, writes:

“This burning is also blamed—along with the neglect of the Chinese toward the end of the Zhou <note>original: 'Chow'</note> dynasty—for the loss of the secret of Hi <note>original: 'Hì'</note> and Ho. According to the mathematicians appointed by the tribunal, these 'Hi' and 'Ho' represented the astronomy of Huangdi <note>original: 'Hwang-ti'; the legendary 'Yellow Emperor'</note> and Yao <note>another legendary ancient ruler</note>. It is also claimed that the secret of the Chariot used to find the **south** (that is, the **compass**) and the methods for tracking the movements of the fixed stars were also lost. At any rate, it is confidently asserted that from the <margin>*</margin> time of Yao until roughly the end of the Zhou dynasty, the Chinese possessed a perfect knowledge of astronomy. Without examining the truth of this specific claim, I have felt it my duty to gather all the information I could find regarding this astronomy.”

<page-num>41</page-num>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

The concluding remarks of Gaubil in the passage quoted above suggest that he did not fully believe all the consequences that were supposed to have resulted from the actions of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China (r. 221–210 BCE)</note>. It may also be inferred that this catastrophe has been equally exaggerated in other matters, just as it is in this one, for the purpose of explaining—in a way that is satisfactory and advantageous to Chinese **self-esteem** <note>original: "amour propre"</note>—their lack <note>original: "penury"</note> of knowledge.

It must be admitted that Qin Shi Huang was a man of great genius, accustomed to a higher material and more intelligent civilization than that possessed by the Chinese whom he subdued at that time.

It is not too much to deduce from this aspect of his character that he was acquainted with the history and national traditions of wherever he may have been educated. Certain circumstances point out that he was acquainted with those of Babylonia, which were current in Bactria <note>an ancient region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note> at or shortly before his time. For instance, there are certain traces in the history of Babylonia of similar destructions of books by its sovereigns that might well have been taken by Qin Shi Huang as examples to imitate.

Moses of Chorene, in his *History of Armenia*, chapter 13, page 40, relates that:

> “Ninus <note>a legendary king of Assyria</note>, puffed up with pride and greedy for celebrity, caused many of the books and histories of the times which had preceded him to be burned, in order that he and his reign alone should be spoken of.”

<summary>This page argues that the historical impact of Qin Shi Huang's book burning may have been exaggerated by later Chinese historians to excuse a lack of ancient records. It also suggests the emperor may have been inspired by similar acts of cultural destruction in Babylonian history, specifically citing the legendary King Ninus.</summary>

This passage is quoted by G. Schlegel in *Chinese Uranography* <note>original: "Uranographie Chinoise"</note>, page 746, and is also mentioned by Volney in *New Research on Ancient History* <note>original: "Recherches nouvelles sur l’histoire ancienne"</note>, volume II, page 46.

Another sovereign of Babylonia, Nabonassar <note>King of Babylon from 747 to 734 BCE</note>, is recorded to have also acted in a similar manner.

Berosus <note>a Hellenistic-era Babylonian writer and astronomer</note>—as quoted by <note>original: "apud"</note> Josephus (see Cory’s *Ancient Fragments*, page 36)—states:

Judging from the coincidence between the actions and motives of Nabonassar and Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note>, it might be inferred at first glance that the latter had imitated the example of the Chaldean monarch. At the very least, it suggests he knew of this event in Babylonian history, just as Nabonassar seems to have known and imitated his predecessor, Ninus.

Rawlinson, in *The Five Great Monarchies* (Volume III, page 38), describes the event this way: 

> “Nabonassar destroyed the records of the kings who had preceded him.
> The result was that the year of his accession (747 BCE) 
> almost necessarily became the era from which all 
> subsequent events had to be dated.”

Qin Shi Huang was not quite as successful as Nabonassar in carrying out his project, even though his motives were almost identical. The era of Nabonassar lasted for a long period, though it appears to have been used only for astronomical purposes (see Sir G. C. Lewis...

[...as stated in the work cited <note>original: "op. cit."</note>, page 27], while the era established by the Chinese monarch <note>Qin Shi Huang</note> hardly survived him.

This practice of burning books also occurs among the traditions of the Khwarazmians <note>original: "Chorasmians or Khwarismiams"; an ancient Iranian civilization in Central Asia</note>. Al-Biruni <note>an 11th-century polymath and historian; original: "Alburini"</note>, in the work cited <note>original: "op. cit."</note>, page 58, describes them as "a branch of the great tree of the Persian nation." He says that Qutayba <note>Qutayba ibn Muslim, an Arab general who conquered the region in the 8th century</note> killed their learned men and priests, <margin>X</margin> and burned their books and writings, so that they "became entirely illiterate."

<margin>X</margin> 
There is also a tradition that Alexander the Great burned the books of the Persians. Al-Biruni (in the work cited, page 127) writes regarding the chronology of the Sasanians <note>the last kingdom of the Persian Empire before the rise of Islam</note> and the Ashkanians <note>the Parthian dynasty of ancient Iran; original: "Ashghânians"</note> that during the period of the latter, the Persian "empire was disorganized, and people were prevented by various circumstances from preserving their chronology. Such were the calamities which Alexander and his Greek lieutenants brought upon them; furthermore, there was the burning <note>original: "conflagration"</note> of all the literature in which the people delighted. And more than that, Alexander burned the greater part of their religious code."

It may be noted that during the time of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note>, hardly a hundred years had passed since Alexander the Great had done this; the memory of it must still have been vivid throughout Eastern Asia.

<meta>This page continues the comparison of rulers who destroyed historical records to rewrite history in their own image, specifically comparing the Chinese Emperor Qin Shi Huang to the Babylonian kings Nabonassar and Ninus.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

...of Nabonassar, given by Berosus <note>a Babylonian priest and historian from the 3rd century BCE</note> (as mentioned above), that upon reading it, the conclusion becomes unavoidable that the history of this portion of Qin Shi Huang's <note>original: "She hwang-ti"; the first emperor of a unified China</note> life is either a Chinese version of the Babylonian incident, or that Qin Shi Huang was fully aware of it. The coincidence of such a strange action taken by two different sovereigns of different countries makes it unlikely that both events had an independent origin, and it can hardly be explained by the theory that history simply repeats itself. Mr. Mayers <note>William Frederick Mayers, a 19th-century scholar of Chinese history</note> remarks: "One of his first decrees (as recorded in history) ordered the abolition of the use of posthumous titles <note>honorific names given to royalty after their death</note>, declaring it his pleasure that he should be known simply as Qin Shi Huang, the first emperor; and thus all successive generations should be distinguished numerically as the second generation, the third generation, and so onward to the ten-thousandth."

Nabonassar, according to Berosus, destroyed the historical records of Babylonia "in order that the list <note>original: "enumeration"</note> of the Chaldean <note>the people of Babylonia</note> kings might begin with him," just as Ninus <note>the legendary founder of Nineveh and the Assyrian Empire</note> had done before him "in order that he and his reign should be the only ones spoken of." If these narratives are compared with what Mr. Mayers states, the very same motives which drove the Babylonian sovereigns to destroy all records of previous kings appear to have motivated Qin Shi Huang. In his destruction of Chinese history, he seems to have attempted to be thought of as the first emperor of China, just as Ninus and Nabonassar had wished to do regarding the Chaldeans.

<summary>This page explores the striking parallels between the Chinese Emperor Qin Shi Huang and the Babylonian kings Nabonassar and Ninus, all of whom allegedly destroyed historical records to ensure that official history began with their own reigns.</summary>

There is another incident in which Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"; the first emperor of a unified China</note> seems to have followed the example of one of the Babylonian monarchs, Nebuchadnezzar <note>original: "Nabuchodonosor"; king of the Neo-Babylonian Empire known for his building projects</note>, who lived closer to his time than Nabonassar. This was his building of a more lavish royal palace than any of the previous rulers of China had constructed. Berosus <note>a Babylonian priest and historian from the 3rd century BCE</note> relates (in Cory's *Ancient Fragments*, page 39):

> "Nebuchadnezzar also added a new palace to those in which his forefathers had lived, adjoining them, but exceeding them in height and splendor."

What George Rawlinson <note>a 19th-century English scholar and historian</note> (Volume III, page 60) writes of Nebuchadnezzar and his character—and the fame that came to Babylonia because of his qualities—might be equally applied to Qin Shi Huang, who was famous for his warlike spirit and his conquest of the Huns <note>nomadic tribes of Central Asia, known in Chinese history as the Xiongnu</note>.

> "Its military glory is due chiefly to him, while the constructive energy which constitutes its special characteristic belongs to it still more markedly, through his character and genius;" and he further says, "that he possessed a grandeur of artistic conceptions and skill in construction which place him on a par with the greatest builders of antiquity.
> "The emperor had a palace built that was worthy of himself. The ancient palace was to the south of the Wei River <note>original: "Oue choui"</note>; he constructed the new one to the north of this river. There was never anything more magnificent nor more vast. Gold, silver, and ebony were lavishly used inside and outside the apartments of the princesses and the wives of the kings whom he had..."

Indeed, it would not be a surprise if it were discovered that Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwangti"</note> even got the idea of building the Great Wall of China—with which he is usually credited—from the great wall of Nebuchadnezzar <note>original: "Nabuchodonosor"</note> that he built around Babylon; or from the Median Wall <note>a defensive wall built to protect Babylonia from the Medes</note> for the defense of the province of Babylon, which cut off the country between the Tigris and Euphrates rivers. This is described by Xenophon, who saw it, as being built of baked bricks cemented with asphalt, and as 20 feet in thickness, 100 feet in height, and 20 parasangs <note>an ancient Persian unit of distance, roughly 3.5 miles or 5.6 kilometers</note> (75 miles) in length (*Anabasis* II. 4, 12). Rawlinson, in the work cited <note>original: "op: cit:"; short for the Latin "opere citato"</note> on page 56, writes:

> "He (Nebuchadnezzar) built the great wall of Babylon, which according to the lowest estimate must have contained more than 500,000,000 square feet of solid masonry, and must have required three or four times that number of bricks."

And in a note on the same page, he writes:

> "Taking the height of the wall at 75 feet, its width at 32 feet, and its circumference at 365 stades <note>an ancient Greek unit of distance, approximately 600 feet</note>, the measurements of Herodotus would raise the total volume <note>original: "cubical contents"</note> to more than 5,400,000,000 feet."

There is a question whether the Great Wall of China existed in the time ascribed to Qin Shi Huang, or even whether it was really built by him. De Mailla (*General History of China* <note>original: "Histoire generale de la Chine"</note> Vol. II. page 373) writes that all Qin Shi Huang did was: 

"He closed the passes of Longxi <note>original: "Long-si"; a region in modern Gansu province</note> of..."

“...Beidi <note>original: "Peti"; nomadic tribes of northern China</note> and of Shang-gu <note>original: "Chang-kien"; a commandery in ancient China</note>, by which the Tartars might penetrate into China. The princes of Zhao <note>original: "Tchao"</note> and Yan <note>original: "Yen"</note>, following his example, constructed walls;” and the editor in a note on page 374 adds: “It is seen by this history, that all that great work has been wrongly attributed to the Emperor Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwangti"</note>.” Should it appear that he was not its constructor, there would be all the more reason to hold that this history may be a Chinese version of a chapter in Babylonian history. Even if it could be established that he did actually build the Great Wall, it is more than probable—considering his other imitations of events in Babylonia—that he also derived the idea of doing so from the history of Nebuchadnezzar <note>original: "Nabuchodonosor"</note>, the Chaldean Monarch.

There is a tradition in Central Asia that a wall resembling in many circumstances the one attributed to Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwangti"</note> was built by Alexander the Great. Georg Horn <note>original: "George Hornius"; a 17th-century Dutch historian</note> in his *Noah's Ark* <note>original: "Arca Noe"</note>, writes:

> “The Nubian Geographer <note>referring to Al-Idrisi, a 12th-century Arab geographer</note> mentions this wall, part 9, climate 6, and calls it the mound of Gog and Magog, and is of the opinion that it was built by Alexander the Great.”

Al-Biruni <note>an 11th-century Persian scholar</note> (Edward Sachau’s translation, 1879, London, page 43) writes of Dhul-Qarnayn <note>original: "Dhu Alkarnaini"; a figure mentioned in the Quran, often identified as Alexander the Great</note>, whom he thinks was a Yemeni prince, but whom others think was Alexander, as follows:

> “He is generally assumed to have entered the darkness of the north, to have seen the remotest frontiers of the inhabitable world, to have fought both against men and demons, to have passed between...”

The religion of Shi Huangdi also appears to have been derived from foreign sources, probably from Babylonia or from Egypt.

It may not perhaps be out of place here to suggest that these eight spirits <note>original: "genii"</note> were the deified eight ancestors of the human race after the Great Flood <note>original: "deluge"</note>—namely, Noah and his wife, and their three sons with their three wives. This incident may throw some further light on the subject of Shi Huangdi’s education.

It is also more than probable that Shi Huangdi was not particularly familiar with the records of the peoples living in the country now called China. According to the accounts of the modern Chinese themselves, these people had existed as several tribes that were continually at war with each other for some hundreds of years before he subdued them and united them into one nation. 

The fact that he acted as though the usual impression produced in men’s minds by listening to or reading the historical records of those who have preceded them had never taken root in his mind regarding China shows one of two things: either the records of China were not known to him, or he did not look upon them as containing a true picture of the past, at least as far as his own early training was concerned. <meta>This page continues the investigation into whether Emperor Shi Huangdi viewed the existing historical records of the Chinese tribes as authentic, or whether he saw them as legendary fabrications that hindered his vision for a unified empire.</meta>

-># THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.<-

...their race, such as are usually placed at the beginning of all such Eastern histories. Even if he knew of them, it would not follow that he acknowledged them as faithful or true descriptions of the past, as far as the inhabitants of China were concerned.

<margin>X</margin>
Besides this, there is the fundamental question to consider: did the historical books of China, as they existed then, actually describe the Chinese people in the same way they are represented in the works that now serve as substitutes for those Shi Huangdi <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> ordered to be destroyed? But even assuming, for the sake of argument, that the current books are identical to those said to have been burned, the originals—with the mythical and legendary character that is still visible in them today—were the kind of records that an intelligent and great sovereign like Shi Huangdi would naturally wish to eradicate. He would want to remove them from a people whom he had to govern and instruct according to the ideas he deemed right and true. The question therefore arises: did Shi Huangdi order the destruction of the books because he knew them to be inaccurate as histories of China, or at least because he saw them as distorted Chinese versions of the histories of other countries?

The Chinese account of the affair and the speech <note>original: "harangue"</note> of his minister, Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze"</note>, urging him toward this severe measure, do not contradict this view of his motives when closely examined.

The narrative of the circumstances surrounding the edict for destroying the books is found in the *Shiji* <note>original: "the Sse," referring to the *Shiji*, or *Records of the Grand Historian*, by Sima Qian</note>.

<summary>The author questions whether Emperor Shi Huangdi destroyed ancient Chinese texts because he recognized them as myths or as "distorted appropriations" of other nations' histories. This suggests the book burning was a deliberate move to replace legendary fabrications with a more "truthful" administrative ideology.</summary>

One of the Emperor’s intimate friends, Zhou Qingchen <note>original: "Chow tsing-chen"</note>, who held an important military post and served as a minister of state, was likely from the same state from which Shi Huangdi himself had come to subdue the previous rulers of China. He concluded a speech of praise to the new monarch by specifically highlighting his wisdom in transforming the semi-independent feudal principalities into provinces of the new empire. He declared that "from the most distant antiquity, no sovereign has reached the height of your majesty’s eminent talents and virtues."

### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

According to the accepted historical theory of the Chinese, the state of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> had been a prominent member of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> empire, and the princes of that state were descended from the family of the first emperors of China.

Eulai <note>original: "Gou-lai"</note>, or Wulai <note>original: "Ou-lai"</note>, the ancestor of the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> tribe, according to Joseph de Guignes in his *History of the Huns* <note>original: "Histoire des Huns"</note>, Volume 1, page 140, was descended from the Emperor Zhuanxu <note>original: "Chwan hü"</note> through Boyi <note>original: "Peh I"</note>. Boyi received the family name Ying <note>original: "Ing"</note> because of the service he rendered to Yu the Great <note>original: "Yü"</note> in draining off the floodwaters that had inundated China.

One of his descendants, named Eulai, was the father of Jiufang <note>original: "Hiu-fang"</note>, who fathered Pangao <note>original: "Pan-chao"</note>. Pangao was the father of Taiji <note>original: "Ta-ki"</note>, who had a son named Daluo <note>original: "Ta-lo"</note>. Feizi <note>original: "Fitsu"</note>, the son of Daluo, was the first prince of Qin. The general history of the empire, as contained in the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king" or Book of Documents</note>, must therefore have been just as well known to the scholars of the state of Qin as it was to those of Qi <note>original: "Thsi"</note>, whose representative spoke and discussed the matter at that court meeting. Consequently, this book would naturally have been familiar to the educated people of the Qin state. These scholars would have known all about the supreme greatness of the ancient sage-kings Yao and Shun, provided that the history describing their exalted merits had been known to them as true. And yet, this high-ranking and presumably educated official did not hesitate to declare that Shi Huangdi <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> was the greatest sovereign he knew of when compared with those of ancient times. He seems to have been well

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

He was well-acquainted with history, for he speaks with confidence and certainty about antiquity. His testimony on this subject is as credible as that of anyone else in the assembly. It seems strange that this assertion by Zhou Qingchen <note>original: "Chow-tsing-chen"</note>—which challenged the fundamental Chinese veneration for Yao, Shun, and Yu—was not immediately contradicted if it could have been proven false. 

However, the next speaker in the debate, a man from the state of Qi <note>original: "Thsi"</note> named Chunyu Yue <note>original: "Chun-yu-yueh"</note>—who, according to Mayers’ *Chinese Reader’s Manual* <note>original: "C.R.M."</note> (page 123), was the "Minister of Learning"—merely remarked in response to what had been stated that he had "heard it said." Notably, he did not mention that he had *read* it in the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Book of Documents</note> or other books. He stated that the sovereigns of the Shang and Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasties had, for more than a thousand years, promoted all their relatives and ministers to positions of regional command. Interestingly, he does not mention the preceding Xia <note>original: "Hia"</note> dynasty, nor the legendary model sovereigns of China, Yao and Shun. 

He argued that because Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti"</note> did not promote his children and younger brothers in a similar way, he would lack the family support that former sovereigns relied upon. He added that he had never heard of a system of government lasting long if it was not modeled on ancient customs.

Neither does the history of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty support the assertion made by Yue. In Section 5, page 196, King Wu <note>original: "Wu wang"</note>, in organizing his government, "divided the territory according to three classes; appointing officers, he selected men of talent." The commentary here notes: "Feng himself was an inspector; there is no mention that the former kings appointed only their own family members to these posts."

It is at least evident from the speech of Yueh <note>likely referring to a minister or historical figure cited in the debate</note>, and also from the decision of the council mentioned above, that the prevailing traditions of government in the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> dynasty did not confirm the truth of the supposed ancient customs of the two preceding dynasties. The practice of those earlier dynasties had been quoted as a guide for the settlement of the new Qin dynasty in its government of all China; and it is questionable, from this very fact, whether these or similar customs had really existed among the traditions and true records of an empire supposedly dating back for a thousand years, as China is claimed to have done.

## THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

Gaozong <note>original: "tsung"; also known as Wu Ding</note>, or Wu Ding <note>original: "Woo ting," a king of the Shang dynasty (c. 1250–1192 BC)</note>, appointed Fu Yue <note>original: "Yue"</note> to office because he resembled a person he had seen in a dream, and whom he believed Shangdi <note>original: "Shangti," the High Deity or Supreme God</note> had granted to him through this vision. This was exactly the practice that Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> had decreed after consulting his council. Furthermore, there is no evidence in the records of the Shang dynasty of provincial governorships being entrusted to princes of the sovereign’s family.

In any case, these customs and records do not appear to have been known as established principles of statecraft to the leader of the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> state, who is reputed to have been the dominant feudal lord following the collapse of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> Empire. If they had been well-known, the minister Yue <note>original: "Yueh"</note> would not have had to appeal to them as obscure precedents, rather than merely mentioning his own personal hearsay regarding these matters.

The minister of Qin Shi Huang who replied to Yue is depicted by the historian Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien," author of the *Records of the Grand Historian*</note> as denying the truth of Yue's remarks regarding the wisdom of following ancient dynastic models as a rule of government. However, Sima Qian also has this minister introduce the first five legendary emperors of China at the beginning of his speech. According to Chinese critics, these legendary figures are not even mentioned in the historical works considered authentic; therefore, it is inconsistent to suggest they could have been known at the time of Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the influential Legalist Prime Minister of the Qin dynasty</note>, yet the text presents him as arguing about them.

In the *Chinese Reader’s Manual* <note>original: "Mayers C.R.M."</note>, page 123, William Frederick Mayers says that Li Si, "originally a humble scholar of the state of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note>, was himself—"

"...himself a well-taught scholar." Consequently, it is even more unlikely that he would have delivered such a speech; this raises doubts about the authenticity of the speech Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the influential Chancellor of the Qin dynasty</note> allegedly gave before the new Emperor <note>Qin Shi Huang</note>. He is, however, depicted as saying that the three dynasties (the Xia, Shang, and Zhou) had not followed the custom mentioned—though the passages quoted above from the *Classic of History* <note>original: "Shoo-king"</note> suggest this was incorrect. 

Moreover, Li Si claimed that Fu Yue <note>original: "Yueh," a legendary minister of the Shang dynasty</note> had appealed to the administration of three dynasties, which was a strange point to make given that Fu Yue had only mentioned two lines of sovereigns. Li Si added emphatically that the actions of those ancient dynasties could not serve as models or rules of conduct for the new Emperor. How can this be reconciled with the frequently repeated, positive statements in the *Classic of History* regarding the universal empire of the Xia <note>original: "Hias"</note> and the Shang dynasties? 

The main point made by Li Si in favor of the new empire, and in opposition to the previous state of China, was that "since the empire is now established and solidly formed, the **laws** and **ordinances** are only to proceed from **one sole authority**." Yet, it was the primary virtue of Yu <note>The Great Yu, legendary founder of the Xia dynasty</note> and the other supposed ancient rulers of China that they were the sole source of legislation and authority.

Could Li Si have drawn such a contrast between his sovereign and the past, and offered such statements in the presence of so many learned men, if he had been familiar with—or even acquainted with—the *Classic of History* and its contents?

<meta>This page continues the critique of Li Si’s speech to the First Emperor of the Qin dynasty, questioning why Li Si would dismiss the historical narratives of ancient sage-kings if he were a well-educated scholar familiar with the empire's records.</meta>

->### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.<-

...narratives of Yao, Shun, and Yu? Or, if he had known of them, could he have shown a more contemptuous disbelief in their being a true history of Chinese antiquity? And yet, as a minister of state and a scholar, he must have known the ancient records of China. He is also depicted as appealing to antiquity, but only to pervert the received traditions of the empire by stating:

> "In ancient times, when the empire was out of joint and in disorder, no one was found capable of restoring its unity."

The dislocation of the empire referred to here had only existed for some hundreds of years—since the Zhou <note>original: "Chow," the dynasty preceding the Qin</note> chief ruler had lost his hold over the other princes who were his feudatories <note>term: noble landowners who held land in exchange for service to the central ruler</note>. What Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the Chancellor of the Qin dynasty</note> is reported to have said could not have been truly applied to "ancient times." During those times, the Chinese maintain that unity of government, authority, and civilization was the prominent feature of the reigns of the great sovereigns of the first two ancient dynasties <note>The Xia and Shang dynasties</note>. This unity was set forth in the *Classic of History* <note>original: "Shoo-king"</note> as an example for posterity.

It was simply a misstatement to say that no one had been found in those times to unite the nation; otherwise, it is a misstatement for the *Classic of History* to say that Yao, Shun, Yu, and their descendants had done so.

Li Si, furthermore, openly accuses the assembled scholars <note>original: "literati"</note> of untruth: "The most pathetic and empty discourses were used in order to trouble minds and distort the truth." He adds that their motive in distorting the truth was "to shake and..."

<summary>This page argues that Li Si’s speech to the First Emperor contradicts the established historical records of the Classic of History, specifically regarding the unification of ancient China under the sage-kings.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Li Si’s argument for the suppression of historical texts, suggesting that the "antiquity" cited by his opponents was used as a political tool against the newly unified Qin state.</meta>

<header>CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.</header>

"...overturn that which the ancient sovereigns had established and founded."

This was a bold statement for a statesman of a rising power who had just overthrown those very sovereigns himself. However, it clarifies the situation. It shows that the literary works being complained about must have been books containing radical or revolutionary ideas. They were likely not the models of good government and obedience to the law that the *Classic of History* <note>original: "Shoo-king," a collection of documents regarding the early history of China</note> and other similar books claim to be.

Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the Chancellor of the Qin dynasty</note> could only have reasonably requested a decree against books of that first type—those whose contents were used by the scholars <note>original: "literati"</note> to stir up public unrest and encourage contempt for the government.

The grievance against the scholars was based on their habit of using "antiquity to undermine the current state of affairs." Yet, the version of antiquity described in the *Classic of History* actually favored obedience to the sovereign under these circumstances. The "current state of affairs" established by Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first Emperor of a unified China</note> was exactly what the *Classic of History* praised as the perfection of good government and the happiness of the people. Through his power and authority, the Emperor had ended the civil wars and ruthless looting that had plagued the Chinese people during the era of the warring feudal states. Consequently, the entire nation was finally enjoying peace and prosperity. Zhou Qingchen <note>original: "Chow Tsing," a scholar-official under the Qin dynasty</note> had remarked in his speech, "All the people now enjoy the happiness of..."

<summary>The author argues that the books targeted for destruction by the Qin dynasty were likely revolutionary texts rather than the classic histories, as the classics actually supported the Emperor's goal of a unified, peaceful state.</summary>
<keywords>Li Si, Qin Shi Huang, Shujing (Shoo-king), Zhou Qingchen, book burning, Qin Dynasty, Chinese antiquity, scholars (literati)</keywords>

"...tranquility; they have ceased to be exposed to the calamities of civil wars," and Yue <note>original: "Yueh"</note>, who replied to that speech, never once called this assertion into question.

Moreover, Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first Emperor of a unified China</note> is nowhere described as a cruel or hard master in the complaints made against him prior to the burning of the books. Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the Emperor's Chancellor</note> specifically points out: "As regards the populations who tranquilly enjoy rest amidst their families." Nowhere is it recorded that Qin Shi Huang had deserved to have his government overturned in the same way as the last monarchs of the Xia <note>original: "Hea"</note> and Shang dynasties had been cast out; those earlier rulers were overthrown because their oppression of the people turned heaven against them, which justified the rebellions that destroyed them.

The works of Mencius <note>an influential Confucian philosopher</note> certainly incite rebellion and value the people over the sovereign. Dr. Legge <note>James Legge, a famous 19th-century Scottish sinologist</note> shows this in his introduction <note>original: "prolegomena"</note> to the classics, in which it appears that Mencius maintained the people's right to dethrone a sovereign; and yet, curious to relate, this revolutionary book, which must have been generally known, is never mentioned in the edict.

Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil, a Jesuit missionary and scholar in China</note>, in his *Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "Chronologie Chinoise"</note>, page 91, says:

> "Some Chinese authors have stated that the books of Mencius were not burned, but this is not at all probable. Mencius's works were read by many, and what he wrote about the government of the ancient princes is precisely what Qin Shi Huang would have wished to see buried in eternal oblivion."

<summary>This page argues that Qin Shi Huang was not initially viewed as a tyrant and explores the paradox of why the revolutionary writings of Mencius were omitted from the Emperor's famous book-burning edict.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of why Qin Shi Huang sought to destroy certain books, questioning whether the classical texts were truly hostile to his rule or if the narrative of their destruction is flawed.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note> and the other classics were all on the side of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first Emperor of a unified China</note> and would have officially supported his government if they had been consulted. If they existed at that time and were accepted as truthful <note>original: "veridical"</note>, it is all the more astonishing that they were not cited in his favor. And yet Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien," the "Grand Historian" of early China</note> gives the supposed reason for their destruction: that they disapproved of the new emperor's conduct. Based on the circumstances, it would follow that the goal of consolidating Qin Shi Huang's power would have been better achieved by preserving these books. The inference from this is that Qin Shi Huang did not consider them to be truthful histories of the Chinese Empire—unless the story of their destruction is altogether disbelieved.

The demand made by Li Si <note>the Emperor’s Chancellor</note> for the destruction of the books first describes them as:

> "the historical and other documents which are preserved in the archives of the official historians <note>original: "historiographers"</note>, with the exception of the memoirs of the State of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note>."

These certainly referred to the records of the feudal <note>original: "feudatory"</note> states whose independence had been destroyed by the success of Qin Shi Huang. They are all placed here on the same footing as those of the Qin state. It was natural to desire to erase the records of their former governments, and thus cause them to be forgotten, in order to confirm the sovereignty of the Qin over them all.

If the supposed imperial Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynastic records had been delivered over to the Qin, just as it is said the ritual tripods <note>ancient bronze vessels symbolizing the Mandate of Heaven and the right to rule</note>...

<summary>This page argues that the classic Chinese texts actually supported the Emperor's authority, suggesting that his order to destroy them may have been motivated by a desire to erase the independent histories of rival feudal states rather than a dislike of the classics themselves.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the "burning of the books" under Qin Shi Huang. It suggests that if the historical records were kept in the imperial archives, they would have been safe from destruction, casting doubt on the traditional narrative of their total loss.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

[...all the states that] were; they would have been kept safely, out of reach of troublemakers <note>original: "mischief-mongers"</note>, and there would be no reason to destroy them. It is clear, therefore, that the decree <note>original: "edict"</note> did not include them. This is because it is later stated: "Whoever in the empire dares to keep in their possession, and in hidden places, copies of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note> and the *Shijing* <note>original: "Shi-king," the Classic of Poetry</note>, or the writings (whatever they may be) of the hundred different schools—that is, all the schools of various teachings then in existence—shall be punished." The only exception was for the officials <note>original: "functionaries"</note> attached to the tribunal of scholars of the first rank <note>original: "literati of the first order"</note>, established in the capital (the *Boshi guan* <note>original: "Poh-see kwan," the Court of Broad Learning or Doctoral Academy</note>, mentioned in the *Zhouli* <note>original: "Chow li," the Rites of Zhou</note>).

First, it should also be noted that the classical and sacred works of the Chinese sages are grouped here with the unorthodox <note>original: "heterodox"</note> works of a hundred different schools of thought. This is hardly consistent with the deep respect supposedly shown to the former.

Furthermore, the people were forbidden "to discuss together the *Shujing* or the *Shijing*," yet they were apparently allowed to discuss the hundred different schools of thought, whose books were considered dangerous and deserving of being burned. All this is too inconsistent to be believable as a factual account of such an important event. It is also worth noting—which supports the conclusion that the imperial archives were not destroyed—that the highest-ranking scholars of the empire, attached to the ministry of learning, were permitted to keep copies of all the banned <note>original: "proscribed"</note> books.

<summary>The author argues that the account of the burning of the books is logically inconsistent, specifically noting that the decree exempted high-ranking officials and that sacred texts were unlikely to be grouped with unorthodox ones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the alleged destruction of Chinese classical texts during the Qin dynasty. It specifically addresses the paradox of why the "Shujing" (Classic of History) was so difficult to recover if scholars had been permitted to keep copies.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

As Pauthier <note>Jean-Pierre Guillaume Pauthier (1801–1873), a French Sinologist</note> remarks in the work cited <note>original Latin: *opere citato* (op. cit.)</note>, page 13:

> "It ought to be assumed that the majority of these people knew how to take advantage of this permission."

And yet, when the Han dynasty is said to have wished—only 25 years after the event—to rediscover and re-establish the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note>, not one copy was to be found among the descendants of these scholars <note>original: "literati"</note>, nor even among the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> imperial archives. The *Shujing* could only be recovered through the oral recitation of Fu Sheng <note>original: "Fuh-shêng," a scholar famous for preserving the text</note>.

It is further to be observed that even a year after the decree <note>original: "edict"</note>, when it is said that 460 scholars 

> "were buried alive because, by their deceitful <note>original: "perfidious"</note> speeches and by other means, they were causing trouble and discontent among the common people <note>original: "black heads," a literal translation of the Chinese term *qian shou*</note>,"

it is not specified whether these revolutionary stirrings were derived from the Classics or from the unorthodox <note>original: "heterodox"</note> books of the 100 different schools of thought. Seeing that the contents of the Classics would not have sown trouble and discontent among the people, it is most probable that these men were punished only for commenting on the dangerous books which had been condemned. It is thus evident that the agitators <note>original: "demagogues"</note> who were executed by order of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> had not drawn their ideas from the *Shujing*, as that book would have provided principles confirming obedience to the ruler's authority.

The son of Qin Shi Huang is recorded to have said by Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts’ien," the Grand Historian of the Han dynasty</note>:

> "that the scholars confined themselves to reading the writings of Confucius, whom they took for their [model...]"

<summary>The author questions the historical accuracy of the total destruction of the Chinese Classics, arguing that the executed scholars likely focused on "dangerous" unorthodox books rather than the Shujing, which supports royal authority.</summary>

### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

> "...guide, and of which they only now ask for the application,"

But this would suggest that either the decree <note>original: "edict"</note> was only directed against these books, or that the appeal made by the son of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of the Qin dynasty</note> was pure nonsense. In the reported speech of Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the Chancellor of the Qin dynasty</note>, it is expressly mentioned that other writings besides the copies of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king," the Classic of History</note> and the *Shijing* <note>original: "Shi-king," the Classic of Poetry</note> were banned <note>original: "proscribed"</note>. Therefore, the offense of the scholars <note>original: "literati"</note> was not merely the one attributed to them in the speech of Qin Shi Huang’s son.

There is too much contradiction in the narrative of this entire event to make it trustworthy, regarding either the accepted opinion of the motives for Qin Shi Huang’s actions or the specific names of the books he ordered to be destroyed. However, taking the narrative as it stands, the motive for this strange act might more probably be found in the worthlessness or inaccuracy of the books as historical accounts of China, rather than in them being a cause of offense to Qin Shi Huang’s government.

In addition to this difficulty—which an analysis of the record of the transaction presents against the existence of sufficient material for forming a reliable chronology of China—it is even a question of whether the destruction of the books affected any significant number of chronological, historical, or even literary works.

Ma Duanlin <note>original: "Ma-twan-lin," a 13th-century Chinese historian and encyclopedist</note>, who is quoted by Pauthier in the work cited <note>original Latin: *op : cit :* (opere citato)</note>, page 18, says:

> "The destruction of the books commanded by 
> Qin Shi Huang did not have anything near the 
> disastrous results that might be supposed."

<summary>The author argues that contradictions in historical accounts make the story of the Qin dynasty book burning unreliable as a basis for Chinese chronology. He suggests the books may have been destroyed because they were historically inaccurate rather than for political reasons.</summary>
<keywords>Qin Shi Huang, Li Si, Shujing, Shijing, Ma Duanlin, Pauthier, Chinese chronology, book burning, historiography</keywords>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The number of books that Chinese scholars claim survived the destruction is too small to justify the observation of Ma Duanlin <note>original: "Ma-twan-lin," a famous 13th-century Chinese encyclopedist and historian</note>. If that small handful constituted the majority of the Chinese literature that existed during the Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> dynasty, then the historical records used to establish later chronology must have been extremely meager and insignificant. 

Ma Duanlin even says in his text <note>original: "*in loco*," meaning "in the place cited"</note> while noting the incompleteness of the classical works: "All these losses were in no way caused by the 'fires of Qin.'" This indicates his own doubts that Qin Shi Huang’s <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> decree was the main reason for the scarcity of historical works. He adds: 

> "From the time of the Han dynasty <note>original: "the Hans"</note> to the present, so few books and the bamboo tablets <note>Before the widespread use of paper, Chinese records were written on vertical strips of bamboo tied together</note> on which they were written have been preserved that hardly one or two out of a hundred remain. It was not the Qin who annihilated them; it was the students themselves who destroyed them."

Therefore, if books disappeared at this rate during the 800 years between the Han era and Ma Duanlin’s time, and if learned Chinese people before the Han dynasty were just as destructive—having no more love for books than the students Ma Duanlin mentions—it may be fairly estimated that the ancient books said to have been written during the Xia <note>original: "Hia"</note>, Shang, and even the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasties (a period of at least 1,500 years characterized by civil wars) would have suffered even more. According to...

<summary>This page argues that the scarcity of ancient Chinese historical records was likely caused by gradual neglect and destruction by scholars over centuries, rather than solely by the book-burning edict of Emperor Qin Shi Huang.</summary>

Chinese), it must have diminished at an even more negative rate. This makes it highly unlikely that any significant quantity of books remained by the end of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty, when Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> began to rule China. Indeed, since even the most dedicated supporters of China’s ancient literary history admit that only a few of the earliest books were ever written, it would be almost miraculous to expect many to survive. Given the local factors of destruction described by Ma Duanlin <note>original: "Ma-twan-lin," a 13th-century historian</note>, only a very small number could have remained after 1,500 years of exposure to such influences.

Ma Duanlin’s explanation almost proves that he considered the historical "book catastrophe" <note>This refers to the "Burning of the Books and Burying of Scholars" in 213 B.C.</note> insufficient to explain the scarcity of historical records. This is especially significant because the Chinese tradition of imperial historians <note>original: "historiographers"</note> was designed not only to produce but also to preserve a continuous record of Chinese history.

To show the level of exaggeration common during the Han dynasty more clearly, there is a statement by Ban Gu <note>original: "Pankoo," the primary author of the *Book of Han*</note> (see Pauthier’s previously cited work <note>original: "Pauthier op: cit:," referring to Guillaume Pauthier, a French Sinologist</note>, page 32):

> “Matters had arrived at the point where, during the reign of Emperor Wu <note>original: "Wu-ti"</note> (140–87 <term>B.C.</term>), portions of the books were still missing. The bamboo tablets <note>Before the invention of paper, Chinese texts were written on vertical strips of bamboo tied together</note> which contained them were so damaged that the rites and the music prescribed for religious ceremonies could not be performed.”

> “The Emperor was moved by this and said with a sigh, ‘I am deeply distressed by this state of affairs.’”

It should, however, be remembered that some of these traditions about the reputed ancient times of the Chinese—which were circulating at the same time and which Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien</note> did not use—have been preserved in so-called Taoist works. These are found especially in the writings of Zhuangzi <note>original: Chwang-tze</note> and Liezi <note>original: Lieh-tsze</note>, and the works of Jia Yi <note>original: Kia I</note>, 
<margin>x</margin>
of whom Mayers, in his *Chinese Reader's Manual* <note>original: C.R.M.</note> page 78, says that "he was a privy counselor to Emperor Wen of Han <note>original: Han wen-ti</note>, 178 B.C., and that he was active in establishing the literary canon." 
These traditions also appear in the *Shanhaijing* <note>original: San-hai-king; "The Classic of Mountains and Seas"</note>, which Prémare, in his "Preliminary Discourse on the *Shujing*" <note>original: Discours preliminaire Au Chou-king</note>, states was probably written by Boyi <note>original: Peh-e</note>—the arranger of the ancestral temple under the Emperor Shun—or even by the Emperor Yu himself.

This work is undoubtedly an ancient book, and there is even some probability of it being a Chinese transcript of the work of Berosus the Babylonian, who was a priest of the temple of Belus; or it may be a Chinese version of the traditions from which Berosus compiled his work. The question of the authorship of the book will be considered in Part II of these researches.

It has been the fashion to draw a distinction between these ancient traditions in China and to classify one portion of them as specifically Taoist. This distinction exists because modern Taoists linked certain absurd doctrines and practices to that portion of the old traditions for which they seem to have been the special caretakers <note>original: depositories</note>.

In consequence of this, these special traditions have been treated as false. At the same time, the *Rujiao* <note>original: Ju-kiao; the "School of the Scholars" or Confucianism</note> traditions, which Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien</note> also had access to and followed, and which the official scholars <note>original: literati</note> alone acknowledge, are held by them as the only true traditions of China. This is done to support the theory that there are two sets of traditions in China: one set true, and the other false.

It may be remarked, however, that the "orthodox" traditions, as they are called, are just as open to the criticism of being mythical <note>original: fabulousness</note> and paradoxical as those of the Taoists.

The legend of the birth of Confucius is an instance of this. “His mother Yan-she <note>original: Yeu-che; Yan Zhengzai</note>, just before her childbirth <note>original: parturition</note>, saw the fabulous animal called the Qilin <note>original: Ki-lin</note> in the courtyard of her house with a piece of jade in its mouth. He will be a king, but without any dominions.’ She gave the jade stone to her husband and said, ‘The child I bear in my womb is a son, and the words written on the stone—which heaven has sent me by the Qilin—apply to him.’ At the time of his birth, two dragons were seen on the roof of Yan-

> "The learned Chinese have never regarded the reigns of Fuxi <note>original: "Fu-hi"</note>, of Huangdi <note>original: "Hwang-ti"</note>, and his successors until Yao as mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note>."

> "None of the learned—I say none, that is to say, not one of them—has ever doubted that Fuxi is the founder and that Huangdi is the legislator of the Chinese monarchy."

There is no inherent reason for assigning any superiority to the ancient Confucian <note>original: "Ju-kiao"</note> traditions over those of the Taoists, as there are paradoxes in both. It is only fair to consider the fact that they all existed together among the Chinese as a collection of ancient ideas regarding the nation's earliest times and its chronology.

Prémare, in his *Vestiges* <note>original: "Vestiges &c," referring to his work on Christian traces in Chinese texts</note>, page 50, writes about the Taoist authors already mentioned above:

> "One thing is certain: they are very ancient, and they do not hold the opinion of Confucius. Some of them preceded Confucius, while others lived shortly after him, and they have nearly all followed a different method from his. Who would dare to claim that the ancient traditions fell only to Confucius, and that no trace <note>original: "vestige"</note> of them has been preserved by other schools?"

Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a French Jesuit missionary who was a leading astronomer and historian in China</note>, in his *Treatise on Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "traite de la chronologie Chinoise"</note> page 62, writes: "One cannot say exactly what the Taoist sect <note>original: "sect of Taoists"</note> consisted of then, or what it consists of now. In the time of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "Tsin She hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note>, it had great reach. Mencius <note>A famous Confucian philosopher and student of Confucius's grandson</note> complained of false sects in his own time." <margin>x</margin>

The differing levels of respect shown to these traditions by competing literary groups in China do not provide a final answer regarding their true value. Perhaps a closer examination of them may reveal an unexpected worth, showing that they contain some Babylonian traditions. Having been preserved in China, these traditions gain further validation by their presence in Chinese literature.

</margin>

De Mailla <note>Joseph-Anna-Maria de Moyriac de Mailla (1669–1748), a Jesuit missionary who translated the "Great Chronology" of China</note>, in the work previously cited <note>original: "op : cit :"</note>, page 19 <note>original: "xix"</note>, when discussing Liu Xiang <note>original: "Liu-hiang"</note> (80–9 BC)—who was a near contemporary of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"</note>—states: "Liu Xiang, in the preface he placed at the beginning of his *Biographies of Exemplary Women* <note>original: "history of illustrious women," a classic work of Chinese biographical literature</note>, argues with great vigor <note>original: "vivacity"</note> against certain minor <note>original: "petty"</note> authors influenced by the ridiculous doctrines of the Taoists, who falsified the beginning of history by mixing it with their wild fantasies <note>original: "extravagant reveries."</note>"

This passage is provided simply to prove that around the time Sima Qian wrote, there were also several Taoist authors who included the traditions current in China at that time in their works, specifically those regarding the chronological beginning of its history.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the survival of Taoist literature during the Qin dynasty and its subsequent categorization by Han dynasty scholars such as Liu Xiang.</meta>

### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY. ###

> "Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a French Jesuit missionary and sinologist</note>, in his *Treatise on Chinese Chronology* <note>original: "traite de la chronolgie Chinoise"</note>, page 65, remarks that 'Li Si <note>original: "Li-sze," the Chancellor of the Qin dynasty known for the "burning of books"</note> and the Emperor Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She Hwang-ti," the first emperor of a unified China</note> were infatuated with the principles of the Taoist sect, and it is therefore probable that a strict search was not made for the books of that sect.'"

If this is correct, it would show that these books were in circulation before Qin Shi Huang's time, and consequently formed a part of the ancient literature of China.

In the catalogues of Chinese books that were recovered during the Han dynasty—which were compiled by Liu Xiang <note>original: "Liu-hiang" (77–6 BC), a scholar and government official</note>—there were, according to Dr. Legge <note>James Legge (1815–1897), a Scottish sinologist and translator of the Chinese Classics</note> in the work previously cited <note>original: "op: cit:"</note>, Volume 1, page 10:
> "more than 13,000 volumes of a larger or smaller size,
> the productions of nearly 600 writers, and arranged in 
> 38 subdivisions of subjects. In the third catalogue, the 
> first division contained the orthodox writers, to the 
> number of 53, with 836 works, or portions of their 
> works. The second subdivision contained the works of 
> the Taoist school, amounting to 993 collections from 
> 37 different authors."

It thus appears that the Taoist authors at that time were not as unimportant as De Mailla <note>Joseph-Anna-Maria de Moyriac de Mailla (1669–1748), a Jesuit missionary and historian</note> insinuates; and when they are compared with the number of what are called the orthodox authors, they hold an important position, totaling four-fifths of the latter, while the number of Taoist works even exceeds those of the *Ru-jiao* <note>original: "Jü-kiao," meaning the "School of the Scholars" or Confucianism</note>.

De Mailla gives a brief description of three principal works of these Taoist writers, stating, "one of these works had..."

These ten **Ki**, or ages, are probably only another version of the ten antediluvian <note>existing before the biblical Great Flood</note> sovereigns of Babylonia mentioned by Berosus <note>a 3rd-century BC Babylonian priest and historian who wrote in Greek</note>. At a meeting of the Society of Biblical Archaeology held in November 1879, Mr. Jules Oppert <note>Jules Oppert (1825–1905) was a prominent French-German Assyriologist who played a key role in deciphering Mesopotamian cuneiform</note> remarked that the chronology of the ten antediluvian <note>original: "antediluvian patriarchs," referring to the ten generations from Adam to Noah who lived before the Great Flood</note> patriarchs corresponded in certain ways with the chronology of the ten Babylonian kings of the same period. These kings are said to have reigned for 432,000 years. Mr. Oppert observed that the biblical duration of the ten patriarchs’ era was 1,656 years; because both of these time periods are divisible by 72, they appear to be connected.

Regarding this subject, and before we examine it further in relation to the Chinese, it may be useful to keep in mind the following quotation from the July 1877 issue of the *Quarterly Review* (page 140). In an article titled "The Science of Electricity as Applied in Peace and War," the author draws attention to the numbers deduced by Halley <note>Edmond Halley (1656–1742), the English astronomer famous for calculating the orbit of Halley's Comet and studying Earth's magnetic field</note> from his research. After noting the four polar periods imagined by Hanstein <note>Christopher Hanstein (1784–1873) was a Norwegian physicist who studied terrestrial magnetism</note>—which were 1,640 and 860 years for the North Pole, and 4,609 and 1,304 years for the South Pole—the reviewer provides a note containing the following passage from Sir William Snow Harris’s *Magnetism* (page 17):

<margin>x</margin>
> "By a curious coincidence, these periods involve the
> number 432, which was sacred to the Indians, Babylonians,
> Greeks, and Egyptians because it was believed to depend on great
> combinations of natural events. Thus, the periods of
> 860, 1,304, 1,740, and 4,609 become—by a slight modifi-
> cation—864, 1,296, 1,728, and 4,320. These are not inad-" <note>The text likely intended "inadmissible" or "inadequate," but the OCR ends here.</note>

> "[ad]missible, considering the complicated observations from which the first numbers are derived. Now, these numbers are each equal to 432 multiplied by 2, 3, 4, and 10, successively. According to Brahman mythology <note>original: "Brahmin mythology"; the text refers to the Yuga cycles of time found in Hindu scriptures</note>, the world is divided into four periods: the first being 432,000 years; the second, 2 times 432,000; the third, 3 times 432,000; and the fourth, 10 times 432,000. According to Hanstein <note>Christopher Hanstein (1784–1873), a Norwegian physicist who mapped the Earth's magnetic poles</note>, it is also worthy of remark that the sun's mean distance from the earth is 432 half-radii of the sun. The moon's mean distance is 432 half-radii of the moon. But what is especially striking is the fact that the number 25,920 (which is 432 multiplied by 60) is the smallest number divisible at once by all four periods. It is therefore the shortest time in which the four magnetic poles can accomplish a cycle. This duration coincides exactly with the period in which the precession of the equinoxes <note>The slow, wobbling motion of Earth's axis that changes the orientation of the stars over a cycle of roughly 26,000 years</note> completes its cycle. This is certainly a curious and remarkable series of coincidences."

It may be noticed here that the Chinese also have a similar traditional period of 432,000 years. This is composed of the 24 reigns of the <term>Tien-hwang</term> <note>Tianhuang, or the "Celestial Sovereigns," the first group of mythical kings in Chinese historiography</note> and the <term>Ti-hwang</term> <note>Dihuang, or the "Earthly Sovereigns," the second group of mythical kings</note>, each lasting 18,000 years.

—shows the analogy between the Chinese and Babylonian theories of chronology.

<margin>Bailly, *History of Ancient Astronomy* <note>original: "Histoire de l’astronomie Ancienne"</note>, page 114, comments on this subject: "The nine brothers of the Renhuang <note>original: "Gin-hwang"; the Human Sovereigns in Chinese mythology</note> who are said to have reigned each over one portion of the nine regions into which the earth was divided, closely resemble the nine sons of the Indian sovereign Acnydrouven <note>likely a reference to a figure from the Puranas, such as Agnidhra, who divided the world among his sons</note>, who reigned over a similar division of the world." He adds, "this fact is very remarkable, and seems decisive for the identity of the two histories, and one can conclude that the fables of the Taoists contain some facts of the ancient history of India."</margin>

The assignment of a longer period by the Chinese for the reigns of the <term>Tianhuang</term> <note>original: "Tien-hwang"; the "Celestial Sovereigns"</note>, than that allotted to the <term>Dihuang</term> <note>original: "Ti-hwang"; the "Terrestrial Sovereigns"</note>, may appear to have been made in accordance with the ideal superiority of the former. However, curiously enough, these two periods of years correspond to a division of the time assigned to half of the ten antediluvian <note>original: "aniediluvian"; meaning "before the Flood"</note> kings of Babylonia already mentioned.

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY #

It may be observed that the number of <term>Sari</term> <note>A Babylonian unit of time equaling 3,600 years</note> of 3,600 years each, which are represented by these years, is 65 for the larger quantity and 55 for the smaller. Since the total number of <term>Sari</term> during which these Babylonian kings are said to have reigned is 120, the division made above is exactly in the same proportion as the division of the 24 Chinese reigns into terms of 13 and 11.

->65 &nbsp;&nbsp; 13 &nbsp;&nbsp; 5<-
->--- ÷ --- = ---<-
->55 &nbsp;&nbsp; 11 &nbsp;&nbsp; 5<-

This shows that the similarity is methodical. The period of 18,000 years assigned by the Chinese as the duration of the reign of each of the <term>Hwangs</term> <note>Sovereigns or Kings in Chinese mythology</note> resembles the Egyptian period of 18,000 years, during which the ancient kings and heroes ruled over that country, as related by Diodorus Siculus <note>original: "Diodorus of Sicily," a Greek historian of the 1st century BC</note> in Book I, chapter 44. A similar period of 18,000 years is likewise stated to have been established in 504 <term>B.C.</term> by the Greek philosopher Heraclitus, though it does not appear whether its purpose was astronomical or chronological (See Lewis, *Historical Survey of the Astronomy of the Ancients* <note>original: "op: cit:"</note>, page 98).

Bailly, in the "preliminary discourse" to his *Treatise on Indian and Oriental Astronomy* <note>original: "Traite de l’astronomie Indienne et Orientale"</note>, page 110 <note>cx</note>, remarks: "This division into kings of the earth and kings of mankind is analogous to that of the Greeks, and especially to that of the Egyptians—into reigns of gods, semi-gods, and men. It also resembles the system of the Persians, who placed two other species of beings, the <term>Dives</term> <note>In Persian mythology, the "Divs" or "Daevas" are often depicted as demonic or supernatural beings</note> and the <term>Peris</term> <note>In Persian mythology, beautiful, winged spirits often contrasted with the Dives</note>, before the appearance of the human species. Similarly, it is like that of the Indians, who also held that two species..."

It is also desirable to call attention to the fact that if the principle of the Babylonian <term>antediluvian</term> <note>The period "before the flood" (the Great Deluge described in various cultures)</note> period is identified—as Mr. Oppert <note>Jules Oppert (1825–1905), a prominent Assyriologist</note> states—with the similar Scriptural period of 1,656 years due to their analogous division by 72, then the <term>Taoist</term> period mentioned above of 3,276,000 years is similarly connected with the chronology of the ten antediluvian <term>patriarchs</term> <note>The long-lived figures, such as Methuselah, listed in the Book of Genesis before the Flood</note> of the Bible, because it is also divisible by 72: 3,276,000 ÷ 72 = 45,500.

<margin>The use of a period of 72 was known in ancient China. <term>Mayers</term> <note>William Frederick Mayers, author of the "Chinese Reader’s Manual" (C.R.M.) published in 1874</note>, page 359, states: "The year is divided into 72 periods of five days each, an arrangement traced to the period of the <term>Chow dynasty</term> <note>The Zhou Dynasty (c. 1046–256 BC)</note>."</margin>

This division of the year is all the more noteworthy because it establishes the fact that the ancient Chinese believed the year consisted of only 360 days <note>72 periods multiplied by 5 days equals exactly 360 days</note>.

### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

---

The other period of 2,760,000 years being equally divisible by 12, it is similarly connected with the Genesis period of 1,656 years <note>According to the Hebrew Bible, 1,656 years passed between the Creation and the Great Flood</note>. Therefore, instead of these Taoist periods being **“extravagant” “reveries”** and inventions of the Taoists, they are closely identified with some of the old traditional periods of the Babylonians, and they were undoubtedly derived from that country. It is hard to imagine that the Chinese and Babylonians independently invented a system of ten primitive ages, each having periods composed of similar elements. It would be even stranger to suppose that the Babylonians derived their system from the Chinese, as that would place Taoist chronology on a pedestal of antiquity that would hardly suit their critics. 

There is no fundamental principle of hostility between the philosophies of Lao-tsze <note>The legendary founder of Taoism</note> and Confucius to make their respective followers as mutually hostile as they are regarding these chronological theories. The only perceptible motive for this must be that the Taoists represented the traditions of one branch of the ancestors of the Chinese, and the Confucianists resent this evident foreign origin of their nation, seeing it as inconsistent with their own arrogant theory of the native beginning of their knowledge and civilization.

Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a French Jesuit missionary to China and a prominent astronomer and historian</note>, in “Chinese Chronology” page 132, states:

> “The Taoist sect has taken a great deal from the
> “ancient religion of the Persians, and it has made use
> “of several traditions and features of the history of the
> “Jews, such as Enoch, the garden of paradise, the tree of
