# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

> "...of life, etc., which it has applied to Chinese history, and to the country of China."

An inevitable retribution, however, haunts the official scholars <note>original: "literati"</note> because they unintentionally admit traditions that clearly show a foreign origin and provide a specific character to many points in their ancient history. Thus, while trying to uphold the credit of their own prideful theories, they are led into contradictions regarding their protests against the Taoists.

It should not be supposed, however, that the anti-Taoist theories have always had their own way; moreover, it is clear that those who have upheld them have even agreed with Taoist opinions to a certain extent.

For it appears from De Mailla <note>Joseph-Anna-Maria de Moyriac de Mailla (1669–1748), a French Jesuit missionary who translated the "Great Annals of China"</note>, in the work previously cited, page 21, that according to the Taoists during the Han dynasty:

> "The ten periods <note>original: "Ki"</note> had been preceded by the **Heavenly Sovereign**, the **Terrestrial Sovereign**, and the **Human Sovereign**, or the **Three Sovereigns** <note>original: "San Hwang"</note>. The Taoists did not stop stirring up disputes among the scholars of that time regarding a tradition which claimed that the *Three Sovereigns* and the *Five Rulers* <note>original: "Wu Ti"</note> had been the first princes of China."

So, from the very beginning of all the Chinese literature upon which their chronology is founded, a dispute is recorded as having existed among the scholars <note>original: "savants"</note> as to whether traditions of persons not mentioned in the Confucian Classics should be believed or not.

De Mailla <note>Joseph-Anna-Maria de Moyriac de Mailla (1669–1748), a Jesuit missionary and translator of Chinese annals</note> also mentions on page 33 that:

> "Kong Anguo <note>original: 'Kong-nga-koue'; a famous Han dynasty scholar and descendant of Confucius</note> and the majority of knowledgeable scholars <note>original: 'clever persons'</note> concluded that Fuxi <note>original: 'Fu-hi'</note>, Shennong <note>original: 'Chin-nong'</note>, and Huangdi <note>original: 'Hwang-ti'</note> were the ***San Hwang***, or Three Sovereigns; and that Shaohao <note>original: 'Chao-hao'</note>, Zhuanxu <note>original: 'Tchuen-hiu'</note>, Ku <note>original: 'Ti-ko'</note>, Yao, and Shun were the ***Wu-ti***, or Five Rulers."

This provides evidence that these "knowledgeable scholars" accepted the historical existence of certain figures who were not mentioned in the so-called "orthodox" Confucian classics, but who were nevertheless known through "outside" or folk traditions.

Although the abstract question of who specifically was reputed to be one of the "Three Sovereigns" or "Five Rulers" is not the primary interest of this current research, it is important to demonstrate that discussions regarding these Taoist traditions were widespread <note>original: 'rife'</note> during the time of Sima Qian <note>original: 'Sze-ma-ts'ien'; the foundational historian of the Han dynasty</note>. At that time, there were two different theories for assigning these ancient Chinese figures to the two categories of rulers mentioned above.

De Mailla testifies on the same page that:

> "Others, whose views seem to have been adopted by Sima Qian, admitted that Huangdi, Zhuanxu <note>original: 'Tchuen-hio'</note>, Ku <note>original: 'Ti-ko'</note>, Yao, and Shun were 'the Five Rulers'; and that Suiren <note>original: 'Soni-giu-chi'</note>, Fuxi, and Shennong were 'the Three Sovereigns'."

While this fact is well-established, it must also be added that the presumed commitment <note>original: 'tenderness'</note> to historical truth, which is supposed to have influenced Sima Qian...

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

...to carefully select only the reliable traditions of Chinese history is not improved by the knowledge that all this research provides.

Sir George Lewis, in the work cited <note>original: op: cit:</note> on page 374, makes the following remarks regarding the Egyptian historian <term>Manetho</term> (who lived 306–247 B.C., about 150 years before <term>Sima Qian</term> <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien; the "Grand Historian" of the Han dynasty</note>) and <term>Berosus</term> (340–270 B.C.), the Chaldean historian. The work of Berosus, according to <term>Syncellus</term> <note>George Syncellus, a 9th-century Byzantine chronicler who preserved fragments of ancient histories</note>, was imitated by Manetho. These remarks are illustrative of Sima Qian’s real character as a historian:

> "Such are the unsolvable difficulties which arise respecting chronology when it is separated from history, passed down by conflicting authorities, and reduced to a mathematical puzzle. There is something attractive to a writer in this discretionary power of dealing with the history of bygone ages. His imagination is captured by the ability to create or destroy dynasties with a stroke of his magic pen; he becomes, in the language of the ancient astrologers, a <term>chronocrator</term> <note>original: chronocrator; Greek for 'ruler of time'</note>. He also appears to possess a sort of backward 'second sight,' by which he is able to look back into the unknown past and discern images invisible to ordinary eyes. He can call up a great middle period <note>original: medieval period</note> of antiquity which has hitherto been buried in oblivion. If his claims to these gifts are accepted, and if he succeeds in tricking the trust of his readers by his easy handling of subjects far removed from ordinary studies, he is regarded as a historical visionary <note>original: seer</note>, ranked far above those obscure record-keepers <note>original: chroniclers</note>,"

<summary>This page critiques the reliability of Sima Qian’s chronology by drawing parallels with ancient historians like Manetho and Berosus, who were accused of "creating" historical periods to fill chronological gaps.</summary>

> "...who occupy themselves with organizing the events
> of a well-ordered history."

It is also well known that the moderation shown by Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien; the "Grand Historian" of the Han Dynasty</note> was so unpopular with the Chinese people that his approach was rejected shortly after his death. Other writers pushed Chinese chronology even further back than Sima Qian had dared, in order to include more ancient, traditional figures in Chinese history.

Joseph-Anna-Maria de Moyriac de Mailla, in the work previously cited <note>original: op: cit:; referring to De Mailla’s "Histoire Générale de la Chine"</note> on page 24, describes how this occurred:

> "These figures were not restored immediately. For a time, historians followed the *Shiji* <note>original: Sse-ki; the "Records of the Grand Historian"</note> of Sima Qian, who began his records only with the Yellow Emperor <note>original: Hwang-ti</note> and left it to others to investigate the nation's origins. During the reign of Emperor Ming of Han <note>original: Han-ming-ti</note> (58 AD), Ban Biao <note>original: Pan-piao</note> was commissioned to fill in the gaps at the beginning of the *Shiji*. His son, Ban Gu <note>original: Pan-ku</note>, continued this work. He collaborated with Chen Zong <note>original: Tchin-tsang</note>, Jia Kui <note>original: Yu-may</note>, Fu Yi <note>original: Hong-ki</note>, and other members of his historical commission <note>original: tribunal of history</note>. Together, they critically examined all previous historical work and supplied the missing information for the beginning of Sima Qian’s history."

The ancient traditions that Sima Qian had rejected were thus officially and popularly validated shortly after his death. This fact alone proves how deeply these stories had taken root in the minds of the Chinese people. It also suggests that these traditions must have been passed down to them by other nations at an even earlier time...

<summary>This page explains how later Han Dynasty historians like Ban Biao and Ban Gu reversed Sima Qian's cautious historical approach by restoring mythical figures and ancient traditions to the official Chinese record.</summary>
<keywords>Sima Qian, Shiji, Ban Gu, Ban Biao, Emperor Ming of Han, Yellow Emperor, Chinese chronology, De Mailla, historiography</keywords>

The discussion regarding these ancient figures and the chronological period mentioned above was not, however, considered finally settled at that time. It will be seen that in various succeeding eras, the *Ju-kiao* <note>original: Ju-kiao; the official class of Confucian literati or scholars</note> or official scholars continued to assert their firm determination to reject them as unhistorical.

De Mailla mentions, after the paragraph quoted from his work, that Liu Ru <note>original: Lieou-ju; possibly Lü Zuqian (Lü-tsu-kieu), a contemporary of Zhu Xi (Chu-hi) from 1137–1181 AD, or Liu Shu, as mentioned in Mayers’ Chinese Reader’s Manual</note>:

> "who lived during the great Song <note>original: Sung</note> dynasty, expressed his surprise that such a question about these ancient personages could have ever occupied the attention of capable people. 'Where,' he writes, 'can there be found in the *Classics* <note>original: King; refers to the "Jing" or Confucian canonical texts</note>, or in the books of Confucius, the words *San-hwang* (the Three Sovereigns) or the *Wu-ti* (the Five Rulers)? It may possibly be true that the first written mention of these traditions occurred in the works of the authors mentioned; but the fact that they previously existed in China is...

It is evident that these legends were also derived from sources other than the Taoists just mentioned.

Liu Shu <note>original: Liu-ju; referring to Liu Shu (1032–1078 AD), a historian and collaborator of Sima Guang</note>, mentioned above, was the author of the <term>Wai-ji</term> <note>original: Wai-ki; "Outer Records" or "Supplementary Records," a work covering prehistoric and legendary Chinese history</note>, or the history of the fabulous period. This work serves as the introduction to Sima Guang's <note>original: Sze-ma-kwang; a prominent historian of the Song Dynasty</note> history, which formed the basis for the work by De Mailla <note>Joseph-Marie de Mailla (1669–1748), a Jesuit missionary who translated the "Great History of China" into French</note>. In this work, many Taoist traditions are recorded, and they seem to have found supporters among Chinese writers ever since. De Mailla, in the work cited <note>original: op: cit:; abbreviation for "opere citato," meaning "in the work cited"</note> on page 49, states that Yuan Huang <note>original: Yuen-hwang</note>, who compiled the <term>Gang-jian</term> <note>original: Kang-kien; an abridged or simplified history</note> (an abridgement of the <term>Jian-yi-shi</term> <note>original: Kien-y-sse</note>) in 1590 AD, upheld these traditions. The opinions of this author are best understood by consulting De Mailla’s account of that book.

It is well known that at a later period, the Chinese incorporated several Indian traditions into their history and adopted Indian customs and modes of thinking. Therefore, it is not at all inconsistent with their practices that they might have also adopted Babylonian traditions at an earlier date without acknowledging their source. 

The details above represent the entire stock of historical resources available at the time of Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-tsien; the "Grand Historian" (c. 145–86 BC) whose "Records of the Grand Historian" established the framework for Chinese history</note> for the construction of the Chinese system of chronology. It is therefore evident that he did not represent a unanimous or authoritative view of ancient Chinese times. Ever since his time, there has been a feeling that the traditions he excluded were entitled to better treatment. Besides the archives and traditions which were used in compiling his history,

## CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien; c. 145–86 BC, a Han dynasty official often called the "Grand Historian" of China</note> also used astronomical calculations to establish some of his chronological eras; this will be considered in the sections that follow.

<margin>X</margin>
Despite all this, however, Sima Qian did not think that he should admit any chronological certainty before 841 BC. Consequently, he only considered the cycle of 60 years <note>The sexagenary cycle, a traditional Chinese method of numbering years using a combination of two sets of characters</note> as reliable from that time onward. One may therefore conclude that all the narratives of the times and events before that date, which he placed in his history, rested only on the previously mentioned traditions. These traditions really had no special claim over others to be selected and preferred as the only ones deserving of credit.

It will be seen further on that the eras corresponding with our own calendar—in which, according to his theory, Sima Qian placed Huangdi <note>original: Hwang-ti; the legendary "Yellow Emperor"</note>, whom he called the first sovereign of China, and also the Emperor Yao—are incorrect. But as this is not a matter for present consideration, such inaccuracies in his history are not noticed here beyond this brief remark. Frérêt <note>original: Frérêt; Nicolas Frérêt (1688–1749), a French scholar and historian</note>, in volume 13, pages 303–304, says Sima Qian based his chronology on the date of Confucius's birth, which he placed in 557 BC.

> "Then he counted 500 years
> back to the Duke of Zhou <note>original: Chow-kung</note>, brother of King Wu <note>original: Wu-wang</note>. He did
> not found his system on astronomical coincidences and
> periods. This method of discovering and assigning
> dates to events originated with Liu Xin <note>original: Liu-hin; a scholar and astronomer of the Han Dynasty</note>, who used the
> *Spring and Autumn Annals* <note>original: Chun Tsiu</note> and the *Zuo Zhuan* <note>original: Tso-chuan</note>, with their 36 eclipses
> of the sun and some winter solstices."

Frérêt (volume 13, page 263) also says: "Ma Duanlin <note>original: Ma-twan-lin; a 13th-century Chinese historian and encyclopedist</note> observes that neither..."

### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

> "Neither Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-tsien; the Han Dynasty 'Grand Historian'</note> nor the ancient commentators of the *Shujing* <note>original: Shoo-king; the *Classic of History*, one of the Five Classics of Chinese literature</note> during the Han Dynasty seem to have seen the complete copy of the *Shangshu* by Kong Anguo <note>original: Kung Ngan-kwo; a Han dynasty scholar and descendant of Confucius</note> (that is, the version of the *Classic of History* published by that author). Furthermore, the work of Zuo Qiuming <note>original: Tso-chi</note>, known as the *Zuo Zhuan* <note>original: Tso-chuan; an ancient narrative history and commentary</note>, had not been published during the time of Sima Qian; consequently, he was ignorant of certain details of ancient history recorded in that book. It is the *Shiben* <note>original: Chipen; an early Chinese genealogical work</note> from which Sima Qian drew the list of emperors since Huangdi <note>original: Hwang-ti; the legendary 'Yellow Emperor'</note>, and according to Xu Fa <note>original: Siu-fa; a 17th-century scholar</note>, this book is apocryphal <note>meaning its authenticity or truthfulness is doubtful</note>."

We must now turn our attention to how the currently accepted chronology of China was established after Sima Qian finished his work. It will become clear that its primary foundation rested on the astronomical calculations and eras mentioned previously.

It is interesting to observe how this was accomplished. It could be said to have begun with Sima Qian, who, according to Frérêt <note>original: Freret; Nicolas Frérêt (1688–1749), a French scholar who studied Chinese history</note> in the work cited on page 35:

> "made the *Taichu* calendar <note>original: Tai-tsou; the 'Grand Inception' calendar established in 104 BC</note> the basis of his chronology (though its authority is disputed). He took as its starting point a winter solstice combined with a <term>syzygy</term> <note>the alignment of the sun, moon, and earth, specifically a new moon</note> occurring six hours before midnight on the first day of a supposed *Jiazi* <note>original: Kia-tze; the first year in the 60-year sexagenary cycle</note> cycle. He assumed this occurred after another solstice on December 24, 124 <term>BC</term>, which was 19 years earlier according to that cycle."

<summary>This page details the limitations of Sima Qian's historical sources, noting that he lacked access to key texts like the Zuo Zhuan, and explains how he used the Taichu calendar and astronomical cycles to calculate early Chinese dates.</summary>
<keywords>Sima Qian, Shujing, Zuo Zhuan, Shiben, Huangdi, Taichu calendar, syzygy, sexagenary cycle, Chinese chronology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Chinese chronology, focusing on how early historians used astronomical events—specifically the alignment of the winter solstice and the lunar cycle—to establish their calendars.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The Chinese consider the convergence of the winter solstice and the syzygy <term>the alignment of the sun, moon, and earth, occurring at a new or full moon</term> at midnight on a day called *Jiazi* <note>original: Kia-tze; the first day of the Chinese sixty-day cycle</note> to be a favorable omen. This is why Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien</note> selected a date that met these requirements—specifically, 105 BC. However, as Frérêt <note>original: Freret</note> demonstrates in the work previously cited (pages 37–38), Sima Qian's calculations were inaccurate; his timeline was therefore built on a fictitious foundation. It was a theory of Mencius that the very first sixty-day cycle began at the midnight of a winter solstice that coincided with a syzygy.

<margin>x</margin>
Starting a chronological period at the winter solstice was also a practice used in India; the Chinese have no exclusive claim to being the originators of this method. In the *Shujing* <note>original: Shoo-king; the Classic of History</note>, the legendary Emperor Yao provides rules for calculating the solstice in his time. Since the solar year began on that day, the primary objective was to determine the exact date of that solstice. This necessity led to various theories for discovering its true date and resulted in the different dates historically assigned to it.

<margin>x x</margin>
The most ancient observation of the winter solstice that the Chinese claim to have recorded is that of the Duke of Zhou <note>original: Chow-kung</note>, the brother of King Wu <note>original: Wu-wang</note>, who was the founder of the Zhou <note>original: Chow</note> dynasty (1104–1098 BC). However, the precise date of this observation is not recorded in the *Zhou Li* <note>original: Chow-li; the Rites of Zhou</note>, even though the details of the observation are described there. The most ancient observation of the solstice where the specific year and the day of the sixty-day cycle are both determined is December 25, 656 BC. According to the *Zuo Zhuan* <note>original: Tso-chuan; an ancient narrative history</note>, this occurred in the fifth year of Duke Xi <note>original: Hi-coung</note>, Prince of Lu <note>original: Hou; likely a reference to the State of Lu</note>. However, Frérêt (Volume 14, page 34) shows that...

It highlights the influence of Mencius's theories and notes that the earliest reliably dated solstice observation occurred in 656 BC.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese Chronology, Sima Qian, Winter Solstice, Syzygy, Jiazi cycle, Shujing, Duke of Zhou, Zuo Zhuan, Nicolas Frérêt</keywords>

This calculation is not exact, as the event actually occurred that year on December 28th. There is also another similar observation mentioned for December 25th, 523 BC, which is also incorrect. No other solstice observations are recorded until the Han dynasty (206 BC); however, Fréret <note>original: Freret</note>, in the work previously cited <note>original: op. cit.</note> on page 35, shows how the Chinese themselves admitted that they could not then find the ancient methods of astronomical calculation. They strangely assert, however, that they could find all the other traditions of their country, a great part of which were embodied by Sima Qian <note>original: Sse-ma-ts'ien</note> in his *Shiji* <note>original: Sse-ki; "Records of the Grand Historian"</note>.

This is confirmed by Souciet <note>original: Souciet</note> in the work previously cited, Volume II, page 2:

> “The European astronomy put in order by the Kangxi Emperor <note>original: Kang-hi</note> and published a few years ago, certifies that at the time of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: Tsin-shi-hwang-ti</note>, the Chinese had lost the method taught by the ancient Chinese—and especially that of Emperor Yao—for the calculation of the planets and fixed stars. What is stated there by the Emperor Kangxi is taken as certain by the authors who have worked on astronomy from the time of the Han dynasty onward. Thus, at the time of Qin Shi Huang, there were neither skilled astronomers, nor books of astronomy, nor any known method. There only remained some confused traditions and the fragments of some hidden books.”

Fréret, in Volume XIII, page 310, in a chapter on the variations which have existed among Chinese scholars <note>original: savants</note> on the subject of their historical chronology, states:

> “Some years after Sima Qian, Liu Xin <note>original: Liu-hin</note> (66 BC)...”

<margin>🞩</margin>
> "The author of the *Santong* <note>original: San-tong; refers to the "Triple Concordance" calendar system</note>, by examining the *Spring and Autumn Annals* <note>original: Tchun-tsieu</note> and the *Commentary of Zuo* <note>original: Tso-chi</note> in 66 BC (according to Gaubil’s *History of Astronomy* <note>original: histoire de l'astronomie</note>, page 7), undertook to fix the chronology of certain historical epochs which he supposed were also astronomical epochs. However, these determinations, which are based upon false astronomical hypotheses, have a great defect: the epochs in question do not actually possess the astronomical characteristics that Liu Xin <note>original: Liu-hin</note> attributes to them."

Souciet, in the work previously cited, Volume II, page 9, writing of Liu Xin, states:
> "It is not necessary for me to remark here on the errors in calculation that Liu Xin, Luo Xiaohong <note>original: Lo-hia-hong</note>, and others would have made, had they undertaken merely to calculate the winter solstice or any other of the *Jieqi* <note>original: Tsie-ki; the twenty-four solar terms of the traditional Chinese calendar</note> 2,000 years before their own time."

And at page 13:
> "These astronomers have lost much time in examining the numbers which Confucius calls the numbers of heaven and earth. The numbers of heaven are 1, 3, 5, and 9. The square of 9 being 81, this <margin>🞩</margin> last number was taken for the parts into which the day was divided. The period of 19 years <note>This is the Metonic cycle, where 19 solar years roughly equal 235 lunar months.</note> was also used, and by multiplying these together <note>81 parts multiplied by 19 years</note>, a period of 1,539 years was formed. This period was called *Tong* <note>original: Tong; meaning "beginning" or "concordance"</note>, and three of these *Tong* made 4,617 years, or the period called *Yuen* <note>original: Yuen; meaning "origin" or "primary cycle"</note>." <margin>🞩</margin>

Liu Xin seems to have been under the influence of the numerical hallucinations <note>The author uses this term to describe what he views as an irrational obsession with numerology in ancient Chinese scholarship.</note> of his people <note>original: race</note>, for he is said to have been the first author of the modern imaginary periods found in Chinese chronological works. # THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

The primary period he invented and presented as a method for calculating historical eras in his book, the *Santong* <note>original: San Tong; the "Triple Concordance" calendar system</note>, consisted of 144,127 solar years. It was said to represent the time from the *Shangyuan* <note>original: Chang-yuen; the "Grand Epoch" or "Great Beginning" in Chinese calendrics</note> to the midnight that marked the winter solstice on the first day of the eleventh moon in the year 104 BC. It is a remarkable fact that Chinese astronomers actually followed this extraordinary method as a model for their eras until AD 1280. This fact makes it quite evident what kind of chronologist Liu Xin <note>original: Liu-hin</note> was, and how little his book is worth.

It is interesting to note that Liu Xin and all the Chinese scholars who attempted to revise or shape their national chronology based all their calculations on astronomical periods or events, just as Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien</note> had used a conveniently timed winter solstice for the same purpose.

Now, even a brief look at these periods shows how unscientific they are when compared with the so-called "old Taoist periods." Yet, these former periods were the pure invention of the orthodox literati <note>The scholarly and official class of imperial China</note>, though they better deserve to be called fantasies than the Taoist periods do.

<margin>X</margin>
After Liu Xin came Ban Gu <note>original: Pan-ku</note> in AD 92 (see Mayers <note>Likely William Frederick Mayers (1831–1878), author of *The Chinese Reader’s Manual*</note>), the compiler of the chronicles of the Western Han dynasty, which served as a continuation of Sima Qian's history. Freret <note>Nicolas Freret (1688–1749), a French scholar known for his work on Chinese history</note> says of him that "he provides a complete system of chronology from Yao <note>A legendary ancient Chinese ruler</note> to the Han dynasty in 206 BC. He places the Qin <note>original: Tsin</note> dynasty at 255 BC, the beginning..."

<summary>This page critiques the astronomical and chronological systems of Liu Xin and Ban Gu, arguing that their mathematical "epochs" were unscientific inventions of the orthodox scholarly class rather than reliable historical records.</summary>

> “...of the Zhou <note>original: Chows</note> dynasty at 1122 BC, the Shang dynasty at 2183 BC, and the Emperor Yao at 2303 BC. However, he went no further back in time than Yao, and so he differed from his predecessor Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien</note>. Ma Duanlin <note>original: Matwanlin; a famous Chinese encyclopedist (1245–1322)</note>, the learned Chinese archaeologist who lived from the end of the Song <note>original: Sung</note> dynasty to the Mongolian Yuan <note>original: Yuen</note> dynasty in AD 1316, wrote about Ban Gu <note>original: Panku</note> in his great work called *The Comprehensive Examination of Literature* <note>original: the antiquarian researches; the *Wenxian Tongkao*</note> (volume 70, chapter 191, pages 15 & 29, quoted by Nicolas Fréret in that place <note>original: in loco</note>). He describes Ban Gu as a writer whose greatest merit lies in his literary style, but who lacks deep scholarship or critical judgment, so that entire confidence cannot be placed in what he relates.”

And yet, Fréret remarks, it is this very chronology by Ban Gu—which is not worthy of full confidence—that has been used as the foundation for all the systems proposed ever since. These later systems have only made slight changes to Ban Gu’s original chronological hypotheses.

Father Gaubil <note>original: Pere Gaubil; Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a Jesuit missionary and astronomer in China</note>, in his *History of Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: histoire de l'astronomie Chinoise</note> (found in volume II, page 22 of *Mathematical, Astronomical, and Chronological Observations* <note>original: observations mathematiques, astronomiques chronologiques</note> by Étienne Souciet), says the following regarding Ban Gu:

> “Ban Gu, the imperial historian who held a great reputation under the emperors Zhangdi and He Di <note>original: Tchang-ti and Hoti; Han dynasty emperors reigning in the late 1st century AD</note>, adopted the system of the *Ji* <note>original: Ki; a period of 1,520 years</note> and the *Bu* <note>original: Pou; a period of 76 years</note>. Having arranged his historical eras by examining the historical memoirs he possessed, he carefully marked the 103rd year of the Shang dynasty as the first year of the *Ji* cycle. He discovered...”

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

> "[Ban Gu made] either a calculation or an observation of a winter solstice on the day *Jia-chen* <note>original: Kia-chin; the 41st day of the 60-day cycle</note> at the moment of midnight and at the moment of the conjunction of the sun and moon in the year 123 BC. By calculating further back for 1,420 years, he found according to that method that 1,520 years before the midnight of the winter solstice in the year 123 BC, the solstice was also on the day *Jia-chen* at midnight and at the moment of the conjunction of the sun and moon. He found that this *Ji* <term>original: Ki; a large calendrical cycle</term> fell in the '108th year of the Shang dynasty.' On page 37, Gaubil continues: 'one can see clearly that at the time of Ban Gu <note>original: Pan-ku</note>, the art of calculating the exact moment of observing the solstice was unknown. It is clear that the system of Ban Gu is false.'"
<margin>X</margin>

This testimony from Father Gaubil <note>original: Pere Gaubil; Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a Jesuit missionary and astronomer</note> may be accepted as an impartial record of how Ban Gu and the Chinese chronologists worked out their dates using such imaginary eras as the *Bu* <term>original: Pou; a calendrical unit of 76 years</term> and the *Ji* of Li Fang <note>original: Li-fang; a Han dynasty calendar reformer</note>, the author of a book called the *Sifen* <term>original: Sse-fen; literally "Four Parts" or "Four Quarters," referring to the Four-Quarter Calendar</term>—or at least the eras said to have been invented by him. However, the *Bu* of 76 years is evidently the period of Callippus <note>a Greek astronomer who refined the Metonic cycle in the 4th century BC</note> and was most probably made known to the Chinese by Western travelers.
<margin>X</margin>

As previously noted, Ban Gu placed the era of Emperor Yao at 2303 BC, whereas Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts‘ien</note> had placed him at 2145 BC. Consequently, there was a difference of over 150 years between them on that single date.

<summary>This page critiques the chronological systems of the historian Ban Gu, highlighting Father Gaubil's argument that Ban Gu's dates were based on flawed astronomical calculations and cycles likely borrowed from Greek astronomers like Callippus.</summary>
<keywords>Ban Gu, Father Gaubil, Sima Qian, Chinese Chronology, Sifen Calendar, Callippus, Emperor Yao, Shang Dynasty, astronomical cycles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chinese historical dating, moving from the astronomical errors of Ban Gu to the specific challenges of establishing the dates for the legendary Emperor Yao.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Determining the date of Emperor Yao has always been considered a crucial requirement in Chinese chronology. This is because, according to the *Shujing* <note>original: Shoo king; the "Classic of History," one of the Five Classics of ancient Chinese literature</note>, he gave the Chinese people their first concepts of astronomy, and also because Confucius never mentions any ruler of China prior to Yao.
<margin>X</margin>

Ban Gu <note>original: Pan-ku; a 1st-century AD historian and author of the "Book of Han"</note> acknowledges that others had placed Yao at 2132 BC rather than 2303 BC—nearly 200 years closer to our era than his own calculation. However, as Freret <note>Nicolas Freret (1688–1749), a French scholar known for his work on chronology</note> remarks in that passage <note>original: in loco</note>, Ban Gu provided no reason for his own view on the matter. Freret (Volume 13, page 139) states that there are eleven different opinions or theories regarding the dates of Yao, and their divergence spans a period of 284 years. He notes that the matter was not authoritatively settled to the satisfaction of the official scholar-bureaucrats <term>original: literati</term>, or adopted by the Chinese Bureau of History and Mathematics, until AD 1068. At that time, Sima Guang <note>original: Sze-ma-kwang; a famous Song Dynasty historian and statesman</note> produced the system of chronology that is currently recognized.

The era of Yao has recently been brought back into prominence by Dr. Legge <note>James Legge (1815–1897), a famous Scottish missionary and sinologist</note> in his introduction to Volume III of the 1879 Oxford edition of the *Sacred Books of the East*. In that work, an attempt was made to establish the accuracy of the date using a stellar chart prepared by the Reverend Charles Pritchard, the Savilian Professor of Astronomy at that university. From this chart, an inference was drawn in favor of the traditional Chinese chronological system.

A careful reading of Dr. Legge’s statements, however, does not reveal any new support for the theoretical...

Dr. Legge <note>James Legge (1815–1897), a famous Scottish missionary and sinologist</note>, in the work already cited <note>original: op. cit.; short for "opere citato," meaning "in the work cited"</note>, page 24, gives the names and supposed positions of certain stars mentioned in the "Canon of Yao" in the *Shujing* <note>original: Shoo-king; the "Classic of History," one of the Five Classics of ancient Chinese literature</note>. The strength of his reasoning regarding these astronomical data primarily relies on a new illustration—a chart showing the appearance of the heavens at the supposed date of Yao. From this chart, he attempts to establish a coincidence in time as a specific era in Chinese chronology.

However, this picture of the starry heavens, brought forward as a new witness in the case, does not provide any special evidence to identify China as the only place where the star positions described in the "Canon of Yao" could have been observed. According to the chart, the same alignment of the stars could have been seen "at any hour of the day above the horizon of any place in central China" (see page 27). Therefore, all along the same degree of latitude through central Asia, this same position of the stars—with an interval of a whole day as they pass the horizon—could have been visible at sunset at different places and at different astronomical times, depending on their longitude. Consequently, the conditions required for the chart's accuracy are lacking when it is used to identify the star positions in the *Shujing* specifically with an observation made in China.

The directions given for observing certain stars in China, which are attributed to Yao, could have been given just as easily by the sovereign of Balkh <note>An ancient city in modern-day Afghanistan, often associated with early Central Asian civilizations</note> in the twenty-fourth

<summary>The author argues that Dr. James Legge's use of astronomical charts to date the Emperor Yao's reign is flawed, because the star positions described in ancient texts could have been observed anywhere along the same latitude in Central Asia, not just in China.</summary>

> "It has *always* been assumed by Chinese scholars that when <term>Yao</term> <note>A legendary ancient Chinese ruler</note> said the star of mid-spring is in <term>Niao</term> <note>The "Bird" constellation, one of the four star groups mentioned in the "Canon of Yao"</note>, he meant the star reaching its highest point <term>culminating</term> at *dusk* during that season at the point of observation; and the same applies to the other stars."

> "When we wish to use the directions of <term>Yao</term> for the purpose of chronological research, the question that arises is this: When did the above-named stars reach their highest point <term>culminate</term> at dusk in China during the equinoxes and solstices?"

This assertion—that it has *always* been assumed by Chinese scholars that it was the <term>culmination</term> <note>the point at which a star reaches its highest altitude above the horizon</note> of those stars at *dusk* which was observed—cannot be verified for any period before the <term>Han dynasty</term> (206 <term>B.C.</term>). It is admitted that the Chinese at that time had forgotten all about their previous <term>astronomy</term>, and they have not since recovered any astronomical documents from more ancient times that would prove such knowledge had always existed in China before the <term>Han</term> period.

Moreover, according to <term>Gaubil</term> <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a French Jesuit missionary and astronomer in China</note> in his *Treatise on Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: "Traité de l'astronomie Chinoise"</note>, page 8:
> "The interpreters who wrote during the time of the <term>Hans</term> specifically assure us that it was at *six o'clock in the evening* that the various stars mentioned in the 'Canon of Yao' passed the <term>meridian</term> <note>the imaginary line passing through the sky directly above the observer from north to south</note> above the <term>horizon</term> during the era of <term>Yao</term>."

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

The question, therefore, is not as Dr. <term>Legge</term> <note>James Legge (1815–1897), a famous Scottish missionary and translator of Chinese classics</note> puts it:
> *Did the stars reach their highest point <term>culminate</term> at dusk?*

Dr. Legge also maintains (page 26) that the stars reaching their highest point at the <term>equinoxes</term> and <term>solstices</term> during the remote period of <term>Yao</term> <note>A legendary sage-king of ancient China, traditionally said to have reigned in the 24th century B.C.</note> could not have been scientifically calculated backward. From this hypothesis, he concludes that the compiler of the <term>Canon of Yao</term> <note>A chapter in the *Book of Documents* describing the astronomical missions ordered by Emperor Yao</note> must have taken the facts regarding the stars’ positions from ancient documents, possibly those written at the same time as the events they describe.

<margin>Why?</margin>
Now, assuming that the author of the <term>Shoo-king</term> <note>Now usually spelled *Shujing* or translated as the *Book of Documents*</note> had access to ancient astronomical documents, the question still remains whether these documents were Chinese. It is highly probable that the only astronomical documents available in the 24th century <term>B.C.</term> were Babylonian. As has already been observed, there were many traditions and fragments of Babylonian science circulating among the Chinese which belong to that period; these have been recorded by Chinese authors as being of native Chinese origin without acknowledging their true source.

Regarding the supposed impossibility of the Chinese scientifically calculating the positions of Yao’s stars backward—due to their ignorance of the <term>precession of the equinoxes</term> <note>The slow change in the orientation of Earth's rotational axis, which shifts the observed positions of stars over thousands of years</note> during Yao's time—it does not seem at all necessary for them to have known this in order to create a fictitious <term>epoch</term> <note>A significant period of time or a starting point in history</note>. There were plenty of existing data from similar observations of stars related to the equinoxes and solstices,

He challenges Dr. Legge's belief that these records must be contemporaneous with the events, suggesting they could have been adapted to create a fictitious historical timeline.</summary>

...existing in India. These records could have been copied and inserted into the *Shoo-king* <note>original: "Shoo-king"; now usually spelled *Shujing*, or the *Book of Documents*</note> to give it a misleading appearance of verifiable antiquity. It should be remembered that the astronomers of the Han dynasty <note>206 B.C. – 220 A.D.</note> who interpreted the *Yao-tien* <note>The "Canon of Yao," a chapter in the *Book of Documents*</note>—and who undoubtedly had a hand in inserting their own comments into it—were disciples of the Hindu astronomers, with whom they were in close connection. They could have learned from them the art of scientifically calculating past events backward and identifying them with specific astronomical phenomena.

It was a regular practice among the Hindu astronomers to create these retrospective dates and support them with accurate observations. However, these observations became fictitious when they were linked to imaginary historical events. Therefore, it is quite likely that this practice was introduced to China from India. The Hindu astronomers were well-acquainted with the precession of the equinoxes <note>The gradual shift in the orientation of Earth's axis, which changes the positions of stars over thousands of years</note> at that time. Even if the Chinese did not learn this directly from their Indian teachers, they could have easily reached the conclusions that enabled them to produce the retrospective stellar calculations they needed for their purpose.

John Bentley, in his book *Hindu Astronomy* (page 75), states:
> "Around this period (204 <term>B.C.</term>), in addition to these improvements, Hindu history was divided into periods for chronological purposes. To ensure these periods were never lost—or could be recovered with a few pieces of data if they were lost or disputed—they were fixed and settled by astronomical computations in the following way:—*The year by*..."

It is worth noting that at almost exactly the same time (204 <term>B.C.</term>) that the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note> invented the process of scientifically calculating past events and linking them with astronomical occurrences, the Han dynasty astronomers (206 <term>B.C.</term>) began their own experiments to improve Chinese astronomy and chronology. Inspired by the Hindu examples, they would have had no difficulty applying similar methods to establish any given period in Chinese history as a chronological foundation. This was especially true for the era of Yao <note>A legendary ancient Chinese ruler traditionally dated to the 3rd millennium B.C.</note>, which has been considered the primary historical milestone to be defined ever since their time. ...year cycle <note>The sexagenary cycle, a 60-year cycle used in China for recording time</note>, by which the Chinese assigned dates to their historical eras, is intimately connected with a conjunction <note>An alignment where two celestial bodies appear close together in the sky</note> of the planet **Jupiter**. There is a striking similarity between the Chinese and Hindu methods of establishing and fixing chronological periods.

If it is argued that the Chinese could not have performed these retroactive calculations by themselves, they might nevertheless have imitated the Hindus in such cases. However, they made only a clumsy use of the knowledge of Hindu theoretical principles of chronology. This is evident from the period of Yao <note>A legendary ancient Chinese ruler traditionally dated to the 3rd millennium B.C.</note>, which is so inexact that it almost betrays its having been compiled through mere imitation, without scientific data.

Dr. Chalmers <note>John Chalmers (1825–1899), a Scottish missionary and sinologist who studied ancient Chinese astronomy</note> so clearly shows the real unscientific character of the legend of the *Yao-tien* <note>The "Canon of Yao," the first chapter of the *Book of Documents*</note>, that his words are given here to complete the investigation of this subject.

> "In accordance with Chinese ideas of a sage, Yao makes it appear in a few pompous sentences that he is perfectly acquainted beforehand with the results of the observations which he orders his astronomers to make: 'You will find the star is in *niao* <note>original: "neaou"; likely referring to the "Bird" constellation, specifically stars in Hydra or Corvus used to determine the spring equinox</note>,' etc. But did they find the stars as Yao said they would find them? We are supposed to believe that they did, of course; but since we are not told, we claim the liberty to doubt. Suppose, for the sake of argument, that Yao, before the observations were made, was dependent on tradition for his knowledge, and that his astronomers were capable of making accurate observations; they would in that case have had to report some..."

<meta>This page continues Dr. John Chalmers' critique of the "Canon of Yao" (Yao-tien). ...failure to verify his statements. But apart from this, we can confidently state that three of the men sent to the four borders of China could not have seen the stars that occupied the equinox and solstice points <note>The positions in the sky where the sun reaches its furthest northern or southern point (solstices) or crosses the celestial equator (equinoxes)</note> reaching their highest point <note>original: culminating</note> on the evenings mentioned. For example, the beginning of the constellation Libra could not be seen reaching its highest point at dusk when the sun is at the start of Cancer; this is because it would have to reach that peak at 6:00 p.m., whereas the sun would not set in any part of China in midsummer much before 7:00 p.m., and the stars would not be visible for at least half an hour after sunset. 

This last fact would equally prevent the observers from seeing their stars reach their highest point during the equinoxes—unless, of course, the time of observation was several centuries later than the date usually assigned to Yao <note>original: Yaou; a legendary emperor traditionally dated 2356–2255 B.C.</note>, so that the stars being observed were no longer exactly aligned with the solstitial colure <note>The great circle on the celestial sphere that passes through the celestial poles and the two solstices</note>. 

The astronomer who went to the *north* in winter is the only one who would have faced no such difficulty. He might have seen his star long before it reached its highest point. However, unless he had an accurate clock, he could not have known that it reached that peak at exactly 6:00 p.m. During the course of a long winter evening, he would sadly lose his track of time. Furthermore, the water clock <note>original: clepsydra</note>, assuming he even had one, might have been frozen solid. In every way, the observation could have been performed more conveniently at a central station than at the northern border.

...has been much overrated. According to the obvious interpretation of the text, Yao <note>original: Yaon; likely a transcription error for Yao, the legendary emperor</note> had reason to expect the stars he mentioned to be in the equinoctial and solstitial colures <note>The great circles on the celestial sphere that pass through the celestial poles and either the equinoxes or the solstices</note>. But what his reason was is left to speculation. It might have been personal observation, or it might have been tradition passed down from his great-grandfather, or even from Noah himself.” See Chalmers’ *Astronomy of the Ancient Chinese*, page 92.

Returning to Chinese authors, it should be remembered, however, that Sima Guang’s <note>original: Sze-ma-kwang; a prominent Song dynasty historian</note> system did not meet with full approval. For Liu-shu—who, according to Mayers, was associated with him in his historical work (or according to De Mailla, it was Liu-tao-yuen)—“gathered up all that Sima Guang had wisely <note>original: judiciously</note> rejected, and worked out Chinese history back to the times of Pangu <note>original: Pan-ku; in Chinese mythology, the first sentient being and the creator of the world</note>, the first ruler of the world after Chaos. He compiled the *Tongjian Waiji* <note>original: Tong Kien Wai-Ki; translated as "The Outer Record of the Comprehensive Mirror," covering legendary history</note>, or the history of the legendary period, which is prefixed to Sima Guang’s annals.” 

The *Waiji* divided Chinese chronology into two parts: the “certain” period, beginning in 827 B.C., and the “uncertain” period, beginning 3,679 years earlier—stretching as far back as 4346 B.C., to Pangu and the origin of the world from Chaos. Another contemporary author named Lopi <note>original: Lo-pi</note>, in his work *Lushi* <note>original: Lon-sse; a history of ancient China by Luo Mi</note>, does not go further back than the beginning of the Xia <note>original: Hia</note> dynasty.

Gustav Schlegel, in his *Chinese Uranography* <note>original: Uranographie Chinoise; a study of Chinese constellations and astronomy</note>, page 752, says that “Lopi was one of the most learned and hard-working antiquarians of China.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historiography of early Chinese chronology, following the work of Pangu (the first ruler in legendary history) and Sima Guang's efforts to organize the timeline of ancient China.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

The next step in the process of forming the chronological system of China occurred about 150 years after Pangu <note>original: Pan-ku; the creator figure in Chinese mythology</note> had finished his chronological arrangement of Chinese history. In A.D. 265, a historical work was discovered which is known as the *Bamboo Annals* <note>The *Bamboo Annals* are ancient chronicles of China written on bamboo slips, discovered in a tomb in the 3rd century AD</note>; this work was used to revise the chronological system once again. At present, the only remark of interest concerning this book is that it places the beginning of Yao’s <note>A legendary sage-emperor</note> reign 158 years later than Pangu had done; this certainly did not strengthen the previously accepted chronology regarding this crucial date in Chinese history.

Shortly after the discovery of the *Bamboo Annals*, "a celebrated scholar and commentator on the ancient classical and historical writings" (see Mayers, *Chinese Reader’s Manual*, page 69) named Huangfu Mi <note>original: Hwang-fu-mi; a physician and scholar of the Jin dynasty</note> published a chronology of Chinese history, which is noted by Ma Duanlin <note>original: Ma-twan-lin; a famous 13th-century encyclopedist</note> and also by Xiu Fa <note>original: Siu-fa</note> (see Wylie). This author, Huangfu Mi, places the era of Yao (see Freret in that place <note>original: in loco</note>) about 180 years later than the *Bamboo Annals* do; so here was yet another disagreement regarding this date. Besides this, he extended the chronology not only back to Huangdi <note>original: Hwang-ti; the Yellow Emperor</note>, as Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien; the "Grand Historian" of China</note> had done, but as far back as Fuxi <note>original: Fu-hi; the first of the Three Sovereigns of ancient China</note>. He placed Fuxi 760 years before Huangdi and 1,127 years before Yao, which was another view different from that of Sima Qian.

After this, there were no more chronologists of importance until A.D. 724, during the Tang dynasty, when the monk <note>original: bonze; a term for a Buddhist monk</note> Yi Xing <note>original: Y-hang; a Tang dynasty astronomer and mathematician</note> (according to Frérêt) set about regulating Chinese chronology by certain astronomical...

> and his own time, the fixed stars had advanced
> nearly 36 degrees. He had fixed the winter solstice
> for the year <term>AD</term> 724, and from that he concluded that
> at the time of Yao, the winter solstice corresponded
> to the first degree of the constellation Xu <note>original: Hiu; a star group in the 'Empty' mansion of the Chinese zodiac</note>.” 

In a note on the same page, Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a French Jesuit missionary and astronomer in China</note> writes: 

> “Yi Xing <note>original: Y-hang; a famous Tang dynasty astronomer and Buddhist monk</note> was evidently
> mistaken about the movement of the precession of the
> fixed stars and in his determination of the winter solstice
> for <term>AD</term> 724. He could not help, therefore, being
> mistaken about the solstice at the time of Yao.”

Gaubil further states that by examining the eclipses mentioned in the *Spring and Autumn Annals* <note>original: Chun-ts'iu</note> and the *Classic of Poetry* <note>original: Shi-king</note>, Yi Xing concluded that the year <term>BC</term> 2128 corresponded to...

<meta>This page continues the evaluation of Yi Xing’s astronomical and chronological calculations, specifically focusing on his errors and the possibility of cross-cultural knowledge between ancient China and the Near East.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

...the fifth year of Zhong Kang <note>original: Tchong-kang; a king of the Xia dynasty</note>, and from that he concluded that the Xia <note>original: Hia</note> dynasty reigned for 432 years, and the Shang dynasty for 628 years.

We may assume, therefore, that Yi Xing <note>original: Y-hang; a Tang dynasty Buddhist monk and astronomer</note> was no more accurate in determining these chronological eras than he was regarding the time of Yao <note>A legendary sage-emperor</note>.

Yi Xing made a significant error in predicting two eclipses that did not happen at the times he predicted. To justify himself, he published a book in which he claimed his calculations were correct but that Heaven had altered the usual laws governing the movement of celestial bodies.

In a note on this event on page 37 of the previously cited work <note>original: *op : cit :* (opere citato)</note>, Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a Jesuit missionary and scholar of Chinese astronomy</note> suggests that Yi Xing might have known about and referred to similar events, such as the sun standing still in the time of Joshua <note>original: Joshue</note> and Hezekiah <note>original: Ezechias; biblical figures associated with solar miracles</note>. He further states that Huainanzi <note>original: Hwai-nan-tsze</note> (also known as Liu An <note>original: Liu-ngan</note>; see Mayers, page 132), who died in 122 <term>BC</term>, "seems to have known about these two great events," and that Father <note>original: Pere</note> d'Entrecolles had provided many details regarding this passage in Liu An's book.

Mayers remarks that Liu An "was a devoted follower of the mystical research of the Taoists" and also notes "his belief in the supernatural."

His writings were preserved through the efforts of Liu Xiang <note>original: Liu-hang; a Han dynasty scholar</note> between 80 and 9 <term>BC</term>.

This observation by Father Gaubil is intriguing, as it suggests that the Chinese during Liu An's time—and particularly the Taoists—might have been familiar with the Hebrew Scriptures or their traditions.

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Tang dynasty astronomer Yi Xing (Y-hang), moving from his astronomical errors to the vastly inflated timelines found in his chronological calculations, as well as subsequent attempts by Song dynasty scholars to fix the date of the legendary Emperor Yao's reign.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Yi Xing <note>original: Y-hang; a famous Buddhist monk and astronomer of the Tang dynasty</note> seems to have taken the eclipse of 724 <term>AD</term> as the starting point of his chronological calculations. One of the conclusions he deduced from it is that from the midnight preceding that event back to the conjunction of the planets at midnight at the *Zhangyuan* <note>original: Chang Yuen; likely referring to the "Grand Origin" or a primary calendrical epoch</note>, there were 969,601,740 solar years. This fact alone would be enough to show what should be thought of the reliability of Yi Xing's chronology. He died in 727 <term>AD</term>.

Rather more than 250 years passed after the death of Yi Xing before any further revisions of Chinese chronology occurred. In 996 <term>AD</term>, during the reign of Emperor Taizong <note>original: Tai-tsong</note> of the Song <note>original: Sung</note> dynasty, the work of regulating the national chronology was resumed, specifically regarding the first year of the reign of Yao <note>A legendary sage-emperor</note>. In order to determine that era, the Emperor ordered a catalogue to be made of all the different proposed dates for that first year. In the memorial presented to the Emperor on the subject, it was declared to be 2326 <term>BC</term>; however, in the same memorial, an inscription from the 3rd century of our era was quoted, which attempted to show that Yao began his reign in 2411 <term>BC</term>. 

The tribunal, however, decided on the year 2331 <term>BC</term>, which was 28 years earlier than the date set by Ban Gu <note>original: Pan-koo; a famous historian of the 1st century AD</note>, which was 2303 <term>BC</term>. About 200 years after this, fresh chronological research was again conducted to determine this date for Yao. In 1204 <term>AD</term>, the astronomers determined that it was 2337 <term>BC</term>, even though Sima Guang <note>original: Sze-ma-kuang; a high official and historian</note> had settled his system of chronology in 1086 and placed the first year of Yao at 2334 <term>BC</term> and that of Fu Xi <note>original: Fu-hi; a legendary cultural hero</note> at 2816 <term>BC</term>.

<summary>This page discusses the unreliable, massive timescales calculated by the astronomer Yi Xing and details the conflicting dates proposed by various Song dynasty scholars for the reign of the legendary Emperor Yao.</summary>

...and Huangdi <note>original: Hwang-ti; the legendary "Yellow Emperor," a foundational figure in Chinese mythology and history</note> at 2582 BC. Nicolas Freret <note>A prominent 18th-century French scholar and chronologist</note> (in the work previously cited, page 323), remarks on this:

> “The variations among the chronologists who were contemporary with Sima Guang <note>original: Sze-ma-kwang; a high-ranking official and historian of the Song dynasty who wrote the comprehensive history 'Zizhi Tongjian'</note> regarding the precise date of the first year of Yao—although minor—are proof that this date was determined through reasoning or conjecture, rather than based on positive testimony.”

Freret (volume 13, page 139) describes the decision this way:

> “The uncertainties <note>original: incertitudes</note> regarding the chronology were finally ended by the publication of an authentic new collection of annals <note>Historical records or chronicles</note> in <term>AD</term> 1068, during the reign of the Emperor Taizong <note>original: Tae-tsong</note>.”

The date of 2337 BC, adopted by the astronomical chronologists in <term>AD</term> 1204 as mentioned above, was the one officially adopted by the tribunal of history and is generally accepted today. However, Freret (volume 13, pages 303-304) rightly observes that the chronology currently followed by the Hanlin Academy <note>original: Academy of Han Lin; an elite institution of scholars who performed secretarial and literary tasks for the imperial court</note> for the earliest ages of Chinese history has not always been unanimously accepted by Chinese scholars, nor is it even accepted now.

It is difficult to know what is or is not accepted as authentic by the Chinese literati. Freret (volume 13, page 118) says that Parrenin <note>Dominique Parrenin, a French Jesuit missionary to China</note> assured Mr. de Mairan <note>Jean-Jacques d'Ortous de Mairan, a French scientist and philosopher</note> in a letter that the existence of the nine sovereigns who came before <note>original: anterior to</note> Huangdi was then admitted in China as undeniable, even though it has been seen that these personages are only described in the traditions which...

Freret observes in the work previously cited <note>original: op : cit : 325</note>, page 325:
> “All this shows that at the very time when the chronology actually followed by the historical tribunal was received, the most capable and sensible among the Chinese—as Sima Guang undoubtedly was—regarded that chronology as a matter of guesswork and subject to great uncertainty. Thus, it is not surprising that, despite the approval given to that chronology by the tribunal and the majority of writers, a learned person should be found to attack it in an excellent work on Chinese literature published in 1561 under the title of *Tianyuan lili* <note>original: Tien-yuen-li-li; a work on astronomy and calendars</note>.”

> “The author of this book was Xu Fa <note>original: Siu-fa</note>, who maintains in his book that the chronology of the *Bamboo Annals* <note>An ancient chronicle of China discovered in a tomb in AD 281, which provides a different timeline than the standard histories</note> is the only one to be followed. He argues this because it is the only surviving document from the age preceding the destruction of the books by Qin Shi Huang <note>original: She-hwang-ti; the First Emperor of the Qin dynasty who ordered the 'Burning of Books' in 213 BC</note>. He observed that after the burning of the books, despite all the care of Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-tseen; the 'Grand Historian' of the Han dynasty</note>, no historical monuments were recovered which went back with certainty beyond 841 <term>BC</term>. He notes that Liu Xin <note>A Han dynasty astronomer and historian</note> and Ban Gu <note>The primary author of the 'Book of Han'</note> had no records other than those which Sima Qian had, and they were too bold in undertaking to fix the chronology of the times which Sima Qian had left undetermined. He adds that since the *Bamboo Annals* had not been discovered in their time, they only had the *Shiben* <note>original: Chi-pen; an early Chinese genealogical work</note> for the basis of their work—a work that was certainly ancient—”

> "[This work is] earlier than Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-tseen; the 'Grand Historian' of the Han dynasty</note>, but it contains many things of little certainty, which Sima Qian did not believe to be of any value. The chronologists after Ban Gu <note>original: Pan-ku; author of the *Book of Han*</note>, who used his work as the foundation for their own systems, have not increased its authority. Furthermore, the changes they made to it—based on their own calculations and various assumptions that were often opposed to one another—prove that they had no reliable principle for developing their chronology. He also carefully compared the inconsistencies and absurdities he noticed in their systems. He dates the first year of Yao <note>A legendary Chinese emperor and one of the Three Sovereigns and Five Emperors</note> to 2145 BC and that of Huangdi <note>original: Hwang-ti; the 'Yellow Emperor,' a mythical ancestor and cultural hero</note> to 2395 BC."

To finish this overview of how the system of Chinese chronology was formed and developed, I should mention that during the Ming dynasty, new efforts were made to correct the various eras of which it was composed. In AD 1573, Prince Zheng <note>original: T'ching; likely referring to Prince Zhu Zaiyu, a Ming prince famous for music theory and mathematics</note>, assisted by an astronomer named Xing Yunlu <note>original: Hing y-lon or Yun-lou</note>, conducted a thorough examination of Chinese chronology. See *Gaubil, History of Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: Hist: Astron: Chin:</note>, page 117.

Next came the influence of the Jesuits in China and their astronomical and chronological calculations. These calculations largely followed the dates provided by the Chinese officials who had preceded them in the Board of Mathematics <note>original: tribunal of mathematics; the imperial bureau responsible for the calendar and celestial observations</note>.

The combined result of all these efforts was the chronological structure built by the *Tongjian Gangmu* <note>original: Tong-kien-Kang-mu; the *Comprehensive Mirror in Aid of Governance, Digest*, a condensed version of Sima Guang's history reorganized by Zhu Xi</note>—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the influence of the Jesuits on Chinese astronomical and chronological calculations, specifically referring to the *Zizhi Tongjian Gangmu* (the "Gangmu"), a historical work revised during the Qing dynasty.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

[the Gang]mu, and officially approved by the Emperor Kangxi <note>original: Kang-hi; the fourth emperor of the Qing dynasty (reigned 1661–1722)</note> as the standard records of the antiquity of the Chinese nation.

The preceding remarks show to what extent this system deserves the confidence that the Chinese place in its accuracy; they will also, it is hoped, be sufficient to explain the sources and the methods used to form their chronology.

It cannot be questioned that "chronology depends on astronomical findings," as Sir George Cornewall Lewis <note>A 19th-century British statesman and scholar known for his skepticism regarding early historical records</note> most rightly observes in his work, which has been referenced in these pages. However, this rule only applies to the scientific use of astronomy as a way to measure time. It in no way supports the unscientific calculations of past eras <note>original: epochs</note> made by the Chinese as a valid method for developing a chronological history. The mere fact that Chinese chronology was formed by such unscientific calculations of past intervals of time, and not from contemporary monuments <note>original: synchronous monuments; records or structures created at the time the events occurred</note>, shows its unreliability. The Chinese can claim no exemption from the general rule stated by Sir George Cornewall Lewis in the work cited <note>original: op: cit: (opere citato); referring to *An Historical Survey of the Astronomy of the Ancients* (1862)</note> on page 358: "There is no example of history founded on contemporary records being reduced to mere chronology."

<summary>This page concludes an evaluation of Chinese chronology, arguing that its reliance on retroactive astronomical calculations rather than contemporary records makes it historically unreliable. It cites the scholar Sir George Cornewall Lewis to support the claim that genuine history must be based on contemporary registration.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese chronology, Emperor Kangxi, Sir George Cornewall Lewis, Zizhi Tongjian Gangmu, astronomical determination, historical reliability, synchronous monuments</keywords>

# CHAPTER III.

### *The Origin and Application of Chinese Cycles.*

Although the immediate purpose of this portion of the Chinese research is not to minutely investigate the truth of Chinese history, it is necessary to glance at the general historical situation in China—which these cycles have been used to support—before addressing the specific question of <term>chronological cycles</term><note>original: "chronological cycles"; likely referring to the Sexagenary cycle, a 60-year revolving system used to date events in Chinese history</note>, which are such an important element in defending its accuracy.

Moreover, these cycles must first be considered in terms of their historical relationship to the system itself. Although their inherent origin will naturally be discussed in this chapter, certain preliminary questions require attention as a foundation for the inquiry.

The cycles, the chronology, and the history of the Chinese are so intertwined that anyone researching the truth of one will find that the others are equally involved in the structure <note>original: "edifice"</note> they have all helped to build. Therefore, any isolated or abstract examination into the reliability of any part of the chronological system would be incomplete.

<vocab>Chinese cycles, Chinese history, chronology, chronological cycles</vocab>

<summary>This chapter introduction establishes that Chinese history, chronology, and time-keeping cycles are deeply interdependent and must be studied together. The author argues that examining the cycles in isolation would fail to account for how they were used to construct the overall framework of Chinese historical records.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese cycles, chronology, historical methodology, sexagenary cycle, ancient history</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into Chinese history by questioning the reliability of its early chronology, arguing that authentic history only begins around 800 BCE.</meta>

->CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

The first question that suggests itself is: what is the earliest period at which the reliable local history and chronology of China begins? The second is: how much certainty can be attached to the earlier histories that are accepted as genuine among the Chinese? Are these anything more than local versions of traditions about people and events that were also common in other parts of eastern Asia in ancient times?

An examination of the evidence regarding the first question leads to the conclusion that this period of chronological certainty cannot be placed any further back than 800 years before the Christian era <note>roughly 800 BCE</note>. Regarding the second question, it appears evident that all narratives and chronologies of events in China before that period have no claim to authenticity as history. They lack the kind of proof established by monumental records <note>original: "monumental records"; referring to physical evidence like inscriptions, statues, or ruins</note> found in other nations, such as the Babylonians, Assyrians, and Egyptians. Consequently, all the dates assigned to these early narratives by Chinese historical writers are inadmissible because there is no evidence to prove they consistently align with reality.

It will even be seen in a later section that these Chinese narratives may be reasonably corrected and explained by the records of other nations. This is because other nations provide historical evidence of the same events, or at least, of 

<summary>The author argues that Chinese historical records only become chronologically certain around 800 BCE. Before this date, Chinese accounts lack the archaeological and monumental evidence found in other ancient civilizations and should be treated as tradition rather than verified history.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese chronology, historical authenticity, 800 BCE, monumental records, Babylonians, Assyrians, Egyptians, ancient traditions</keywords>

### THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES. ###

similar events and circumstances having also occurred in other countries.

Regarding the first conclusion mentioned—that the period of reliable Chinese history does not begin more than 800 years BCE—one must primarily rely on the testimony of scholars who have carefully examined this question.

Father Premare <note>original: "Pere Premare"; a Jesuit missionary and noted sinologist</note>, who was already quoted in the previous chapter, provides the following evidence on the subject in his introductory essay to the French translation of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king"; the Classic of History, one of the five foundational texts of Chinese literature</note> on page 4:

> "Sima Guang <note>original: "Sze-ma-kwang"; a high-ranking official and historian of the Song Dynasty</note> begins his great history with the reign of King Weilie <note>original: "Wei-Lieh-wang"</note> in 425 BCE: specifically with the civil wars that lasted until the Prince of Qin <note>original: "Tsin"</note>, after he became master of all China, had himself called Qin Shi Huang <note>original: "She-hwang-ti"</note>, or the first sovereign lord.
> 
> Zhu Xi <note>original: "Chu-he"; a famous Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> also begins his *Comprehensive Mirror in Aid of Governance* <note>original: "Kang-mou"; referring to the *Zizhi Tongjian Gangmu*</note> with King Weilie, just as Sima Guang does; and for a long time, this has been the era most commonly adopted by historians.
> 
> There are others who think that history can go back further—specifically to King Ping <note>original: "Ping Wang"</note>, or 770 BCE, near the time of Romulus <note>the legendary founder of Rome</note>. Some say that it can go back to the years of the Gonghe Regency <note>original: "Kong-Ho"</note>, which would be 841 BCE. Here then, according to the most skilled Chinese critics, is the point to which one can go back without great risk; everything that precedes it is regarded as very uncertain. Even the most lenient Chinese scholars do not ascribe any certainty to dates earlier than 800 years before our era."

## CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES. ##

Klaproth <note>Julius Klaproth (1783–1835), a celebrated German orientalist and linguist</note>, in his *Memoirs Relating to Asia* <note>original: "Memoires relatifs a l'Asie"</note>, Volume 1, page 400, remarks:

> "For the purpose of tracing back to a more distant antiquity, both the learned and the unlearned, up to the present time, have endeavored to take advantage of Chinese history as that of the most ancient people, but without truly knowing what that history is."

And at page 406, after mentioning the historical work of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"; often called the "Grand Historian" of China, who lived during the Han dynasty</note>, he adds:

> "Although he took advantage of all the materials that existed in his own time, the history of China nevertheless remained very incomplete and incoherent prior to the sixth century before Christ. The documents that were consulted were barely consistent with each other, and it is only after one hundred years later that Chinese chronology no longer shows such disparity. Because of this, I place the beginning of 'uncertain history' at the first year of the first cycle, 2637 <term>B.C.</term>; and 'certain history' at 782 <term>B.C.</term>"

At page 389 of the same work, Klaproth explains what he means by "uncertain history":

> "A history in which the facts are true, or at least not improbable; it concerns real people, but lacks a chronology, or at least lacks a chronology founded on proof."

Dr. Legge <note>James Legge (1815–1897), a Scottish missionary and famous translator of Chinese texts</note>, in his translation of the Chinese classics <term>Vol. III</term>, page 89, writes:

> "From the review I have thus taken of the documents purporting to belong to..."

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES #

<margin>x</margin>
...into consideration the persistent assertion of native Chinese historians that their national annals undoubtedly began to be written during the reign of Huangdi <note>original: "Hwang-ti"; also known as the Yellow Emperor, a legendary sovereign and cultural hero</note> in 2704 <term>B.C.</term>, and that these annals have been composed by imperial historians in every reign throughout the entire course of their national existence since that date. Consequently, the question of historical inaccuracy gives way to the question of their chronology.

As has been already stated in a previous chapter, there are no authentic contemporary monuments yet discovered that confirm this chronological accuracy of the Chinese. They insist, nevertheless—without offering any satisfactory proof of the correctness of the statement—that since the time of Huangdi, a regular chronological succession of the chief events that have occurred in China has been distinctly recorded and marked in their annals. They claim this was done using an ancient 60-year cycle, marking each year with its proper cyclical character. Such insistence, however, rests merely on unsupported assertions made by the Chinese that this system of chronicling events by cycle, which they say was invented by Huangdi, has securely traced their history from 2704 <term>B.C.</term> to the present time.

European authors, such as Fréret <note>Nicolas Fréret (1688–1749), a French scholar of ancient history</note> and Léon Carré <note>Léon Carré (1810–1891), a French writer on Oriental history and religion</note>, place great emphasis on the advantage of this <term>cyclical method</term> <note>the sexagenary cycle, a system of 60 terms used for recording days and years in China and other East Asian nations</note> of recording Chinese history as providing a sure test of chronological truth; but these authors only base their...

These assertions, however, by the Chinese and others, should be met with two questions:
1. Whether these successive events were contemporaneously recorded <note>original: "synchronically recorded"</note>, from the supposed era <note>original: "epoch"</note> of <term>Huangdi</term> <note>original: "Hwang-ti"; the legendary "Yellow Emperor"</note> by the above-mentioned method of cyclical years, and what evidence exists that this was actually the case?
2. Whether these occurrences were instead organized into a chronological sequence much later, according to their cycle, through an artificially systematic backward look <note>original: "retrospect"</note> at Chinese history?

<term>De Guignes</term> <note>Joseph de Guignes (1721–1800), a prominent French Orientalist and scholar of Chinese history</note> in his preface to the <term>Shujing</term> <note>original: "Shoo-king"; the Classic of History, one of the foundational texts of Chinese literature</note>, page <term>7</term> <note>original: "vii"</note>, writes on this subject:

> "One must be very gullible <note>original: "credulous"</note> to accept that all those facts were written, or even happened, in such distant times," and at page <term>34</term> <note>original: "xxxiv"</note>: "The dates are only provided through a process of reasoning, or in a speculative <note>original: "conjectural"</note> manner, and not based on positive evidence."

To answer these questions satisfactorily, it would have to be proved as an <term>essential condition</term> <note>original Latin: "sine qua non"</note>—the necessary basis for the truth and certainty of this system of Chinese cyclical chronology—that this 60-year cycle, on which so much emphasis is placed, was not only invented and in existence during the time of <term>Huangdi</term>, but that it was known and used in China at that remote period at which the Chinese assert that <term>Huangdi</term> lived, and that it has been used by them without interruption ever since. It is obvious that unless this be proved, the Chinese argu-
