# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES #

> “...the writer is considered untruthful by the best Chinese critics. The cycle—a rotation of ten characters combined in turn with twelve others—necessarily results in sixty combinations; this is the famous *Jiazi* <note>original: "Kia-tse"; the sexagenary or sixty-year cycle used in Chinese chronology</note> which is so highly praised. I admit that it serves to name the years and the days that have been made to correspond to these sixty names, the order of which is fixed. By using this, some errors can be corrected; however, I must add that it is impossible to determine exactly when the Chinese began to organize their years according to this sequence. In itself, this cycle does not belong to the years any more than it does to the months or the days.”
>
> “Even if it were true that Confucius used it in his *Chunqiu* <note>original: "Tchuen Tsiu"; the *Spring and Autumn Annals*, a core Chinese classic</note>, the antiquity of the practice would not go further back than 722 B.C., because no evidence can be produced to prove that the Chinese followed this custom in the most distant antiquity.” “What basis can there be for all these timelines that Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"; the Han dynasty official considered the father of Chinese historiography</note> chose to arrange according to the *Jiazi* cycle, climbing back like a ladder as far as the Yellow Emperor <note>original: "Hwang-ti"</note>? He might just as easily have ascended as high as Pangu <note>original: "Pwan-kou"; the first living being and creator of the world in Chinese mythology</note>, and his history would have been no more reliable for it.”

Father <note>original: "Pere"</note> Souciet, in his *Mathematical Observations, etc.* <note>original: "Observations Mathematiques"</note>, Paris 1732, Volume 2, page 137, writes:—

> “It was easy, after the fact, and once this cycle was invented, to apply it to the years that preceded it...”

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES #

> “...just as we have applied the era of Jesus Christ to all the ages which preceded Dionysius Exiguus <note>a 6th-century monk who invented the Anno Domini (A.D.) dating system</note>, who invented it.”
>
> “This invention cannot be earlier than the first century of the Christian era, or even later than that, and the origin of its use might still be said to be immemorial <note>originating in the distant past; very old</note>. It would be necessary to know who was the first author who used this cycle, and the time when he lived.”

It seems clear from the testimony of the learned men who have studied the origin and value of Chinese chronology that the cycle of 60 years was <margin>+</margin> not used in China earlier than a century before the Christian era. It might possibly have been known in China before then, as the use of such an epoch <term>epoch: a fixed point in time used as the start of an era</term> for counting years was known and used in central and eastern Asia in most ancient times. It is even probable that the Chinese derived their knowledge of it from some of those nations who, as we know by historical evidence, used it long before there is any evidence of its having been known by the Chinese.

In *The Dawn of History* by C. F. Keary <note>original: "Keany"; Charles Francis Keary (1848–1917) was a British historian and novelist</note>, London, 1878, page 186, there is a curious remark on this subject:

> “The Indians of Virginia kept a record of events in the form of a series of wheels of sixty spokes, each wheel representing the life of a man, sixty years being the average life of a man among those Indians.”

The Babylonians certainly used the period of 60 years, or the *sossos* <note>original: "sossos"; a Babylonian unit of 60 used in their base-60 (sexagesimal) numbering system</note> according to Berosus <note>a Babylonian writer and priest from the 3rd century B.C.</note>; and they...

<summary>This page argues that the Chinese 60-year chronological cycle is not as ancient as claimed, likely appearing around 100 B.C. and perhaps borrowed from other Asian or Babylonian traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese chronology, 60-year cycle, Jiazi, Dionysius Exiguus, C. F. Keary, Berosus, Babylonian sossos, sexagenary cycle</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Chinese sixty-year cycle was likely adopted from earlier West Asian civilizations, specifically the Babylonians and Accadians, rather than being an original Chinese invention.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES #

They, in all probability, had received it from their ancestors or from the earlier inhabitants of Babylonia, the Accadians <note>the Akkadians were an ancient Mesopotamian civilization that preceded the Babylonians</note>.

A. H. Sayce, in his essay on Babylonian literature, page 50, writes:

> “The Accadians made sixty their unit, and in their
> higher calculations reckoned it as an implied large number <note>original: "unexpressed multitude"; referring to how their sexagesimal (base-60) system functioned without a placeholder for zero</note>. 
> Their fractions were on the duodecimal system <note>a base-12 system</note>, with a denominator of 60 always understood.”

Although there is not yet direct proof that the 60-year period had been transmitted to the Babylonians from the Accadians, there is such a resemblance between the Accadian numerical system and the three Babylonian chronological eras—the *Sarus* (3,600 years), the *Nerus* (600 years), and the *Sossos* (60 years)—that it is highly probable these eras originated from the Accadian method of reckoning.

The main thing to note is that the 60-year cycle is not an imaginary or legendary era like the Hindu *Kali Yuga* <term>Kali Yuga: in Hindu cosmology, the fourth and current "dark age" of the world</term> and other similar strange periods, but a real historical term of years actually used in ancient times by the Babylonians and others.

Sir George C. Lewis, in the work cited <note>original: "op : cit :"; Lewis was a 19th-century statesman and scholar who wrote "An Historical Survey of the Astronomy of the Ancients"</note> on page 117, writes:

> “Four great years, of 4, 8, 19, and 60 years respectively, 
> are mentioned by Stobaeus in the *Physical Excerpts*, Book 1, Chapter 8 <note>original: "Stob. Ecl. Phys. I. 8."</note>. The period of 
> 4 years is omitted, and the period of 60 years is 
> stated as 59, in the corresponding passages of Plutarch in *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, Book 2, Chapter 32 <note>original: "Plutarch, Plac, II. 32"</note>; 
> Galen in Chapter 16; and Eusebius in *Preparation for the Gospel*, page 64 <note>original: "Euseb, prœpar Evangel : 64"</note>. 
> So this period was not altogether unknown even 
> in Greece in ancient times.”

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Chinese 60-year cycle likely originated in Western Asia, specifically through cultural transmission from Babylonia and Persia via the ancient city of Balkh.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES #

Whether the Chinese derived their knowledge of this cycle directly from the Babylonians cannot be confirmed, as there is a lack of evidence regarding ancient communication between these two nations.

It is likely that in ancient times, the first settlers in China received many of their methods for calculating time from their neighbors in Balkh <note>Balkh, also known as Bactra, was an ancient city in modern-day Afghanistan and a major center of learning on the Silk Road</note>. Thus, the early Chinese likely derived their knowledge of these methods indirectly from Babylonia and Persia, where the astronomers and chronologists of Balkh had originally obtained them. Sir Henry Rawlinson pointed out the literary influence of Balkh over all of Eastern Asia in an article on "Central Asia" in the *Quarterly Review* (October 1866, No. 240). Because the Chinese claim to have existed as a civilized nation at the same time that Balkh was in its most flourishing days, it stands to reason <note>original: *argumentum ad hominem*, a Latin phrase meaning "an argument to the man"; here it suggests that the conclusion follows from the Chinese people's own claims about their history</note> that they must have had contact with such a center of learning, even if they will not acknowledge it. Furthermore, because they lack a truly scientific understanding of the principles of astronomical chronology, they seem to have forgotten much of what they must have learned there.

Sir Henry Rawlinson writes:

> "Those who have been accustomed to regard Central Asia only in its current state of political and social decline may find it difficult to imagine that it was ever a center of arts and industry, or that it had made any great advances in civilization. Yet, this was undoubtedly the case. It is true that we are not able, as we are in the case of..."

<summary>The author argues that China likely acquired its chronological systems from Babylonia and Persia via the ancient academic hub of Balkh, despite Chinese claims of independent development.</summary>
<keywords>Balkh, Sir Henry Rawlinson, Central Asia, Babylonians, cultural transmission, astronomical chronology, Chinese history</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the excerpt from Sir Henry Rawlinson’s article in the "Quarterly Review" (October 1866), providing historical context for the high state of civilization in Central Asia, which the author argues influenced early Chinese chronological systems.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY #

> Egypt, Babylonia, or Assyria do not possess contemporary monuments that can be used to prove a Central Asiatic development during a period of remote antiquity; however, the evidence for this, gathered from a broad range of logical reasoning, is nonetheless significant and certain. The belief in a very early empire in Central Asia, existing at the same time as the establishment of the Assyrian monarchy, was common among the Greeks long before Alexander the Great’s expedition to the East. This belief could only have been derived from the traditions current at the court of the Achaemenian kings <note>The Achaemenid Empire (c. 550–330 BCE) was the first Persian Empire, founded by Cyrus the Great</note>. . . . The empire began with Sogdiana, Merv, and Bactria; in its later development, it included the modern provinces of Khorasan and Afghanistan. Finally, at its period of greatest expansion, it stretched from Seistan in the south to the Jaxartes River in the north, and from the Indus River in the east until it touched the extreme limit of the Median frontier in the west.”

> “We are primarily concerned with the Eastern Iranians as the founders of Central Asian civilization. Based on the authority of the *Vendidad* <note>The *Vendidad* is a section of the *Avesta*, the sacred texts of Zoroastrianism, focusing on religious laws and mythology</note>, this people may be supposed to have achieved this first stage of development in Sogdiana <note>original: "Sughd"</note>. . . A most important piece of evidence, however, regarding the very high state of power and civilization they attained, is found in the information preserved by the celebrated Abu Rihan <note>Also known as Al-Biruni (973–1048 CE), a brilliant Khwarezmian scholar and polymath who wrote extensively on history, astronomy, and chronology</note>. He was himself a native of that country and the only early Arab writer who investigated the antiquities of the East in a true spirit of historical criticism. This writer gives us the names of the twelve months and of the thirty [days]...

<summary>This page cites Sir Henry Rawlinson and the scholar Al-Biruni to argue that a powerful, highly civilized Iranian empire existed in Central Asia (Sogdiana and Bactria) coeval with the Assyrians. This civilization is presented as the likely source of the advanced chronological knowledge that eventually reached China.</summary>

<keywords>Central Asia, Sogdiana, Bactria, Sir Henry Rawlinson, Abu Rihan, Al-Biruni, Iranian civilization, Achaemenian kings, Vendidad, historical chronology</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the quote from Sir Henry Rawlinson's article, discussing the sophisticated astronomical systems of Central Asia and their potential influence on other ancient civilizations.</meta>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES ###

...days of the months, and of the five extra days <note>original: "Epagominœ" (from Greek *epagomenae*), the intercalary days added to a calendar to align it with the solar year</note>, together with the names of the signs of the zodiac, of the seven planets, and lastly of the mansions of the moon. According to Abu Rihan <note>Al-Biruni (973–1048), a renowned Persian scholar and polymath</note>, the solar calendar of Khwarezm <note>original: "Kharism," an ancient region in Central Asia</note> was the most perfect scheme for measuring time with which he was acquainted. It was maintained by the astronomers of that country that both the solar and lunar zodiacs had originated with them; moreover, the very name by which an astronomer was designated in the language of Khwarezm was taken from the star cluster <note>original: "asterism"</note> of the eighth mansion of the moon. All this information is exceedingly curious in its bearing upon the controversy which has so long raged in the scientific world regarding the superior antiquity of the lunar zodiacs used respectively by the Indians and Chinese. It leads to a suspicion that neither the one nor the other of these systems may have been original, but that their similarity may be explained by their derivation from a common center in Bactria, <margin>In Bactria,</margin> where astronomy was first cultivated by the Eastern Iranians.”

Sir William Drummond <note>A Scottish diplomat and scholar (1771–1828) known for his works on ancient history</note>, in the work cited above (Volume I, page 322), states that:

> “Balkh <note>An ancient city in modern-day Afghanistan, historically a center of Zoroastrianism and learning</note> was for many ages the principal residence of the Magi <note>Zoroastrian priests and practitioners of astrology and science</note>. It was consequently the early and the favorite seat of science; and even in later times, when philosophy had fled from the banks of the Ganges, when Babylonia had fallen, and when Memphis <note>An ancient capital of Egypt</note> was no more, Balkh afforded a last retreat to the arts and sciences of the East <note>original: "Oriental Muses"</note>.”

<summary>This page argues that the advanced astronomical systems of India and China may share a common origin in the ancient Central Asian region of Bactria. It cites the superior solar calendar of Khwarezm and the historical reputation of the city of Balkh as a center of scientific learning for the Magi.</summary>
<keywords>Bactria, Balkh, Khwarezm, Al-Biruni, lunar zodiac, solar calendar, Magi, Sir William Drummond, astronomy, Central Asian civilization</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of the sixty-year cycle, moving from the comparative tables of India, Tibet, and China to a theory regarding their shared roots in the ancient city of Balkh and the Babylonian empire.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

<margin>20</margin>
Albiruni <note>original: Abu Rihan; Al-Biruni (973–1048) was a Persian scholar and polymath</note> (see Sachau’s English translation, London, 1879, page 100), traces the connection between the Chaldeans of Babylonia and the city of Balkh <note>Balkh was a major center of the ancient region of Bactria, located in modern-day Afghanistan</note>. He writes:

> “The Chaldeans are not identical with the Kayanians <note>An ancient dynasty of Iranian tradition and folklore</note>, but were their governors of Babylonia. The original residence of the Kayanians was Balkh, and when they came down to Mesopotamia, people began calling them by the same name which they had formerly applied to their governors: the Chaldeans.”

This remark by Albiruni suggests that, in ancient times, Babylonia was a province of the eastern Iranian empire of Bactria, which had Balkh as its capital. In view of this, it is probable that Babylonian or Chaldean knowledge of astronomy and other sciences was derived from Balkh, a seat of ancient learning. It is likely from this same source that the Chinese also derived similar knowledge. Babylonian and Bactrian culture appear identical, though they originated in northeastern Asia.

As we can trace a probability that the Chinese derived their knowledge from Bactria, we should also keep in mind that a similar 60-year period has been used in India since ancient times. It is quite probable that the Hindus may have obtained their knowledge of it from the Babylonians. Indeed, their own period of the *Kali Yuga* <note>original: Kali youga; the "Age of Kali," the final of the four stages the world passes through in Hindu scripture</note>, consisting of 432,000 years, is precisely the same as the Babylonian period of the ten ancient Chaldean kings, whose reigns lasted 432,000 years according to Syncellus <note>original: Syncellus; George Syncellus, a 9th-century Byzantine chronicler who preserved fragments of ancient Babylonian history</note>.

<summary>The author argues that Babylonian, Chinese, and Indian astronomical systems—including the 60-year cycle—likely share a common origin in the ancient learning center of Balkh (Bactria). This theory is supported by matching chronological periods, such as the 432,000-year cycle found in both Hindu and Babylonian records.</summary>

<keywords>Albiruni, Chaldeans, Balkh, Bactria, Kayanians, Babylonia, Chinese chronology, 60-year cycle, Kali Yuga, Syncellus, astronomy history</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that ancient chronological cycles, specifically the 432,000-year period and the 60-year cycle, were transmitted between Babylon, India, and China, suggesting a shared astronomical heritage.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

As the coincidence of two nations sharing such a tradition in their legendary history is unlikely to be accidental, and because Babylonian history extends much further into antiquity than that of the Hindus, it is probable that the Hindus derived the tradition of this long period—the *Kali Yuga* <note>original: "Kali youga"; in Hindu cosmology, the "Age of Vice," the last of the four stages the world passes through</note>—from the Babylonians. Consequently, it is likely that they also obtained the 60-year period, which the Indians call the "cycle of Jupiter," from the same source.

In connection with this, it has already been observed in a previous chapter that the Chinese also have a period of 432,000 years associated with their early sovereigns. It is more probable that they borrowed this from the Indians than from the Babylonians; thus, it is even more likely that the Chinese learned of the 60-year period from the Indians as well.

Father Premare <note>original: "Pere Premare"; Joseph Henri Marie de Prémare (1666–1736), a French Jesuit missionary and sinologist</note>, in the previously cited *Preliminary Discourse* <note>original: "discours preliminaire"</note> (page LXVI), explains the existence and origin of this 432,000-year period in China:

> "The dynasty of the *Tien-huang* <note>original: "Tien hwang"; the Celestial Kings or 'Heavenly August Ones' of Chinese mythology</note> had thirteen kings of the same name, and for this reason they are called brothers. To each one of them has been attributed 18,000 years of life or reign."

The total of these thirteen reigns is 234,000 years. On the same and following page, Premare, writing of the *Ti-huang* <note>original: "Ti-hwang"; the Terrestrial Kings or 'Earthly August Ones'</note>, or the sovereigns of earth, says:

> "There are eleven kings of the same name. Each one of these eleven kings has either reigned or lived 18,000 years, which makes a total of 198,000 years."

<note>The sum of these two periods, 234,000 and 198,000, equals the Babylonian and Indian cycle of 432,000 years.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the 432,000-year chronological cycle found in Chinese mythology was likely adopted from Indian or Babylonian sources. It details how the reigns of the legendary Heavenly and Terrestrial Kings in Chinese tradition sum exactly to this significant ancient number.</summary>

<keywords>Kali Yuga, cycle of Jupiter, Tien-huang, Ti-huang, Father Premare, Babylonian chronology, Hindu astronomy, 432000 year cycle</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 432,000-year chronological cycle, comparing how this specific legendary timeframe appears in the records of the Babylonians, Hindus, and Chinese.</meta>

->THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES. 141<-

The total of these two lines of twenty-four sovereigns makes the 432,000 years mentioned above.

There are thus three nations, all claiming great antiquity, and each having a curiously similar tradition of a similar imaginary period of time during which their ancestors existed. As far as the Chinese are concerned, the books in which the description of this period is found are generally supposed to be no older than a few centuries before the common era <note>original: "Christian era"</note>, unless the opinion regarding the antiquity of the *Shan Hai Jing* <note>original: "San hai-king"; the 'Classic of Mountains and Seas,' an ancient compilation of mythic geography and folklore</note> is accepted as correct. It is mentioned as follows by Prémare in his *Preliminary Discourse* <note>original: "Discours preliminaire"</note> (page 74):

> "The *Shan Hai Jing* is a book so ancient that some persons attribute it to the Emperor Yu, and others to Boyi <note>original: "Pe-ye"; likely referring to Boyi, a legendary official who served Emperor Yu</note>, who lived at the same time."

If this is so, the probability is not strong that the Chinese derived this legendary period directly from the Babylonians. Instead, the greater probability is that they learned about it from the Hindus, as they are known to have received knowledge from them regarding many other matters of chronology and astronomy near that time. Possibly there may have been also Babylonian or Bactrian <note>Bactria was an ancient region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern-day Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note> oral traditions on these subjects floating about among the Chinese people. These were gathered up in Taoist works and in books like the *Shan Hai Jing*, just as the Confucian historians allege that they learned many things—besides the contents of the *Shujing* <note>original: "Shoo-king"; the 'Classic of History' or 'Book of Documents'</note>, the *Zuo Zhuan* <note>original: "Tso-chuen"; an influential ancient Chinese narrative history</note>, and the *Bamboo Annals*—from ancient popular traditions of

<summary>This page explores the shared 432,000-year legendary cycle among Babylonians, Hindus, and Chinese, suggesting the Chinese likely acquired this chronological framework through Indian influence or Central Asian oral traditions.</summary>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

<margin>China, which had been orally preserved by the nation.</margin>

It is not, however, necessary to look back to the Babylonian use of the *sossos* <term>sossos: an ancient Babylonian unit of time representing 60 years</term>, or cycle of 60 years, to find the origin of a similar era in China; for it has been suggested by Dr. Chalmers <note>John Chalmers (1825–1899), a Scottish missionary and noted sinologist</note> in his dissertation quoted above (pages 97-98) that the Chinese may have derived their 60-year cycle directly from the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note>. He writes:

> <margin>X</margin> “The first attempt to arrange the years in cycles of
> “sixty is found in Sima Qian’s <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts‘ien"; Sima Qian (c. 145–86 BC) was the official Grand Historian of the Han dynasty</note> historical records, in
> “a table constructed for the purpose of intercalation <term>intercalation: the insertion of extra days or months into a calendar to keep it aligned with the solar year or seasons</term>,
> “and extending over a period of 76 years, the first
> “year being 103 BCE. But instead of using the Chinese
> “cyclical characters, he employs words of two and three
> “syllables which, considered from a Chinese point of
> “view, must be pronounced barbarous. We give the
> <margin>Names</margin> “names applied to the first 13 years. Perhaps some
> “one acquainted with the ancient language of the Hindus
> “may hereafter be able to identify them. The
> <margin>Jupiter</margin> “second word in each name has some connection with
> <margin>Sheh-te</margin> “the planet Jupiter; and Sima says that *Sheh-te*
> “(part of the first name) means Jupiter.
> “His commentator adds that Jupiter belongs to the
> “east and is the essence of wood—the spirit of the green
> “God, Ling-wei-jang.
> “This last word is one of six meaningless three-syllable names
> “applied to the God of the North Pole and the five
> “elementary Gods during the *Han* dynasty, for which
> “also we must seek a foreign origin.”

Dr. Chalmers continues: “Various attempts have been made to analyze the second word *Sheh-te-kih* (in Cantonese, *Ship-tai-hak*). Is *Ship-tai* intended to represent the Hindu <note>original: "Hin-doo"</note> name of Jupiter, *Brihaspati* <note>original: "Vrishaspati (or Brhaspati)"</note>? And is *hak* (the Hindu word *Chakra* <note>Chakra: a Sanskrit word for "wheel" or "cycle"</note>) the Hindu character or cycle applied to the first year of Sima Qian’s table to determine which of the twelve branches it ought to be identified with? Sima himself, besides saying that *Sheh-te* is Jupiter, explains the term to mean the position of that planet in the ecliptic <term>ecliptic: the apparent path of the Sun across the celestial sphere as seen from Earth</term>.”

Note: *Schid* in the Avestan <note>original: "Zend"; Zend-Avesta refers to the sacred texts of Zoroastrianism</note> language stands for Jupiter.

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

> "In a work called the *Classic of Stars* <note>original: "Classic of stars"; likely referring to the *Xing Jing*, an ancient Chinese star catalogue</note>, *Sheti* <note>original: "Sheh-te"</note> is said to denote a 'spiritual instrument of Western nations.' *Brihaspati* <note>original: "Brhaspati"</note> originally means 'Lord of Worship' (see page 279 of Burgess's translation of the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>The *Surya Siddhanta* is a foundational Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy</note>). It also refers to the planet *Jupiter* (pages 279–275)."

> "Now, this 'confusion of words without knowledge' is easily explained if we assume that the 60-year cycle was introduced from the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note>. In the time of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"</note>, the Chinese were indebted to the Hindus for other even more important things."

Dr. Chalmers’ suggestion mentioned above is very helpful to this investigation. The Hindus had a 60-year cycle called the "Cycle of Jupiter." They assigned a specific name to each year of that cycle, just as Sima Qian gave special names to each year in his own cycle—which, according to Chalmers, he linked to calculations involving the planet Jupiter. It is therefore evident that Sima Qian's cycle was probably a transcription of the Hindu Cycle of Jupiter.

Referring to this cycle, Mr. Cowasjee Patell, in his work on chronology (page 44), remarks:

> "The *Brihaspati Chakra* <note>original: "Vrihaspati Chakra"; the Sanskrit name for the 60-year cycle of the planet Jupiter</note>, or Cycle of Jupiter, is regarded as one of the most ancient chronological systems in all of Asia. The origin of the Cycle of Jupiter is unknown."

Warren, in *Kala Sankalita* <note>original: "San Kalita"; referring to John Warren’s 1825 work *Kala Sankalita*, a collection of memoirs on Indian timekeeping</note>, page 199, writes:

> "I have not been able to discover the origin of the practice of reckoning time with reference to the revolution—"

-> # THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES. <-

<margin>
<unclear>How</unclear>

> “[revolu]tions of the planet Jupiter, but it is no doubt very ancient.”

Possibly it may have come to the Hindus from the Babylonians, together with other astronomico-chronological or astrological periods and astronomical knowledge that they derived from that source, which have been pointed out by Guerin (*Treatise on Indian Astronomy* <note>original: "Astronomie Indienne"</note>. Paris 1847, page 183.) Possibly the *sossos* <note>A Babylonian numerical unit or cycle representing the number 60</note> or 60-year cycle of the Babylonians might have been a cycle of Jupiter; in any case, the coincidence merits consideration.

Bailly, in his *Treatise on Indian and Oriental Astronomy* <note>original: "Traité de l’astronomie Indienne et Orientale"</note>, page 276, says that:

> “Just as the period of 12 years is related to the movement of Jupiter and his return to the same aspect from the earth, so this period of 60 years is that which brings about the same configurations of Jupiter and Saturn with the earth. It is the phenomenon, often seen, of these two planets being found in near conjunction <note>A conjunction occurs when two celestial bodies appear close to each other in the sky</note> with each other, and in opposition to the sun, that doubtless has sufficed to fix the period of 60 years.”

At page 331 of his *History of Ancient Astronomy* <note>original: "Histoire de l’astronomie ancienne"</note> he explains this further:

> “Jupiter, seen from the earth, returns to the same point in the zodiac at the end of 12 years and five days, and he returns for the fifth time in 60 years and 25 days. Mars finds itself equally in the same position with regard to the earth after 15 years less 18 days, and also after 60 years less 72 days. Saturn only returns to the same degree of the ecliptic <note>The circular path on the celestial sphere that the sun appears to follow over the course of a year</note> at the end...”

<page-num>146</page-num>

<header>CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.</header>

> “[The orbit of Saturn is] about 29 years, and it is evident from the slowness of its movements that at the end of 60 years it is not far off from the same point. The period of 60 years seems to be that of the conjunction <note>A conjunction occurs when planets appear close together in the sky from the perspective of Earth</note> of the three mentioned superior planets in the same sign of the zodiac, and even within a narrower space of it. The ancients, especially those in the East <note>original: "orientals"</note>, paid great attention to the conjunction of the planets with each other; and when these planets nearly met each other, which they did about every 60 years, they retained the memory of the event.”

This would give a very different story for the origin of the 60-year cycle compared to the legend of <term>Hwang-ti</term> <note>Huangdi, or the Yellow Emperor, a mythological ancestor and cultural hero in Chinese history</note> having instructed <term>Tanao</term> <note>Da Rao, a legendary minister said to have created the sexagenary (60-year) cycle system</note> to observe the elements, and having then invented this period.

The Chinese have no positive records of a 60-year cycle of <term>Jupiter</term> being known by them in ancient times. Therefore, there is nothing to support the notion that the <term>Hindus</term> derived it from the Chinese.

This cycle of <term>Jupiter</term> forms part of the <term>Hindu</term> astronomical system. It is specifically mentioned in the “<term>Sûrya Siddhanta</term>” <note>An ancient and influential Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy</note>, translated by <term>Burgess</term>, chapter 14, verse 12.

> “In chapter 12, verse 8 of this book, <term>Maya</term> <note>A mythical figure often credited with receiving astronomical knowledge from the Sun God</note> makes inquiries as to ‘how many modes of measuring time (<term>mâna</term>) are there?’ and in the first chapter mentioned [Chapter 14], it is stated:
>
> 1. The modes of measuring time (<term>mâna</term>) are nine: namely those of <term>Brahma</term> <note>The creator god in Hinduism</note>, of the Gods, of the fathers <note>The Pitris, or ancestral spirits</note>, of <term>Prajâpati</term> <note>A lord of creation</note>, of <term>Jupiter</term>, and solar (<term>saura</term>), civil, [and lunar...]”

"...(savana) lunar and <term>sidereal time</term> <note>Time measured by the movement of the stars rather than the sun</note>." The verbal connection between the <term>Hindu</term> measurement of time called <term>Saura</term> <note>A solar year in the Hindu calendar</note> (one <term>Chacra</term> year of 360 days; see Warren, *opus citatum* <note>original: "op : cit :"; meaning "the work cited"</note>, page 202) and the <term>Chaldean</term> <note>An ancient civilization in Mesopotamia known for its early advancements in astronomy</note> period for the same purpose, called <term>Saros</term>, presents a coincidence that deserves attention.

<margin>“Of four modes, namely solar, lunar, sidereal, and civil time, practical use is made among men; by that of Jupiter is to be determined the year of the cycle of sixty years: of the rest, no use is ever made.”</margin>

In chapter I, verse 55, it states: “Multiply by twelve the past revolutions of Jupiter, add the signs of the current revolution, and divide by sixty; the remainder marks the year of Jupiter's cycle counting from <term>Vijaasa</term> <note>Likely referring to Vijaya, the traditional first year of the 60-year Jovian cycle</note>.” And in a note to this passage, it is stated:

<margin>“This is the rule for finding the current year of the cycle of sixty years, which is in use throughout all India, and which is called the cycle of Jupiter, because the length of its years is measured by the passage of the planet by its mean motion through one of the signs of the zodiac.”</margin>

“It has not been satisfactorily determined, as far as we are aware, where the cycle originated, what its age is, or why it was made to consist of sixty years, including five whole revolutions of the planet. There was, indeed, also in use a cycle of 12 Jupiter-years, which is the time of one <term>sidereal revolution</term> <note>The time it takes for a planet to return to the same position relative to the stars</note> (see below XIV, 17).”

Besides this 60-year cycle, the Hindus seem to have also had other periods of time composed of *sixty* elements. In Professor Whitney’s notes to Colebrooke’s essay on the *Vedas*, printed in Volume II, page 125 of *The Life and Essays of H. T. Colebrooke*, it is stated when describing one of the <term>Jyotisha</term> <note>Ancient Hindu treatises on astronomy and astrology</note> treatises:

<margin>
1830
30
✓
</margin>

> “Its special subject is the <term>Yuga</term> <note>An age or era in Hindu timekeeping; here referring to a specific five-year period</note>, or *lustrum*, which is designed to begin at the winter solstice and to consist of 1,830 days, or of sixty solar months. This is equivalent to *five years of twelve months, with an extra month—all of thirty days each—and some of the oldest Vedic references seem to imply such a system of reckoning.*”

> “The *Jyotisha* does not teach any division of the hour (<term>muhurta</term> <note>A traditional Hindu unit of time, approximately 48 minutes</note>) into *sixtieths*, but only into half-hours, or *sixtieths of a day* (<term>nadika</term> <note>A unit of time equivalent to 24 minutes</note>).”

> “Bailly also observes in the work cited <note>original: "op : cit :"; Latin: *opus citatum*</note>, page 70: ‘The Roman <term>lustrum</term> <note>A period of five years</note> of five years would be a period of 60 months: an intermediate period between the period of 60 days and 60 years.’”

Colebrooke himself, writing on this *Jyotisha* in the same volume, page 97, states:

> “This ancient Hindu calendar is evidently the foundation of the one that, after successive corrections, is now used by the Hindus throughout India. > "...times—<term>Mrigasiras</term> <note>One of the 28 lunar mansions or nakshatras in Hindu astrology, corresponding to part of the constellation Orion</note> is the fifth Mansion, and thus becomes the ruler of the first <term>Ghurri</term> <note>An Indian unit of time equivalent to 24 minutes; there are 60 ghurris in a 24-hour day</note> of Monday."

How the Chinese used a period of 60 for numbering days or hours will be seen later on.

Bailly, in his *Treatise on Indian and Oriental Astronomy* <note>original: "Traite de l'astronomie Indienne et Orientale"</note>, page 86, says:

<margin>x</margin>
<margin>x</margin>
> "The Indian interval of two months, called the <term>Rondou</term> <note>A period or "year" of 60 days used in certain ancient calendars</note>, is the period of 60 days known in China. This revolution, or 'year' of two months, was known and employed in Egypt."

It is therefore improbable that the cycle of 60 days was a Chinese invention. Meanwhile, it may be looked upon as certain that the cycle of 60 years had an historically proved existence in Babylonia and India long before any similar existence of this period can be shown by historical evidence to have existed in China. Although the maxim "after this, therefore because of this" <note>original: "post hoc, propter hoc"; a logical fallacy suggesting that because one event follows another, the first must have caused the second</note> is not absolutely conclusive, it at least shows that the Chinese cannot claim any priority for the use of this chronological cycle over other nations who undoubtedly possessed and anciently used this system of 60 years as a measurement of time.

<margin>x</margin>
It should, however, be remembered here that the Hindu names of the years in the 60-year cycle are all different from each other; whereas those used in the table of <term>Sima Qian</term> <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"; the Han dynasty official often called the "Father of Chinese Historiography"</note> are repeated as regards half of each name after every ten years, and after every twelve years for the other half of the name. This is done in the same way as the ordinary modern Chinese cycle of 60 is formed by the combination of the ten <term>Kan</term> <note>The "Heavenly Stems"</note> and the twelve <term>Chi</term> <note>The "Earthly Branches"</note>.

<page-num>153</page-num>

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES

This is even more remarkable because it reveals a set of foreign names that correspond to those in the "stems" and "branches" of 10 and 12 <note>The "Ten Heavenly Stems" and "Twelve Earthly Branches" are the foundation of the Chinese sexagesimal cycle used for naming years, days, and hours.</note>. The Chinese claim these are the origins of their 60-year cycle. Consequently, the modern theory for building the 60-year cycle numerically matches the *structure* of Sima Qian's <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"; Sima Qian (c. 145–86 BC) was a Han dynasty historian known as the "Father of Chinese Historiography."</note> arrangement, since both combine two series of names to create a unique name for each year. However, it differs significantly regarding the specific ten and twelve *names* Sima Qian actually used for this purpose.

At the same time, while Sima Qian's system does not exactly match Indian <note>original: "Hindoo"</note> names, it is almost certainly of foreign origin. Because a direct Indian origin is only slightly likely, this suggests that Sima Qian's names might come from an even older source—perhaps the Babylonian <term>Sossos</term> <note>A Babylonian unit of time or quantity representing 60.</note>, a 60-year period. We should, however, keep in mind what Dr. Legge states (on page 13, above <note>original: "supra"</note>): namely, that it is "very questionable whether these foreign names for the years were used in ancient China."

Before we continue investigating where the Chinese or Indians got this <term>sexagesimal</term> <note>A system based on the number 60.</note> period of years, it would be useful to first look at how the Chinese learned about the 76-year cycle. Sima Qian mentions this period and seemingly links it to the 60-year cycle.

<page-num>154</page-num>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES

It is clear to see that this 76-year period is essentially the same as the one known as the "Callippic period." <note>The Callippic cycle was a 76-year period used to synchronize the solar year and the lunar month.</note> Callippus, for whom it is named, lived in the 4th century <term>B.C.</term> and was a contemporary and associate of Aristotle. This Callippic period, which began at the summer solstice of its first year, was not an original, simple invention based on an immediate observation of the relationship between our timekeeping and the movements of the heavenly bodies. Instead, it was the result of a previously known 19-year cycle. This shorter cycle is said to have been invented by Meton in the 5th century <term>B.C.</term> <note>The Metonic cycle (19 years) is a period after which the phases of the moon recur on the same days of the year.</note> However, the Metonic cycle was eventually found to be inadequate because it did not contain an exact, whole number of solar revolutions when compared to a full series of lunar phases <note>original: "lunar epochs"</note>. To make it more accurate, Callippus quadrupled the 19-year period to 76 years. This adjustment was found to reduce the difference between lunar and solar years, making them align more closely at the end of the period.

<margin>X</margin> In William Smith’s *Dictionary of Greek and Roman Biography and Mythology*, under the entry for **METON**, it is correctly noted: "Whether Meton was himself the inventor of this remarkable period, or whether he found it elsewhere, cannot now be known." Therefore, the fact that the cycle is named after Meton should not prevent us from searching for its origins in even more ancient times.

In fact, the 19-year cycle was known to Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome (reigned c. 715–673 BC), who is traditionally credited with the reform of the Roman calendar.</note> two centuries before it was used...

</margin>
...this cycle was attributed to Meton, according to the testimony of Livy (Book 1, chapter 19). Whatever objections may be raised against Livy's authority regarding the personal connection of Numa Pompilius with this period (see Dyer’s *History of the Kings of Rome* <note>original: "Dyers history of the early kings of Rome," a 19th-century work by Thomas Henry Dyer</note>), the fact remains that in Livy’s time, the Roman people believed the 19-year cycle had existed since the earliest days of their monarchy. Even if no certain, fixed date can be properly assigned to its first invention or the start of its use among the Romans, it is still very probable that it existed before the early chronological period associated with Numa’s connection to Rome.

<margin>X</margin>
Livy’s attribution of this 19-year cycle to Numa Pompilius <note>Numa Pompilius was the legendary second king of Rome, credited with many religious and political institutions</note> includes a detail that makes his testimony particularly interesting for our current investigation. Livy states that Numa used the 19-year cycle to determine the order of intercalary months <term>intercalary months</term> <note>Extra months inserted into a calendar to make the solar and lunar years align</note>. Seven of these months must be distributed over 19 consecutive years to synchronize the lunar and solar years and make them align at the end of that period.

What is especially interesting in the present inquiry is that Numa's use of the 19-year cycle for placing the intercalary moon is identical in purpose to how the Chinese use the same term of years. This similarity of usage—if it could be proved to have existed in China anywhere near the time of Numa—might provide evidence that the Chinese had derived their knowledge of this period...

...from the same source where Numa obtained it; unless it is argued that either the Chinese learned it from Numa, or that Numa learned it from the Chinese. Neither of these theories has any historical foundation. On the other hand, there is strong historical evidence to prove that Roman knowledge of astronomy and its related sciences was derived from the Babylonians. This makes it highly probable, if not certain, that the Roman knowledge of the 19-year period and its practical use was also obtained from the same Babylonian source from which they acquired their ideas about the planets and astronomy. The fact that the Babylonians used a year of 360 days with five extra days, combined with the precision of their observations, shows that they must have known the 19-year period as the way to adjust their calculations, which are thought to be as ancient as 2233 <term>B.C.</term>

Regarding the time when the Metonic cycle <note>The 19-year cycle used to synchronize lunar months with the solar year, named after the Greek astronomer Meton of Athens</note> was first known in China, the following excerpts from scholars of Chinese history may shed some light on the introduction of that cycle, as well as the 76-year cycle mentioned by Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-tseen," the "Father of Chinese Historiography" (c. 145–86 BC)</note>.

> “A commentator from the period <term>A.D.</term>

> ‘In 19 years there would be seven intercalary moons <term>intercalary moons</term> <note>Extra months added to a calendar year to keep it synchronized with the seasons</note>, 
> which would make the solar and lunar years exactly
> even and constitute a complete cycle.’ And he adds, 
> ‘The ancient year has 12 months, each consisting of 30...”

> "...in which the moon made 254 revolutions. The difference between 254 and 235 is 19. Divide 254 by 19, said Li-fang, and the quotient is 13 plus 7/19ths. From this, he concluded that the moon's specific daily movement is 13 Chinese degrees and 7/19ths. Li-fang, therefore, seeing that the 19-year cycle was imperfect, invented one of 76 years, composed of four 19-year cycles. He expressed this new period with the character *Pou* <note>original: Pou; a period of 76 years used to synchronize the lunar and solar calendars</note>."

The foundation, therefore, of Sima Qian's <note>original: Sze-ma-ts'ien; a foundational Chinese historian (c. 145–86 BC) known as the "Grand Historian"</note> calculation regarding the 60-year cycle, and the 76-year period it is linked to, must have been very close to his own time; the same applies to the 19-year cycle. There is no evidence showing where the Chinese obtained their knowledge of these periods. Sima Qian assumes they date back to the time of Yao <note>original: Yao; a legendary Chinese ruler, one of the Three Sovereigns and Five Emperors, traditionally dated to c. 2300 BC</note>, just as the Chinese attribute other astronomical data to him. However, there is as little evidence for this as for any other origin. Consequently, the only way to resolve this search is to attribute this knowledge to the same Hindu source to which the 60-year cycle is traced—unless some vague tradition, barely understood by the Chinese, had remained with them from other ancient celestial knowledge they possibly gained from the eastern Iranians of Bactria <note>original: Bactria; an ancient region in Central Asia, encompassing parts of modern-day Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note>. At any rate, there is no evidence that the Chinese knew of these periods before the 3rd century <term>B.C.</term> Souciet <note>original: Souciet; Étienne Souciet (1685–1744), a French Jesuit scholar who analyzed Chinese astronomical records</note> (Volume II, page 29) has a remarkable passage on the fabricated Chinese calendars, which

These are the sources of the mythical chronology that is now being examined:—
<margin>X</margin>

> "It was during the period of the Western Han dynasty that the six calendars were published, which were initially presented as being ancient. The first was the Calendar of Huangdi <note>original: Hwangti; the legendary Yellow Emperor</note>; the second, that of Zhuanxu <note>original: Tchuen Hiu</note>; the third, that of Yu, the founder of the Xia <note>original: Hia</note> dynasty; the fourth, that of Cheng Tang <note>original: Tching Tang</note>, the founder of the Shang dynasty; the fifth, that of King Wu <note>original: Vou-vang</note>, the founder of the Zhou <note>original: Chow</note> dynasty; and the sixth, that of the Duke of Zhou <note>original: Tcheou-kong</note>, the Prince of Lu. It was not long before suspicions arose regarding the authenticity of these calendars, which were otherwise poorly constructed; they are now generally considered to have been the work of Han dynasty scholars,"

and at page 31, after stating that the primary use of astronomy was for astrological purposes, and that the mysterious numbers of the Eight Trigrams <note>original: 8 Kwa; the Ba Gua, symbols used in Chinese cosmology to represent the fundamental principles of reality</note> of Fuxi <note>original: Fu-hi</note> were the basis of their calculations, he adds:—
<margin>1.2 / 14.3 / 24.3 / 12.3 / 14 / 24 / 3 / 4 / 5+</margin>

This passage sheds some light on why Sima Qian <note>original: Sze-ma-tsien</note> combined the 76-year period—the quadruple of the 19-year cycle—with the 60-year cycle. Dr. Chalmers, in the work cited above <note>original: loc: cit: (loco citato); in the place cited</note>, writes:—

> "...there is scarcely a shadow of doubt that this was the case. In no other way can we account for the sudden appearance in Sima Qian’s <note>original: Tseen’s; referring to the Grand Historian of the Han dynasty</note> history of a method so far in advance of anything known before in China."

Therefore, the most that can be deduced from the striking similarities between the Romans and the Chinese in matters of astronomy and related subjects is that both the Chinese and the Romans likely derived their knowledge of the 19-year period <note>The Metonic cycle, a period of 19 years after which the moon's phases recur on the same days of the solar year</note> directly from the Babylonians. However, as will be shown shortly, the fact that the Chinese only recently acquired this knowledge—at least when compared to the ancient astronomical records of Babylon—makes it more likely that the Chinese learned it from Bactria <note>An ancient region in Central Asia, modern-day Afghanistan and Tajikistan, which served as a cultural crossroads</note>. The cycle had undoubtedly been made known there through Assyrian and Persian traditions, or perhaps through the Greeks, who had themselves gained that knowledge from the Chaldeans <note>A people of Mesopotamia famous in antiquity for their advanced astronomical and astrological knowledge</note> of Babylonia.

Later, evidence may be presented concerning the actual use of the 19-year cycle in Babylonia during a period far more ancient than its known introduction into China.

In the meantime, the observable similarities between Roman and Babylonian astronomical science may further clarify where the Romans got their knowledge on the subject. Since there is also a great similarity between Roman and Chinese practices in related matters, the mutual coincidence of both Roman and Chinese specialized science with Babylonian knowledge...

### THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

...can only be reasonably accounted for by attributing the original source of knowledge for both nations on this point to Babylonia, as its remote common origin.

The Marquis Fortia d’Urban <note>Agricol-Joseph-François, Marquis de Fortia d'Urban (1761–1843), a French historian</note>, in Volume II of his *Antediluvian History of China* <note>original: *Histoire antidiluvienne de la Chine*</note>, devotes a whole chapter to comparing the similarity between the Roman and Chinese calendars and other matters connected with them; and it is principally on his observations that the present remarks on this subject are based.

The first thing he brings to notice is that the winter solstice of the Romans was indicated at the 8th of the Calends <note>The first day of the month in the ancient Roman calendar. The "8th of the Calends of January" corresponds to December 25th</note> of January, and that this winter solstice was the starting point of the year with the Romans, just as it was in primitive times with the Chinese. This setting of time is attributed to the Chaldeans <note>An ancient people of Mesopotamia renowned for their astronomical observations</note>: "The winter solstice is observed just as the Chaldeans do" <note>original Latin: *Brumale solstitium sicut Chaldæi observant*</note>.

<margin>x</margin>
The opinion that this Roman custom of beginning the year had a Chaldean origin is strengthened by the fact that the Romans took their twelve superior divinities—which they identified with the 12 signs of the zodiac—from the Chaldean system of 12 superior deities, each presiding over a month of the year and over a sign of the stellar zodiac. The Latin poet Manilius <note>Marcus Manilius, a Roman poet who wrote a famous poem on astronomy called *Astronomica* in the 1st century AD</note>, in book 1, verse 24, narrating the origin of Roman astronomy, places it among the "peoples under the very East, whom the Euphrates divides" <note>original Latin: *gentes oriente sub ipso quas secat Euphrates*</note>. Father Pingré <note>Alexandre Guy Pingré (1711–1796), a French astronomer and priest</note> points out in a note that this line refers to Babylonia; and in book 2, verse 426 and following, he gives a list of the corresponding Roman divinities who preside over the months and...

<margin>The Romans adopted the Chaldean system only after switching from a ten-month to a twelve-month year.</margin>Indeed, as the Romans originally had only ten months in the year, they could only have adopted the Chaldean system when the twelve-month year was introduced.

They might have acquired it from the Etruscans <note>An advanced ancient civilization in Italy that preceded and heavily influenced Rome</note>, who also recognized these twelve superior deities. The Asiatic origin of the Etruscans—as discussed in Robert Ellis’s *Asiatic Affinities of the Old Italians*—whether it be Armenian or Lydian <note>From Lydia, an ancient kingdom in western Asia Minor (modern Turkey)</note>, makes it likely that they inherited this through their Asiatic ancestors from the Chaldeans. <margin>It is likely the Etruscans brought this knowledge from Asia, as scholars agree Babylonia was the cradle of astronomy.</margin> It seems to be an accepted truth among modern experts on this subject that Chaldean Babylonia was the original source of all astronomical science. François Lenormant <note>A French Assyriologist and archaeologist (1837–1883)</note> considers this established by historical evidence. Archibald Sayce <note>A pioneering English Assyriologist and linguist (1845–1933)</note>, in his *History of Babylonia* on page 32, states something even more interesting for the general discussion of Chinese chronology: "Babylonian literature included chronological tables." These may one day be fully deciphered and reveal <note>original: "evince"</note> the system used there for computing and synchronizing <note>original: "according"</note> the times and seasons.

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

The Chaldean <note>An ancient people of Mesopotamia renowned for their early and influential astronomical observations</note> origin—whether direct or indirect—of the astronomical system used by the Romans seems, therefore, certain. The next similarity between the Roman and Chinese chronological systems is found in the division of the year, which was the primary goal of all ancient astronomical science.

<margin>The Chinese and Romans both divided the year into twenty-four sections.</margin>
As is well known, the Chinese divide the year into twenty-four solar terms <note>original: *Tsie-ki*</note>, which are supposed to last fifteen days each; in practice, the Romans followed a similar system.

It cannot be said, however, that each solar term is exactly fifteen days long, for that would result in a year of only 360 days. The difference between that and a 365-day year must have been either distributed among the twenty-four terms or added at the end of the year as <term>epagomonal</term> days <note>extra days added to a calendar to bring it into alignment with the solar year</note>.

The Chaldeans also divided the year into twenty-four parts, and this same division existed among the Persians. In *Babylonian Literature* (page 54), Archibald Sayce <note>Archibald Sayce (1845–1933), a pioneering British Assyriologist</note> writes:
> "The month was divided into two halves of fifteen days each, which were further subdivided into three periods of five days."

<margin>Weather markers in the Chinese calendar.</margin>
In the Chinese system, each solar term <note>original: *Tsie-ki*</note> is marked with characters indicating weather conditions <note>original: meteorological phenomena</note> and the start of seasons and half-seasons. Similar markers are found in the calendars of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, a 2nd-century Greco-Roman mathematician and astronomer</note> and the Romans. However, these Western calendars were more precise than the Chinese ones, as they tracked cold, heat, rain, various winds, and storms by the stars. The rising and setting of specific constellations were synchronized <note>original: synchronic</note> each year with these natural events <note>original: phænomena</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the similarities between Chinese, Roman, and Chaldean calendar systems, specifically their shared division of the year into twenty-four parts and the use of astronomical markers to predict seasonal weather.</summary>

<keywords>Chaldean astronomy, Roman calendar, Chinese solar terms, Tsie-ki, epagomonal days, Archibald Sayce, Ptolemy, meteorology, constellations</keywords>

Father Souciet <note>original: *Pere Souciet*; Étienne Souciet (1671–1744) was a Jesuit scholar who published works on Chinese astronomy</note>, in his *Astronomical Observations* <note>original: *observations astronomiques*</note>, Volume 3, page 70, confirms this: “The first period of the solar terms begins at the winter solstice.”

<margin>x</margin>
By comparing the Roman and Chinese calendars, it is clear that the seasonal divisions in both systems correspond to the same days. This can be seen by comparing the two calendars every 45 days throughout the year—measuring from the midpoint of one season to the next—starting from the winter solstice.

Forty-seven days after the winter solstice, on the 5th of February <note>original: *5th nones of February*; in the Roman calendar, the Nones fell on the 5th or 7th day of the month</note>, the Roman calendar marks the beginning of spring. Forty-four days after that, it marks the vernal equinox.

This matches the Chinese calendar’s designation for the division of spring; it defines one season as consisting of six solar terms <note>original: *Tsie-ki*</note>, or roughly 90 to 91 days. <margin>x</margin>

| Description | Days |
| :--- | :--- |
| From the Winter solstice (December 22) to the end of the month | 9 days |
| In January | 31 days |
| From January until the beginning of spring (February 5) | 5 days |
| **Total for first half-season** | **45 days** |
| Remaining days in February | 23 days |
| In March, until the vernal equinox | 22 days |
| --- | --- |
| **Total days for the season** | **90 days** |

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a Roman scholar and writer</note>, however, marks the beginning of spring precisely at the 45th day after the winter solstice, exactly the same as the Chinese do.

The Roman calendar then marks the beginning of summer 45 days after this; and the Chinese calendar also marks the commencement of the same season after three more solar terms <note>original: *Tsie-ki*; these are the 24 points in the Chinese solar calendar</note> of 15 days each, or at the 10th solar term of the year.

Here then is an absolute accordance between the Roman and Chinese arrangement of the seasons. It should be noted that this Roman Calendar is the old <margin>x</margin> calendar, or the *Fasti* <term>chronological records or calendars</term> of Numa Pompilius <note>the legendary second king of Rome</note>, who is supposed to have derived it through the Sabines <note>an ancient people of central Italy</note> from the ancient Chaldeans <note>an ethnic group in Mesopotamia known for their early expertise in astronomy</note>.

On the 46th day after this—or the 8th day before the Calends <term>the first day of the month</term> of July—the Roman Calendar marks the summer solstice. Similarly, the Chinese Calendar, after three solar terms <note>original: *Tsie-ki*</note> of 15 days each (or at the 13th solar term), also marks the term of summer, just as it had marked the term of winter, or the winter solstice, at the 1st solar term. The two terms mentioned above represent the apparent motion of the sun in its declination—that is, its travel from south to north, or from north to south.

The Roman Calendar marks these periods as 91 days each, or 182 days in total.

The Chinese mark them as 182 days, 62 *Ki* <term>a unit of time in ancient Chinese astronomy, roughly equivalent to 14 minutes and 24 seconds</term>, and 12 minutes.

Father Souciet <note>original: *Pere Souciet*; Étienne Souciet (1671–1744) was a Jesuit scholar</note>, in his *Astronomical Observations, etc.* <note>original: *observations astronomiques, &c.*</note>, volume III, page 72, thus explains the Chinese calculation of the two seasons, from the winter solstice to the—

This is the season of Yng <note>original: *Yng*; likely a transcription of *Yang*, the bright, active principle associated with the sun and summer</note>. Divide the Yng into two parts. The first will be Yng-tsou <note>The beginning of the Yang period</note>, from the winter solstice to the equinox of spring; 88 days, 90 Ki, 92'. The second will be Yng-mo <note>The end of the Yang period</note>, from the equinox of spring to the summer solstice; 93 days, 71 Ki, 20'.”

To understand this better, it should be remembered that the Chinese anciently divided the day into 100 <term>Ki</term> <note>original: *Ki*, also spelled *Ke* (刻); these are "marks" or "quarters" on a water clock, historically representing 1/100th of a day</note>; each Ki had 100 minutes. This usage lasted until the Jesuit Father Schall <note>Johann Adam Schall von Bell (1591–1666), a German Jesuit missionary and astronomer who became a high-ranking official in the Qing Dynasty's Board of Astronomy</note>, according to common report, persuaded them to divide the day into 24 hours. This report, however, may not be quite correct, as Dr. Chalmers <note>John Chalmers (1825–1899), a Scottish missionary and sinologist</note> in his already cited dissertation, page 96, says:—“According to native authorities, the duodecimal division of the day <note>division into twelve parts</note> was not adopted until the time of the Han <note>The Han Dynasty, 206 BC–220 AD</note>;” so that the Chinese also had that method before Father Schall’s time. In fact, the two methods—of the <term>Ke</term> and the hours—were both in use. In the “Memoirs” <note>original: *Memoires*; likely referring to the *Mémoires concernant les Chinois*, a major 18th-century French collection of research on China</note> volume XIII, page 230, it is stated:

> “The twelve <term>Tchi</term> <note>original: *Tchi*, now usually transliterated as *Zhi* (支); refers to the Twelve Earthly Branches used in timekeeping</note> are also used to measure the hours, to each of which the name of a Tchi is given. The Chinese hours contain two of our hours.”

And Father Souciet <note>Étienne Souciet (1685–1744), a French Jesuit scholar who published works on Chinese astronomy</note>, volume III, page 52, in a note, mentions:

> “The day and the night have twelve hours, which formerly made up one hundred Ki. Each hour had 8 Ki and some minutes. Each Ki had 100', each minute had 100''.”

The Chinese calendar marks the division of autumn after three <term>Tsie-ki</term> <note>original: *Tsie-ki*; now Romanized as *Jieqi* (節氣), these are the 24 solar terms that divide the year based on the sun's position</note>, or two days sooner than the Roman calendar. Again, 45 days after that, it marks the winter solstice, returning to the point where this comparison started and where the two calendars reunite.

The number of days in the Roman calendar, from the summer to the winter solstice, is 183, which with the 182 previously accounted for, makes a year of 365 days. Marquis de Fortia d’Urban <note>Agricol-Joseph-François, Marquis de Fortia d'Urban (1756–1843), a French historian and polymath</note> justly remarks on this striking coincidence in volume II, page 86: "This agreement between the calendars of two peoples, situated at the two extremities of the ancient continent, is at the least, very remarkable. It seems that the center of observation was in Chaldea <note>An ancient region in southern Mesopotamia, famous for its early astronomers</note>, from where Numa <note>Numa Pompilius (753–673 BC), the legendary second king of Rome, to whom the reform of the Roman calendar is traditionally attributed</note> and the Chinese received these divisions of time, or <term>kal</term> <note>Likely referring to the root of the word 'calendar' (Latin *kalendae*), or a specific ancient measurement of time</note>, which are found in both the Chinese and Roman calendars."

Besides the similarity noticed above between the Roman and Chinese calendars regarding the division of the seasons, there is also another similarity in the annual meteorological indications <note>weather patterns</note>, which is all the more remarkable given the difference in climate between Italy and China.

In the 6th <term>Tsie-ki</term> <note>original: *Tsie-ki*; these are the 24 solar terms of the traditional Chinese calendar</note>, it is marked "copious showers." In the Roman calendar, the same day is marked "Rain" <note>original: *Pluvia*</note>. In the 9th solar term, the indication is "corn-refreshing showers"; and in the Roman calendar, the previous day marks "Humid day" <note>original: *Dies humidus*</note>. In the 11th solar term, the period is marked "the minor completion," "wheat harvest," or "abundance." The Roman calendar has the "Horn of Plenty of Amalthea" <note>original: *Cornucopia of Amaltheus*; a symbol of abundance and nourishment in Greek and Roman mythology</note>. In the 14th solar term, it is marked "Little heat." In the Roman calendar, two days earlier, it is marked "heat" <note>original: *calor*</note>.

In the 15th solar term, the Chinese have "great heat." The Romans, two days later, mark "sultry mist" <note>original: *caligo Æstuosa*</note>. In the 16th solar term, the Chinese use "relaxation of heat." The Romans have "rain."

The 20th solar term has "cold dews." The Roman date is "rain."

The 21st solar term has "hoar frost appearing." The Roman calendar notes, "Winter starts with cold and frost" <note>original: *Hiemat cum frigore et gelicidio*</note>. Given these similarities existing between the two calendars of Rome and China, it seems surprising that the 60-year cycle—which the Chinese assert was used by them at the same time as the 24 solar terms—was not used by the Romans also, in conjunction with their own calendar. It would appear that the 60-year cycle was not practically known to the Romans or other Western peoples at that time, though Diodorus Siculus <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BC known for his monumental work *Bibliotheca historica*</note> mentions it as an ancient institution.

It has been already mentioned that it was known to the Greeks.

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Fortia d’Urban <note>Marquis de Fortia d'Urban (1758–1843), a French historian and geographer</note>, in explanation of this (Volume II, page 77), says:
> "There was never anything similar to it in the West, and the origin of it must be sought for in the East."

<margin>x</margin>
The Romans calculated their chronology by periods of five years each, and they do not seem to have made any use of the 60-year cycle; possibly because it did not suit them. However, their <term>lustrum</term> <note>a five-year period used in the Roman calendar, originally associated with a census and ritual purification</note> of five years has an affinity with Oriental measurements of time that establishes a curious connection between them. It has already been seen that the Hindu 60-year cycle of Jupiter was not the only cycle connected with that planet. The original cycle of Jupiter was only 12 years, which was the time of one <term>sidereal revolution</term> <note>the time it takes for a planet to orbit the sun once relative to the stars</note> of that planet, as is mentioned in the 14th chapter, paragraph 17 of the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>one of the oldest known astronomical texts in Sanskrit, fundamental to Indian astronomy</note>. The length of one "year of Jupiter" is measured by its passage and by its average motion through one of the 12 signs of the Zodiac.

<margin>x</margin>
The 60-year cycle of Jupiter was formed from that 12-year cycle later on among the Hindus by using five of these original <term>duodenary</term> <note>consisting of twelve parts</note> cycles—or five whole revolutions of this planet through the 12 signs of the Zodiac. This is similar to the Roman <term>lustrum</term>, which was composed of five annual solar cycles.

Burgess <note>Ebenezer Burgess, an American missionary and translator of the Surya Siddhanta in the mid-19th century</note>, in his translation of the *Surya Siddhanta* (page 27), states:
> "The Hindus in ancient times also had a five-year cycle by which they appear first to have regulated time, and which, with an <term>intercalated month</term> <note>a leap month added to the calendar to keep the lunar year in sync with the solar year</note>, anciently"

<summary>This page discusses the astronomical origins of the 60-year cycle, tracing it back to the Hindu 12-year cycle of Jupiter and comparing the use of five-year periods (the Roman lustrum) across cultures.</summary>

> "...maintained the correspondence of the 360-day year with the true solar year."
>
> "This was the *Yuga* <note>Sanskrit: an age or epochal cycle</note>, or age, which was originally used to indicate a cycle or period by which the alignment of conflicting ways of measuring time was maintained. *Yuga* still signifies a <term>lustrum</term> <note>a five-year period</note> of five years. Thus, the Hindu cycle of 60 years was formed from a *Yuga*, or five of Jupiter's revolutions."

At page 271, Burgess <note>Ebenezer Burgess (1805–1870), an American missionary known for translating the Indian astronomical text, the *Surya Siddhanta*</note> further states:

> "In this calculation, Jupiter’s revolution is treated as if it determined a year, just like the sun’s revolution does. The 12 parts into which it is divided—each nearly equaling a solar year—are, by the same analogy, treated as months and accordingly receive the names of the solar months."

Burgess continues:

> "Warren <note>John Warren (1769–1830), a British astronomer in India and author of 'Kala Sankalita' (1825), a study of Southern Indian chronology</note> provides a brief account of the twelve-year cycle in *Kala Sankalita* <note>Sanskrit: 'Collection of Time'</note>, page 212, etc., regarding the <term>Vrihaspati</term> <note>the Sanskrit name for the planet Jupiter</note> cycle of twelve years. The sixty-year cycle contains five cycles of twelve years each, with each cycle supposedly equal to one 'year' of the planet. I only mention this cycle because I found it mentioned in some books, but I know of no nation or tribe that actually reckons time according to that account."

The names of the five cycles, or *Yugas*, are as follows:

| Name of the Year | Presiding Deity |
| :--- | :--- |
| Sumi vatsara <note>Modern Sanskrit: Samvatsara</note> | Agni <note>the Hindu god of fire</note> |
| Pari vatsara <note>Modern Sanskrit: Parivatsara</note> | Arca <note>a name for the Sun god, Surya</note> |
| Idu vatsara <note>Modern Sanskrit: Idavatsara</note> | Chandra <note>the Hindu moon god</note> |
| Anu vatsara <note>Modern Sanskrit: Anuvatsara</note> | Brahma <note>the Hindu creator god</note> |
| Udra vatsara <note>Modern Sanskrit: Udvatsara</note> | Shiva <note>the Hindu god of transformation and destruction</note> |

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The names of the 12 years in this cycle are those of the Hindu lunar months, beginning with Karttika <note>the eighth month of the Hindu calendar, typically falling in October or November</note>, and so on.

“According to both Warren and Davis <note>Samuel Davis (1760–1819), a British surveyor and mathematician who published early studies of Indian astronomy in *Asiatic Researches*</note> (*Asiatic Researches*, Volume 3, page 217, etc.), the 12-year cycle is subordinate to the 60-year cycle. The latter is divided into five such cycles, to which special names are applied; the successive years of each receive, in order, the titles of the solar months. The names of the five cycles are those that properly belong to the years of the <term>lustrum</term> <note>a five-year period used in ancient Rome for purification and census-taking; here used to describe the five-year sub-cycles in Hindu time-keeping</note> of five years in ancient use.”

The coincidence between this Hindu usage and the Roman five-year <term>lustrum</term> is therefore undoubted.

Warren, in the place cited <note>original: "loc: cit:"</note>, says, “he knows of no nation or tribe making any use of this 12-year cycle, but only finds it mentioned in books. The Southern Indians, if they ever did, have long ceased to pay attention to the months of the Chakra <note>Sanskrit: 'wheel' or 'cycle'</note> year.”

This remark about the Southern Indians is significant; if they never used the cycle of Jupiter, it could not have been an original <term>Turanian</term> <note>a historical term used in the 19th century to refer to various non-Aryan, non-Semitic peoples of Central and Northern Asia</note> system, and the Chinese could not have known it from their own ancient traditions.

<margin>x</margin>
Cowasjee Patell <note>a 19th-century Parsi scholar and author of *Cowasjee Patell's Chronology* (1866)</note>, in his work on Chronology, page 46, states: “The true cycle of Jupiter being twelve years, the Tibetans count by this cycle when calculating their age. In the ordinary affairs of life, they employ the 60-year cycle, each year of which has its distinct name, just as it has in India.”

The Tibetan writers on the *Kala chakra* <note>Sanskrit: 'Wheel of Time,' a Buddhist tantric system that includes detailed astronomical and chronological teachings</note> system state “that the 60-year cycle was introduced from India, about..."

The author suggests these systems were likely imported from India to Tibet and China, rather than originating in a broader Central Asian tradition.</summary>

> "1025 AD, and that it was only introduced into India

Patell <note>Cowasjee Sorabjee Patell, author of "Chronology" (1866)</note>, on page 50, after quoting a French translation of *Al-Biruni* <note>Abū al-Rayḥān al-Bīrūnī (973–1048), a Persian polymath who wrote a famous study of India</note> concerning Indian cycles, includes a note by Reinaud <note>Joseph Toussaint Reinaud (1795–1867), a French orientalist</note>:
> "The result of this passage, in its entirety, seems to 
> "be that the cycle of 60 was of recent institution, and 
> "was specific only to a certain portion of India. The 
> "calculation presented by Al-Biruni leads me to believe

Bentley <note>John Bentley (died 1824), a member of the Asiatic Society who controversially argued that ancient Indian astronomical works were much newer than claimed</note> places Varahamihira <note>an influential 6th-century Indian astronomer and mathematician</note>, the supposed author of the *Surya Siddhanta*, in the 10th century of the Christian era. however, neither his theory, nor those of Patell, Reinaud, and Bentley, are to be relied upon on this point. The *Surya Siddhanta* <note>the "Sun Treatise," a foundational text of Indian astronomy</note> has been shown to be no more recent than 346 AD through astronomical comparisons of the time of the vernal equinox <note>the moment the sun crosses the celestial equator; the shift in its position over centuries allows historians to date ancient observations</note>, mentioned in the 8th chapter of that book. See Guerin, *Indian Astronomy* <note>original: "Astronomie Indienne" (1847)</note>, chapter 3, page 21, who there proves that the *Surya Siddhanta* was written in Sanskrit verses by a Brahmin called Shourdijyo at that date, which is the source of its name. However, it contains a record of the supposed revolutions by the sun itself given to *Moyo* (or *Maya* <note>a mythical figure in Hindu texts, often described as a great architect or astronomer to whom the Sun god revealed knowledge</note>; see *Surya Siddhanta*, Chapter 1, page 2) made long before they were thus recorded by Shourdijyo. Therefore, the tradition of the 60-year cycle was not in any way as modern as Patell and others would suggest <note>original: "insinuate"</note>.

If the suppositions of Reinaud and Al-Biruni were true, it would place the origin of the 60-year cycle in China at an even more modern date than needs to be assumed; for—

<summary>This page argues against the 19th-century view that Indian 60-year cycles were a late invention (c. 959 AD), citing astronomical evidence from the Surya Siddhanta to date the system back to at least 346 AD.</summary>
