<meta>This page continues the discussion of Asian chronological systems, specifically examining whether the 60-year cycles used in India, Tibet, and China share a common origin or were developed independently.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

If the cycle arrived in Tibet from India so late, it would have traveled east into China even later; unless one relies on the unsupported theory <note>original: "gratuitous hypothesis"</note> that the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note> received it from China and afterward passed it on to the Tibetans.

It is remarkable that the Tibetans give a different unique name to each year of the 60-year cycle (which are translations of the Sanskrit names), just as the Hindus do. However, they also use names for the years of the cycle that are Tibetan translations of the Chinese composite names <note>Chinese year names are "composite" because they combine two sets of characters: the 10 "Heavenly Stems" and 12 "Earthly Branches."</note>.

Patell <note>Cowasjee Sorabjee Patell, author of "Chronology" (1866)</note>, on page 47 of the work cited <note>original: "op : cit :"</note>, calls attention to the mismatch <note>original: "discrepancy"</note> between the start of the Indian and Chinese cycles. The first year of the Indian cycle corresponds to the fourth year of the Chinese cycle, and he urges this as proof that the two cycles are not connected or derived from each other.

This may be the case if one only considers the names or the order of each year in the cycle. However, it seems clear that the Chinese and Indian cycles are both founded on the movement of the planet Jupiter <note>Jupiter takes approximately 12 years to orbit the sun; five of these orbits equal the 60-year cycle</note>. An examination of the different ways in which the two nations worked out or recorded their cycles—each according to their own peculiar system—may show that the 12-year cycle, which is the common foundation of both, originally belonged to the system of some other more primitive nation from which they both derived it, either directly and independently or indirectly.

<summary>This page analyzes the relationship between Tibetan, Indian, and Chinese 60-year cycles, arguing that despite differences in their start dates and naming conventions, they likely share a common ancient origin based on the 12-year orbital period of Jupiter.</summary>
<keywords>Tibet, Hindus, China, India, 60-year cycle, 12-year cycle, Jupiter’s motion, Sanskrit, Patell, chronology, astronomy</keywords>

->### THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.<-

...either because one knew it first and then shared it with the other.

As previously noted on page 143, in Sima Qian's <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien," a Han dynasty historian often called the "Father of Chinese Historiography"</note> list of the 12 *chi* <note>The 12 "Earthly Branches," used in East Asian timekeeping to track years, months, and hours</note>—which combine to form the 60-year cycle—the first name, *Shih-te*, means the planet Jupiter and refers to its movement across the heavens. It is likely that the other eleven names in Sima Qian's list also represent specific movements of the same planet and carry the same meanings as the more modern Chinese names for the 12 *chi*. This remains to be proven, but the standard names for the 12 Chinese *chi* are clearly used in the same way as the Hindu <note>original: "Hindoos"</note> words in Sima Qian's list. Therefore, they are probably connected to the 12-year cycle of Jupiter.

In his book *Chaldean Magic*, Lenormant states on page 81 that the mystical <note>original: "cabalistic"</note> name "Zedekiel" means Jupiter. The striking similarity between *Shih-te-ki* and *Zedeki* almost suggests that Sima Qian's list of names may have a mystical or "cabalistic" origin.

On page 96 of the work previously cited <note>original: "op : cit :" (Latin: *opere citato*)</note>, Chalmers states: "The twelve 'terrestrial branches,' as they are called, were most likely first invented to distinguish the twelve sections into which the horizon is divided." On page 95, he adds: "This division of the zodiac was probably introduced to China at the end of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow," a dynasty ruling roughly 1046–256 BC</note> or the beginning of the Han dynasty." He refers to a passage in the *Zuo Zhuan* <note>original: "Tso Chuan," an ancient Chinese narrative history and commentary</note> to show that two of these signs are mentioned for astrological purposes in connection with the planet Jupiter.

And on page 93, 
> “The use of the planet Jupiter for astrological purposes belongs to the early Han dynasty. At that time, the period of Jupiter’s orbit was believed to be exactly 12 years, so that it spent one year in each sign of the zodiac; therefore, it is called the ‘year star.’”

Souciet <note>original: "Pere Souciet," Étienne Souciet (1685–1744), a Jesuit scholar who published extensively on Chinese astronomy</note>, in Volume II, page 84, says that Yi Xing <note>original: "Y-hang," a famous Buddhist monk, mathematician, and astronomer of the Tang dynasty (683–727 AD)</note> particularly examined the movements of Jupiter and laid down as a principle that this planet does not take exactly 12 years for its revolution in the zodiac. Thus, it is clear that by the Song <note>original: "Sung"</note> dynasty, the 12-year cycle of Jupiter was well known to the Chinese.

In Volume III, page 30, Souciet writes:
> “During the time of the Xia <note>original: "Hia," the first dynasty in traditional Chinese history</note> dynasty, the year was called *Sui* <note>original: "Souy," the pinyin is *sui*</note>;
<margin>X</margin>
> “The character *Sui* means the planet Jupiter. It was believed at that time that the revolution of one year is called *Sui*. This interpretation dates to the Jin <note>original: "Tsin," likely referring to the Jin dynasty (266–420 AD) or the earlier Qin dynasty (221–206 BC)</note>.”

No doubt the attribution of the tradition about Jupiter to the Xia dynasty was a modern invention, like other events assigned by the Chinese to that supposed period of their history. 
<margin>X</margin>
> “The author of the *Guoyu* <note>original: "Kwei Yu," an ancient Chinese text also known as the *Discourses of the States*</note>, whoever he may be—though he lived around the time of Confucius—supposed that Jupiter completed the twelfth part of its course through the equator, or the zodiac, in one solar year.”

The statements of Dr. Chalmers and of Father Souciet go a long way to prove that the Chinese used the 12-year Jupiter cycle as the basis of their 60-year cycle, 
<margin>X</margin>
just as the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note> did. But the statement of...

<margin>?</margin>
Souciet <note>original: "Souciet," referring to Étienne Souciet (1685–1744), a Jesuit scholar who published on Chinese astronomy</note> provides no proof as to what period the 12-year cycle of Jupiter was first known to the Chinese. It would instead seem from the work of Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien," the "Grand Historian" of the Han dynasty (c. 145–86 BC)</note> that it first appeared in China during the Han dynasty. However, it is a question of whether that part of Sima Qian's work which refers to it is not actually the work of a more recent author, grafted onto the original book.

Some light may be shed on this point by determining at what date the Chinese had a cycle of 12 years that was unconnected to the 12-year cycle of Jupiter from the same period.

De Mailla <note>original: "De Mailla," Joseph-Marie-Anne de Moyriac de Mailla (1669–1748), a Jesuit missionary who wrote a foundational history of China</note>, in a note in *History of China*, Volume VI, page 317, says:
<margin>x</margin>
> "The Chinese have a cycle of twelve years, which they apply also to the hours; it is by this cycle that they determine the year of their birth."

It will be remembered that the Tibetans <note>original: "Thibetans"</note>, in calculating their own ages from the year of their birth, count by the 12-year cycle of Jupiter. It is therefore evident that Father De Mailla here refers to the same cycle. <margin>x</margin> He adds, "These years and these hours have the names of twelve animals"—which further identifies the Mongol cycle of animals with the short cycle of Jupiter.

It may be observed, however, that De Mailla does not state in the passage just quoted whether the computation of 12 hours preceded the cycle of 12 years, or vice versa.

> "The Tianhuang <note>original: "Tien-hwang," or the Heavenly Sovereign, a legendary first ruler of China</note> made a cycle of ten, and another of twelve."

...of ten and twelve years, or of ten and twelve hours, or days. Continuing from the previous discussion, however, the author of the *Memoirs* <note>original: "Memoires," likely referring to the "Mémoires concernant les Chinois," a multi-volume 18th-century collection of Jesuit research on China</note> states: "Before the Tianhuang <note>original: "Tien-hwang," the "Heavenly Emperors," a group of legendary prehistoric rulers in Chinese mythology</note>, the name of the year was unknown. And at page 182: "The Dihuang <note>original: "Ti-hwang," the "Earthly Emperors," another group of legendary rulers</note> used the words 'month' or 'lunation' <note>the time between two new moons</note> to describe the interval of 30 days."

<margin>Origin</margin>
If this is all that can be said by Chinese records about the origin of the cycles of 10 and 12, it can only amount to a vague tradition of the existence of these periods in ancient times. It gives no fixed date for their introduction into China, and we are left to speculate as to their origin.

<margin>60</margin>
The formation of a cycle of 60 <note>the "sexagenary cycle," a cycle of sixty terms used for recording days or years in China</note>, by a combination of two separate cycles of 10 and 12 each, must be very ancient. Even if the Chinese method of combining the cycles of 10 and 12 together was not the very first method used to create a chronological period, it may at least be a survival of some ancestral worship theory. This theory might have been adopted in ancient times for this purpose and has since been long forgotten.

<margin>Stems</margin>
<margin>Branches</margin>
It was not without reason that the Chinese designated the cycle of ten as a cycle of "stems" and the cycle of twelve as a cycle of "branches" <note>the "Heavenly Stems" and "Earthly Branches" are the two components used to build the 60-year cycle</note>. The reason for these peculiar designations seems to have been forgotten by the Chinese, or at least they give no satisfactory explanation for why they are called this. There...

<page-num>181</page-num>
# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

There are other countries besides China in which a similar, if not the same, chronological arrangement seems to have been used, and where the memory of it still lingers. Al-Biruni <note>original: "Albiruni," a renowned 11th-century Persian scholar and polymath</note>, in the work cited <note>original: "op: cit:," likely referring to "The Chronology of Ancient Nations"</note> on page 2, writing of the chronology of Central Asia, states:

> "A learned man once asked me about the eras of different nations, and about the difference between their roots (or stems)—meaning the starting points or epochs where they begin—and their branches—meaning the months and years on which they are based."

<margin>

These roots, or stems, were thus the epochs where an era begins. It may be suggested that the ten Chinese *Gans* <note>original: "Kans," the "Heavenly Stems" used in the traditional Chinese calendar</note>, or stems (or roots), signify ten epochs that were at the beginning of an era, the exact dates of which are unrecorded. 

Could these be the Chinese names for the ten periods of the lives of their ancestors—and ours—the ten patriarchs from Adam to Noah? A vague tradition of this still exists in the "Ten *Ji*" <note>original: "ten Ki," referring to the ten legendary "periods" or "ages" of the prehistoric era in traditional Chinese mythology</note> of the primitive history of China. 

In addition to the suggestion provided by this analogy, there is another question: how or why were these ten epochs (roots or stems) developed into a continuous system by being joined with twelve "branches" <note>the twelve "Earthly Branches," which combine with the ten stems to form the 60-year cycle</note>, through which their duration was meant to be preserved? Perhaps, once the mystery of Chinese history has been fully understood, it may be discovered that a very ancient historical theory has been unconsciously preserved in China—one which future research may eventually unravel.

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

Francois Lenormant, in the *Contemporary Review* (April 1880, page 582, "Biblical Genealogies"), offers such interesting remarks on this subject that they are included here to illustrate it:

> "It appears that in the book where Berosus <note>original: "Berosus," a 3rd-century BCE Babylonian priest and historian who wrote in Greek</note> presented the Chaldean <note>ancient Mesopotamian</note> traditions, the first ten generations after the flood constituted a cycle or an epoch. This was undoubtedly still entirely mythical, forming a parallel to the ten antediluvian <note>original: "antidiluvian," meaning before the Great Flood</note> reigns (Berosus as cited by Josephus: book 1, chapter 7, section 2).
>
> On page 583, he adds: 'The total duration of the ten antediluvian reigns was 120 *sari* <note>original: "sari," or saros, a Babylonian unit of time equaling 3,600 years</note>, or periods of 3,600 years—that is to say, 432,000 years. A tenth of this period is 43,200 years, or 12 *sari*. For the Chaldeans, this constituted a celestial revolution and served as a true cosmic day. Each of its *sari* contained 60 *sossi* <note>original: "sossi," a Babylonian unit of 60 years</note> of 60 years, just as the day was divided into 12 hours of 60 minutes, with each containing 60 seconds. 
>
> By using only 12 hours in the full day cycle <note>original: "nychthemeron," a Greek term for a 24-hour period of day and night</note> instead of 24 as we do, the Chaldean-Babylonians tracked the daily revolution of the sun on the same plan as its annual revolution and the zodiac. Consequently, each one of the *sari* in the 43,200-year period corresponded to a sign and to a month of the year, as well as to an hour of the day. But this period was again multiplied by 12, and thus a more extended sidereal <note>relating to the stars or constellations</note> revolution was obtained of 144 *sari*, or 518,400 years. 
>
> Movers <note>Franz Karl Movers (1806–1856), a German scholar who studied Phoenician and Semitic history</note> recognized long ago that the fact of the equal duration of the ten antediluvian..."

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

...placing the *Ti-hwang* <note>original: "Ti-hwang," the "Sovereigns of Earth" in Chinese mythology</note>, or sovereigns of earth, in the sign of the Ox.

The subject is one that demands a comparative investigation beyond the limits of this small work.

It may also be mentioned that Patell, in the work already cited <note>original: "op: cit:"</note> on page 5, says:

> "The Turkish era (from the time of Timur <note>also known as Tamerlane, the 14th-century Central Asian conqueror</note>)
> "commenced with the creation of the world, and is com-
> "puted in cycles of 12 solar years each."

<margin>x</margin>
The cycle of 12 years was used in Babylonia in very ancient times. Sayce <note>Archibald Sayce (1845–1933), a pioneering British Assyriologist and linguist</note>, in the work already cited on page 53, mentions:

> "The Akkadians <note>original: "Accadians," an ancient Mesopotamian civilization</note> had, as if in anticipation of Dr.
> "Hunter <note>likely referring to 19th-century theories regarding 11 or 12-year solar/weather cycles</note>, expected the same weather after a cycle of
> "12 years."

The Chinese might, therefore, have indirectly obtained the 12-year cycle from the Babylonians. However, if they did not learn it from that source, the earliest possible introduction of such a period into China could only have been the result of their own simple observations. These observations would have shown them that there were twelve lunar revolutions from one season of the year until its apparent recurrence <note>original: "reoccurrence"</note> in the next year. Consequently, they realized that this number of periods might be used to divide the year into 12 parts; by expanding this calculation, they might have created a cyclical division of 12 years.

From that division of the year, the calculation would possibly descend to a division of the day into 12 parts, so as to make its composition analogous to that of the year. However, it has already been seen that this division of the day into 12 hours was not adopted in...

<page-num>185</page-num>

# THE ORIGIN AND SOURCE OF CHINESE CHRONOLOGY.

In China, up until the time of the Han dynasty <note>206 BCE – 220 CE</note>, the 12-hour cycle could not have been the basis of the Chinese 60-year cycle unless the latter was even more recent.

The "double hour," or *cas-bu* as the Akkadians <note>original: "Accadians"</note> called it—which is equal to two of our hours—had long been in use in Babylonia (Sayce, *in the work already cited, p. 54*). The Chinese might, therefore, have acquired the division of the day into 12 hours from the Babylonians and used it alongside their other division into 100 *Ke* <note>original: "Ki," a traditional Chinese unit of time where a day is divided into 100 segments</note>, even if this knowledge was only passed down through tradition without appearing in any formal books.

The division of the day into 12 hours is also mentioned in the *Zend Avesta* <note>the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism</note>, and the Persian Iranians might have communicated this system to the Chinese.

It even appears possible that in China, the division of the year into 12 months occurred after the division of the day into 12 hours. Étienne Souciet <note>a French Jesuit scholar (1671–1744) who published works on Chinese astronomy</note>, in Volume III, page 15, mentions:

> "It is no less certain that the moons, or
> "lunar months, have been constantly designated by the
> "signs whose characters represent the twelve
> "hours. For example, the eleventh month may be
> "called *Zhou* <note>original: "Tcheou," referring to one of the Earthly Branches</note>, because the sun during the eleventh
> "month always enters the sign *Zhou*," and in a note
> "to this passage he adds: "The 12 characters of the
> "12 hours thus express the order of the months: *Yin* <note>original: "Yu"</note> as the
> "first month, *Mao* as the second, *Chen* <note>original: "Chin"</note> as the third, etc., etc."

This reversed order of time periods, however, may be less real than the passages above suggest; for the names of the hours that are said to be used to designate the months may have been only one set of the

> “The months were all named in

This adaptation of stellar names to the different months is, at the very least, remarkable. Cowasjee Sorabjee Patell <note>author of a major work on Indian and Asian chronology</note>, in the work cited previously on page 35, says:

<margin>X</margin>
> “It is remarkable
> “that the *Tze* <note>original: "Tze," referring to the twelve Earthly Branches used in the Chinese calendar</note>, which are strictly portions of sidereal <note>original: “star”</note> time,
> “give their names to the lunar months.” <margin>12-part time</margin>

There can be no doubt that the division of the year into 12 parts was a most ancient usage in central and eastern Asia. It is possible that the number 12 might have been applied to create a cycle of years without any connection to the 12-year period of Jupiter; but this division of the year into 12 parts, and the similar duodecimal <note>a system based on the number twelve</note> division of the day, are currently unconnected with any systematic calculation of time by a period of 12 years—unless one refers to the 12-year orbital period of the planet Jupiter. Therefore, to bring them all into logical and historical relation, it would be necessary to show that the cycle of 12 years was as ancient as the 12-month period by which the dura- <note>The text continues "duration" on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into ancient Chinese timekeeping, arguing that the 12-year Jupiter cycle was a later addition to their astronomical systems and likely influenced by Western knowledge during the transition to the Han dynasty.</meta>

->THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES. 187<-

...calculation <note>original fragment: “tion”</note> of the year was reckoned; and this can hardly be said of the 12-year cycle of Jupiter. In China, this was certainly of more modern origin than the other twelve-part <note>original: “duodenary”</note> cycles. This is especially true as far as the Chinese are concerned, for it seems clear that in the 3rd century <term>BCE</term> <note>Before the Common Era</note> they knew nothing scientifically certain about the ancient methods of computing time. Étienne Souciet <note>a Jesuit scholar and historian of Chinese astronomy</note>, in Volume 3, page 4, states:

> “Chinese astronomers unanimously agree that by the year 206 <term>BCE</term>, when the founder of the **Han dynasty** took possession of the empire, hardly anything was known of the ancient methods which had been taught by the founders of the monarchy.”

The division of the year into 12 months may possibly have prepared the way for the division of the heavens into the 12 signs of the zodiac, and thus facilitated the focus on the cycle of Jupiter through them. However, it is supposed that the Chinese anciently divided the heavens into 28 parts or <term>sieu</term> <note>lunar mansions or star houses</note>. As some writers—such as Gustave Schlegel in the *work previously cited* <note>original: “op; cit:”</note>—urge that the 12 signs of the zodiac were deduced from these 28 parts, there is no ground for saying that they derived the 12 signs of the zodiac from the twelve-fold division of the year, or that they measured the passage of Jupiter through them in that way.

Dr. John Chalmers <note>a 19th-century British sinologist and missionary</note>, in his *previously cited work* on page 95, says: 

> “The introduction of the 12 zodiacal signs dates from the end of the **Zhou** <note>original: “Chow”</note> or the beginning of the **Han dynasty**.” 

If this is so, it must have been learned from Western nations. In a note to this, he states: “We do not find that the ancient Chinese made much practical use of the 12 signs.”

<summary>This page argues that the Chinese 12-year Jupiter cycle and the 12 zodiac signs were likely late introductions, possibly from Western sources, rather than ancient components of Chinese astronomy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Chinese 12 and 60-year cycles, specifically questioning their antiquity and suggesting they were adaptations of Indian (Hindu) astronomical systems rather than native inventions from the era of legendary emperors.</meta>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

<margin>Étienne Souciet, in the *previously cited work* <note>original: "op: cit:"</note>, volume 2, page 122, states: "During the Tang dynasty, a famous Buddhist monk <term>bonze of Fo</term> <note>A "bonze" is a term for a monk, and "Fo" is the Chinese name for Buddha.</note> named Pou-kong taught the Chinese the names of the twelve signs of the zodiac: Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and so on."</margin>

The use of the 12-year cycle in China is attributed to the time of Tchuen-ho <note>also known as Zhuanxu, a legendary monarch of ancient China</note>, or even to that of Fu-hi <note>Fuxi, the first of the mythical Three Sovereigns</note>. However, there does not seem to be any historical evidence that this is actually the case. Nor does there seem to be any reason—based on comparisons with other similar Chinese methods of calculating time already mentioned—to believe they were users or inventors of such a cycle during the era ascribed to those ancient figures.

It is even questionable whether the Chinese ever used a ten-year cycle for years. Consequently, there is no certainty that the 60-year cycle could have originally been a composite of two such minor cycles of years, as has been previously assumed.

It is, however, evident that their 60-unit cycle was arranged by combining the two cycles of 10 and 12, applied to either days or months. It is quite consistent with the Chinese practice of "sinicizing" <note>making something Chinese in character or form</note> all knowledge received from others that when the Indian <term>Hindoo</term> 60-year cycle was introduced to them, they would have arranged it in the same way as their previous 60-interval time periods. They did this instead of literally copying the Indian structure and naming conventions of the 60-year cycle of Jupiter. Yet, by departing from the Indian method of marking the 60-year cycle, they did not succeed in erasing all traces of its origin...

<summary>This page argues that the Chinese 12 and 60-year cycles are not as ancient as legendary history suggests, but were likely adapted from Indian astronomical systems and modified to fit existing Chinese timekeeping structures.</summary>
<keywords>Tang dynasty, Pou-kong, zodiac, Tchuen-ho, Fu-hi, 60-year cycle, Indian (Hindoo) cycle, Jupiter's cycle, Chinese chronology</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of Chinese cycles, specifically looking at how the Chinese may have appropriated the cycle of Jupiter and adapted foreign astronomical methods into their own systems, including the use of a 60-month lunar cycle.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES. 189

...traces of where they derived it from; indeed, they left proof in Sima Qian's <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien," a Han dynasty historian often called the "Grand Historian" of China</note> list of cyclical years that it was actually the cycle of Jupiter which they had appropriated and transformed.

J. B. Biot, in *Studies on Indian and Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: "Etudes Sur l'astronomie Indienne et Chinoise"</note>, Paris 1852, page x, makes the following remarks on this system of appropriation:

> "When examining the applications of astronomical rules and calculations used by various nations of antiquity to see whether they show signs of originality or of having been borrowed from other peoples, if it is discovered that the use of foreign methods has been intentionally disguised and adapted to local customs and superstitions, it becomes evidence that there was a communication of ideas between them that has not been acknowledged." <note>original French: "On examinant ce que les applications des règles et des calculs astronomiques... une communication d'idées qui n'a pas été avouée."</note>

It is interesting to observe, in connection with this question, that among the Chinese there was also a lunar cycle of 60 months. Patell, in the work previously cited <note>original: "op : cit :"</note> on page 36, states:

> "The months <note>original: "moons"</note> of the civil year are also distinguished by their place in the cycle of sixty. Since the intercalary months <term>intercalary moons</term> <note>extra months added periodically to a calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year and seasons</note> are not counted—because during one of those lunations the sun enters no new zodiac sign—there are only twelve regular months in a year. Therefore, the cycle is renewed every five years. Thus, since the first month of 1862 was the start of a new cycle, the first month of every sixth year (reckoned either backward or forward from that date) will also begin a new lunar cycle of 60 months."

<summary>This page argues that Chinese astronomical cycles, such as the cycle of Jupiter and the 60-month lunar cycle, were likely adapted from foreign systems and "disguised" to fit local Chinese traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Sima Qian, cycle of Jupiter, J. B. Biot, lunar cycle, intercalary months, astronomical appropriation, Sze-ma-ts'ien, Patell</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into Chinese chronological systems, specifically focusing on the 60-month lunar cycle and the 60-day cycle, comparing their structure to the 60-year cycle of Jupiter.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

There is no evidence regarding which year this lunar cycle of 60 months <note>original: "moons"</note> was introduced into China. However, it is worthy of attention because, in addition to being a five-year cycle, it represents a combination of five sets of twelve months to create a 60-month period. This is similar to the combination of these numbers that we have already seen as the foundation for the 60-year cycle of Jupiter; that cycle was formed by multiplying the twelve years of the sidereal revolution <term>sidereal revolution</term> <note>the time it takes for a planet to complete one orbit around the sun relative to the stars</note> of Jupiter by five to create a larger, specialized cycle for that planet. Because this 60-month lunar cycle was not formed by combining the numbers 10 and 12—as other Chinese 60-part cycles were arranged—the names for each month <note>original: "moon"</note> of the cycle were not created or adapted following that same plan.

As previously mentioned, Cowasjee Patell <note>original: "Patell," referring to the author of 'Patell's Chronology' (1866)</note> points out that the names of the lunar months are actually the names of the 12 *Tze* <term>Tze</term> <note>the "Twelve Terrestrial Branches" used in the Chinese calendar</note> and are therefore solar in origin. Jean-Baptiste Souciet <note>original: "Souciet," an 18th-century Jesuit scholar</note>, in the work previously cited <note>original Latin: "opere citato"</note>, page 135, confirms this:

> “The twelve *chi* <note>another term for the 'branches' or divisions of the zodiac</note> are used to mark the months <note>original: "moons"</note> of the year. The intercalary month <term>intercalary moon</term> <note>a leap month added periodically to the calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note> has no specific character assigned to it in this cycle.”

The lunar cycle, therefore, seems to have originated from the solar cycle; however, aside from being a repetition of other sexagesimal <term>sexagesimal</term> <note>relating to the number sixty</note> cycles, it has no fundamental scientific connection to them.

The Chinese also have another sexagesimal cycle of days, which are organized in the calendar into 60-day cycles. Each day of the cycle has a specific name, according to William Frederick Mayers <note>original: "Mayers"</note> in the *Chinese Reader’s Manual* <note>original: "C.R.M."</note>, page 348.

This system is similar to the Egyptian "year" of two months, or sixty days, but it is a matter of uncertainty at what time this cycle of 60 days was introduced into China. Dr. Chalmers, discussing this question in the work previously cited <note>original Latin: "op : cit :" (opere citato)</note> on page 96, states:

> "The application of the cycle to days is undoubtedly a very ancient practice. However, it would seem from a passage in the *Shoo* <note>The *Shujing*, or *Classic of History*, one of the Five Classics of ancient Chinese literature</note>, Part II, Book IV, paragraph 8, that the days were originally arranged only in groups of ten by means of the 10 'celestial stems' <term>celestial stems</term> <note>a Chinese system of ordinal numbers used for dating</note>. The Emperor Yu is recorded as saying, 'I remained with my wife only the days Sin, Jên, Kwei, and Kia.' These are the last three and the first of the set of characters mentioned above. The natural inference from their use here is that they were invented to divide the month into three equal parts (three decades <term>decades</term> <note>ten-day periods</note>), and that in the course of time, they were combined to make the famous cycle of 60. The first mention of the cyclical name of a day is found in the *Shoo*, Part IV, Book IV, page 1, in the 12th month of the 1st year of Tai Jia <note>original: "Tae-këă," a king of the Shang Dynasty</note>, or 1752 B.C. However, this chronology is utterly valueless, and we have no sufficient data to verify the day; moreover, this is the only instance of the use of the cycle that occurs before 1121 B.C. in the same chronology. In the *Books of Zhou* <note>original: "Chow"</note>, it is frequently employed."

<summary>This page explores the 10 Celestial Stems and the ancient 60-day cycle in China, suggesting they originated as a way to divide the month into three ten-day decades.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese cycles, Ten Celestial Stems, Shujing, Dr. Chalmers, Emperor Yu, Tai Jia, Zhou Dynasty, chronology, 60-day cycle</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origin of the ten-day division of the month (decades) in China, comparing it to similar systems used by the Greeks, Chaldeans, and Indians.</meta>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The suggestion made by Dr. Chalmers is supported by the fact that a similar division of the month into three decades <term>decades</term> <note>ten-day periods, from the Greek "dekas"</note> was used in ancient Athens. However, there is no evidence to show how this resemblance occurred between the monthly division used by the Greeks and the similar measurement of time used by the Chinese. Both the Athenians and the Chinese faced the same difficulties regarding months of different lengths, yet they both managed to keep the ten-day calculation intact and divide each month in their own specific ways.

The division of time into periods of ten days was very ancient, though its origin is not entirely clear. It seems to have been used extensively for astrological purposes. Guerin, in the work previously cited <note>original Latin: "op: cit:" (opere citato)</note> on pages 181 and 185, says it was used by the Chaldeans <term>Chaldeans</term> <note>an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their early developments in astronomy and astrology</note>. He quotes the historian Diodorus Siculus, who states:

> “Every ten days a star is sent to Earth by the planets,
> “and a star leaves the Earth to inform them of what is
> “passing there. These observers of the actions of
> “men, who followed each other every ten days in the
> “Zodiac, and rose above the horizon or descended
> “every ten days, are the *Drekhanos*, or the *Dekhans*,
> “which means ‘one who points out,’ or ‘one who looks’ at what is
> “passing.”

He then adds that the *Decania* <note>a term for the division of the zodiac into ten-degree segments</note> mentioned in the fourth book <note>original Latin: "Lib. iv." (Liber iv)</note> of Manilius’s work, lines 296–300, probably comes from this Indian word.

Colebrooke, in his “Life and Essays,” Volume III, pages 325–327, also discusses this subject. He states: “The 36 *Dreshkanos*, or *Decani* <term>decans</term> <note>groups of stars used in ancient astronomy to divide the 360-degree zodiac into 36 parts</note>, were depicted with different attributes...”

<summary>This page explores the ancient global practice of dividing the month into ten-day periods, noting similarities between Chinese, Greek, and Chaldean systems. It suggests an astrological origin for these "decades," linking them to the movement of stars and planetary "observers."</summary>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Decani"—ten-day astrological divisions—and their linguistic roots, comparing Indian, Egyptian, and Chinese systems of timekeeping.</meta>

->### THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.<-

> "...and dresses. This astrological concept was admittedly <note>original: "confessedly"</note> adopted from foreign nations. Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer</note> attributes it to Nechepso, a king of Egypt. The names of the *Decani* <term>Decani</term> <note>original Latin: "Decani"; refers to the thirty-six spirits or deities who rule over ten-degree segments of the zodiac</note> are clearly of foreign origin <note>original: "barbarous"</note>. It is not improbable that some similarity in sound in the Egyptian or Chaldean name may have suggested the creation of this distorted word. The Sanskrit name apparently comes from the same source; I do not suppose it to be originally Sanskrit, since in that language it carries no linguistic meaning."

This last portion of Colebrooke's <note>Henry Thomas Colebrooke (1765–1837), a pioneering European scholar of Sanskrit and ancient Indian mathematics</note> remarks is disputed by Guerin in the previously cited passage <note>original Latin abbreviation: "loc: cit:" (loco citato)</note>, who states that the Sanskrit word *Dekhano* means "one who observes or looks at what is going on." This meaning of *Dekhano* is quite in accordance with the Chinese sound *Kan*, which is one syllable of the Sanskrit word; in Chinese, *Kan* signifies "to look at" and is used to denote the 10 *Kan*, or the ten-part decimal cycle <term>decimal cycle</term> <note>a system of counting or measuring time based on the number ten</note> which is now under examination. It is possible that the Chinese 10 *Kan*—the names of which are the same as those mentioned above belonging to the days—may thus be identified with the Hindu *Decani* and with the similar ten-day period used by the Chaldeans <term>Chaldeans</term> <note>an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their early developments in astronomy and astrology</note>.

The chief point to notice in the matter is that this decimal cycle, or "ten *Kan*," was not originally used for years but for days. Although it was used to make up a sexagesimal cycle <term>sexagesimal cycle</term> <note>a cycle of sixty, a common base for timekeeping in ancient cultures (e.g., 60 minutes in an hour or the Chinese 60-year cycle)</note> of days, it is most unlikely that it was the origin of the sixty-year cycle or the sixty-month cycle. The sixty-day cycle was formed by multiplying the ten-day periods by six, and the sixty months...

<summary>This page explores the linguistic and cultural connections between the Chinese ten-day cycle (Kan) and the astrological "Decani" of Egypt, India, and Chaldea, arguing that these systems shared a common origin in tracking days rather than years.</summary>

<keywords>Chinese cycles, Decani, Nechepso, Sanskrit, Chaldean, Kan, decimal cycle, sexagesimal cycle, Henry Thomas Colebrooke, Guerin, astrology, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origin of the "10 Kan" or decimal cycle, comparing Chinese timekeeping methods with those of the Greeks, Egyptians, Hindus, and Japanese.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The cycle was found by multiplying 12 by five, and the cycle of 60 years was created by intertwining combinations of 12 and 10. Therefore, the 60-day cycle could not have been the origin of the 60-year cycle. Furthermore, there is no evidence to show that the Athenians, who used a 10-day cycle, ever extended their "decades" <note>original: "decades of days"; a ten-day week used in the ancient Greek calendar</note> beyond a series of three (totaling 30 days). This was similar to the division of time used by the Egyptians, even though the Greeks possessed <margin>the sexagesimal <term>sexagesimal</term> <note>a system of counting or measurement based on the number 60, much like how we still measure minutes and seconds</note> arithmetic of Ptolemy</margin> and practically used it for dividing the equatorial degree into 60 degrees, 60 minutes, and 60 seconds.

The only Asian period that resembles the Chinese cycle of 60 days is the Hindu cycle of the *Ritu* <note>Sanskrit: "Ritu"; refers to the six seasons in the Hindu calendar</note>. Patell <note>Cowasjee Patell, a 19th-century author who wrote extensively on historical calendars</note>, in his previously cited work <note>original Latin abbreviation: "op: cit:" (opere citato)</note> on pages 38 and 39, writes:

> "The Hindu solar year is divided into six seasons (*Ritu*) of two sidereal <term>sidereal</term> <note>time measured by the position of the stars rather than the sun</note> months each, the succession of which is always the same."

There is a high probability that the Chinese cycle of 60 days came from the Hindus, just as the 60-year cycle did. 

However, assuming that the cycle of 60 days was derived from the 10 *Kan* <note>the "Heavenly Stems," a Chinese system of ordinal numbers used in conjunction with the 12 "Earthly Branches" to track time</note>, the question arises: where did the Chinese get the number ten as a period of days, or indeed, for any intervals of time?

Patell, writing about the Japanese in the same work on page 37, states that they have an era composed of cycles of 10 and 12, and he remarks:

> "The words in the cycle of 10 are the names of the five elements, duplicated by taking those names in both the masculine and feminine endings: *je* and *to*."

<summary>This page explores the mathematical origins of the Chinese 60-day and 60-year cycles, comparing them to Greek, Egyptian, and Hindu systems. The author suggests these cycles likely originated from Hindu influences rather than developing independently in China or Greece.</summary>

Their elements are Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water, and they are arranged as follows:

<margin>The Japanese system of ten likely mirrors an earlier Chinese practice of doubling five basic concepts.</margin>
Thus, if the Japanese derived their "cycles of 10" from the Chinese, as is suspected, it is probable that the Chinese also originally created their cycle of 10 by duplicating five objects. The Chinese do the same thing in a different way, for they make:

| | | | | | | | |
|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|
| Kia | and | Yih | correspond | to | the | element | Wood |
| Ping | ,, | Ting | ,, | ,, | ,, | ,, | Fire |
| Wu | ,, | Ke | ,, | ,, | ,, | ,, | Earth |
| Keng | ,, | Sing | ,, | ,, | ,, | ,, | Metal |
| Sen | ,, | Kwei | ,, | ,, | ,, | ,, | Water |

This system gives *two Kans* <note>original: "Kans"; referring to the "Ten Heavenly Stems" (*Tiāngān*), a Chinese system of ordinal numbers used in chronology and astrology</note> to each of the five elements. (See Mayers <note>William Frederick Mayers (1831–1878), a British diplomat and scholar known for his work *The Chinese Reader's Manual*</note>, page 348.)

The Tartars <note>a historical term used here to refer to various Central Asian nomadic groups, including the Mongols and Manchus</note> create their cycle of 10 by duplicating five colors: Green, Greenish, Red, Reddish, Yellow, Yellowish, White, Whitish, Black, and Blackish. <margin>The 10-part cycle was also associated with the five known planets and their symbolic colors.</margin> They also derive this cycle by duplicating the five planets. Each of the five planets was associated with a specific color, and the names of these planets were used to name the days.

The Tibetans also use a similar method of duplicating five names in order to create their cycle of ten.

<margin>This doubling of objects, by assuming that they were of two sexes, was used by the Etruscans <note>an ancient civilization in Italy, predating the Roman Empire</note>. As the connection of that nation with ancient Asia seems now to be established (see Ellis’s *Asian Affinities of the Old Italians*), the practice may possibly be traced to the same Asiatic origin from which the Tartars and Chinese derived it.</margin>

George Dennis <note>British diplomat and explorer (1814–1898) known for his study of Etruscan civilization</note> gives an instance of this practice among the Etruscans in Volume 1, page 4, of <margin>Cities and Cemeteries of Etruria</margin> *Cities and Cemeteries of Etruria*. There were the twelve great gods, six of each sex, called *Dii Consentes* <note>original: "Dii Consentes"; literally the "Consenting Gods" or "Gods of the Council"</note> or *Complici* <note>original: "Complici"; the "Unified" or "Enveloping" gods</note>. They composed the council of *Tinia* <note>the supreme deity in Etruscan mythology, equivalent to the Roman Jupiter</note>.

It was also in use among the Egyptians. Seneca, in *Natural Questions*, book 3, chapter 14 <note>original: "quæst: nat: lib. iii. c. xiv."</note>, says that they had a cycle of ten elements, distinguished as male and female.

The Romans also had a cycle of 10 hours (see Hyginus, *Fable 183* <note>original: "Hyginus fabula clxxxiii." Referring to Gaius Julius Hyginus, a Roman author</note>), where he gives a special series of 10 hours and cites their names in Greek, which possibly may belong to the cycle of 10 days; they also had a cycle of 10 months which made up the year before the time of Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome, credited with reforming the Roman calendar</note>.

<margin>When, therefore, it is asserted by the Chinese that Hwang-ti <note>The "Yellow Emperor," a legendary Chinese sovereign and cultural hero</note> invented the cycles of 10 and 12, it can only really mean that these cycles are known to have descended...</margin>

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

...[des]cended from very ancient times: for looking back into the remote history of Rome, one sees that Romulus, or someone who lived in Italy at the period assigned to him, divided the year into 10 months, or lunar months. After <margin>X</margin> this, Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome</note> divided and expanded the year into 12 months, so that both the divisions of the year by 10 and 12 existed in the West long before there is historical evidence <margin>X</margin> that they existed in China. It may be reasonably asked whether the supposed invention by Huangdi <note>original: "Hwang-ti"; the legendary "Yellow Emperor" of China</note> of the cycle of 10 is not somehow connected with the decimal division of the year used by the primitive Romans; especially as it is uncertain what intervals of time the denary <note>a cycle based on the number ten</note> cycle of Huangdi was applied to, whether of days, months, or years.

The same may be said of the cycle of 12, though that period is known to have existed in ancient times among the Chaldeans <note>an ancient people of Mesopotamia known for their astronomy</note>, and Numa probably obtained it from them as far as the division of the year was concerned.

The Chinese may possibly have also derived their knowledge of it from the Chaldeans, as far as the division of the lunar year was concerned. However, this system of 12 lunar months of 30 days each, constituting a year, was among the primitive traditions of mankind from antediluvian <note>the period before the biblical Great Flood</note> times concerning the length of the year; and consequently, it preceded the Chaldeans. The Chaldeans, however, adopted it and made their <term>Sarus</term> <note>an ancient Babylonian unit of measurement; while often referring to an eclipse cycle, the author here defines it as a 10-year period of 3,600 days</note> equal to 10 years of 360 days each, or 3,600 days, while their year was 12 lunar months of 30 days each. Thus, the Chaldeans had a cycle of 10 years long before the Chinese <margin><unclear>22 7</unclear></margin>

<margin>No other nations</margin> have any claim to having used it. With regard to the question of the antiquity of the cycle, which is discussed here, it should be noted that the cyclical names of the 12-part cycle currently used are not even as ancient as Sima Qian <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien"; the "Grand Historian" of the Han Dynasty (c. 145–86 BC)</note>, but were introduced after his time, at least as far as formal chronology is concerned.

There is another circumstance connected with the Chinese cycles of 10 and 12 which may further assist in finding how they originated in China.

These cycles are called the 10 <term>Kan</term> <note>the "Celestial Stems"</note> and the 12 <term>Chi</term> <note>the "Earthly Branches"</note>. These have certain Chinese names, and it is from these that the series and naming of the cycle of 60 <note>the sexagenary cycle, used for counting years, months, and days</note> is said to be formed.

But the 12 *Chi* also have other names—specifically, the names of animals—and each one of the *Chi* is distinguished by one of these. They are the:

| 2 | Ox | 8 | Goat |
| 3 | Tiger | 9 | Monkey |
| 4 | Hare | 10 | Rooster |

| 6 | Serpent | 12 | Pig |

The same writer is of the opinion that the system was introduced at that time by Tartar <note>a historical term used to describe various nomadic Mongolic and Turkic peoples of Central and North Asia</note> immigration.”

Abel Rémusat, in his *Research on the Tartar Languages* <note>original: "*Recherches sur les langues Tartares*"</note>, pages 300–307, has the following to say on the 12 animal names of the *Chi* <note>the 'Earthly Branches' of the Chinese calendar</note>: 

“I wish to speak of the cycles of twelve animals, conceived by the Kyrgyz and now in use through nearly all of eastern Asia. The model for it has undeniably been the twelve-part <note>original: 'duodenary'</note> cycle employed by the Chinese since the highest antiquity; but the idea of substituting the names of domestic animals instead of the insignificant characters which compose it belongs to the Kyrgyz <note>original: 'Kiei-kia-sse'</note>.”

“Besides the advantage of being more easily retained in the mind, the cycle of animals also provides astrologers with new resources by attaching a symbol to each year, to each day of the cycle of sixty <note>original: '*hexacontæride*'</note>, and even to each hour of the day. This symbol is derived from the disposition—whether real or fictitious—attributed to each of the twelve animals. As regards the selection of these latter, it is difficult to say what brought it about. The ox, the hare, the horse, the sheep, the rooster <note>original: 'fowl'</note>, the dog, and the pig are animals useful to man, and one can conceive that he wished to give the...”

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

It will be noticed from the passages above that the twelve-animal cycle was used to name <note>original: "denominate"</note> the twelve hours of the day as well as the twelve years of the cycle. That it was also applied to the twelve months <note>original: "moons"</note> of the year is evident from Fortia d'Urban's *in the work previously cited* <note>original: "op: cit:" (Latin: opere citato)</note>, Volume 2, page 6. Quoting the history of China by De Mailla regarding the reign of Yao <note>a legendary ancient Chinese ruler</note>, he remarks:

> "He chose the month <note>original: "moon"</note> *Ping-yu* <note>likely referring to the Bingyin month</note>, otherwise called the month of the Tiger—the third in the order of the cycle—to be the first month of the civil year."

The twelve-animal cycle was also used to denote periods lasting many years. Father <note>original: "Pere"</note> Amiot, in a dissertation on the Three Sovereigns <note>original: "Hwangs"; the legendary Three August Ones of Chinese mythology</note> included in the fourth chapter of Premare's "preliminary discourse" <note>original: "discours preliminaire"</note> to the *Classic of History* <note>original: "Shoo-king"</note>, page 64, mentions:

> "The Heavens began their operations at the 'revolution of the Rat'; the Earth began its operations at that of the Ox; and Humanity was produced at the revolution of the Tiger."

He later explains that each of these periods lasted 10,800 years, and twelve of these make up one full revolution <note>totaling 129,600 years, a concept known as a "Great Year" in some traditions</note>. It is worth remembering here that the Egyptians divided each sign of the zodiac into nine parts, which results in 108 divisions. Given the common practice of expanding numerical periods in ancient texts, the resemblance between these Egyptian divisions and the Chinese periods is easily explained.

It should be observed that these quotations are not included here as evidence that the application of the animal cycle actually occurred during the time of Yao; or

The 28 *sieu* <term>sieu: The twenty-eight lunar mansions or "stations" of the moon in traditional Chinese astronomy</term> of the Chinese are also named after twenty-eight animals, which include the twelve animals that make up the Chinese zodiac <note>original: "animal cycle"</note>. These twenty-eight animals are placed in slightly different sequences by various writers on the subject, as can be seen in the lists provided below.

Abbé Perny <note>Paul Hubert Perny (1818–1907), a French missionary and linguist who published a significant Chinese-French dictionary</note>, in the appendix to the second volume of his dictionary (page 107), organizes the 28 *sieu* in the following order and attaches the list of planets believed to be connected with them.

| No. | Animal | Associated Planet |
|---|---|---|
| 1. | Serpent | Jupiter |
| 2. | Dragon | Venus |
| 3. | Ho <note>Identified here as a species of beaver; in Chinese astronomy, this corresponds to the "Timber Dog" or "Muzhi"</note> | Saturn |
| 4. | Hare | Sun |
| 5. | Fox | Moon |
| 6. | Tiger | Mars |
| 7. | Leopard | Mercury |
| 8. | Unicorn <note>Likely the *Qilin*, a mythical Chinese creature often equated with the unicorn in Western historical texts</note> | Jupiter |
| 9. | Ox | Venus |
| 10. | Bat | Saturn |
| 11. | Rat | Sun |
| 12. | Swallow | Moon |
| 13. | Pig | Mars |

Furthermore, it appears that there are only three planets—Jupiter, Venus, and Mars—along with the Sun, assigned to *this* series of twelve animals. Of these, the planet Jupiter is at the head of the list, similar to the position of *She-te-ki* in Sze-ma-ts'ien’s <note>Sze-ma-ts'ien: referring to Sima Qian (c. 145–86 BC), the great historian of the Han dynasty</note> list of the 12 *Chi* <term>Chi: The "Terrestrial Branches," a sequence of twelve terms used to record time and directions in East Asia</term>, where that name represents Jupiter. It should also be noted that the correspondence of Jupiter with the animal series in this list occurs only once, and that Saturn, the Moon, and Mercury do not align with the 12 animals in the list above.

Gustave Schlegel, in his work *Chinese Uranography* <note>original French: "Uranographie Chinoise"</note>, Volume 1, pages 583 and 622, provides the list of the 28 *sieu* <term>sieu: The "Lunar Mansions" or "Lodgings," sectors of the sky through which the moon passes</term> and the corresponding animals and planets in the following different order. He says he transcribed this from the work on astronomy by Siufa <note>Siufa: likely referring to Xu Fa, a Qing dynasty scholar and astronomer</note>.

# CHINESE AND CHRONOLOGY CYCLES.

| Planet | [Number in the 28] | [Sign] | Zodiac Animal | Index |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Venus | 23. | | Ram | 10 |
| Sun | 25. | | Horse | 11 |
| Mars | 27. | | Serpent | 12 |

Therefore, the Serpent is the last animal on this list instead of being the first, as it is in the list provided by Perny <note>Abbé Paul Perny (1818–1907), a French missionary and sinologist.</note>. Furthermore, the Dragon is the first animal in Schlegel’s list <note>Gustave Schlegel (1840–1903), a Dutch sinologist who specialized in Chinese astronomy.</note>. On page 563, Dr. Schlegel cites Xu Fa <note>original: "Siu-fa," a Qing dynasty scholar and astronomer</note>, who states that the dictionary *Shuowen Jiezi* <note>original: "Show-wen," a famous early 2nd-century Chinese dictionary</note> also says the hour *Si* <note>original: "Sse," the sixth of the twelve Earthly Branches, associated with the time between 9:00 AM and 11:00 AM</note> is the symbol of the serpent. This supports Perny’s ordering of the series, as *Si* is the first of the <term>Chi</term> <note>The Twelve Earthly Branches (Dizhi), used in ancient Chinese chronology to mark years, months, days, and hours</note> that corresponds to the twelve animals. This would even suggest that the Rat, though now placed first, was not at the head of the list in ancient times. Additionally, the Dragon is first in Schlegel’s list, whereas it is second in Perny’s series.

The planets are also assigned differently in Schlegel’s list. None of the twelve animals in his list correspond with Jupiter, Saturn, the Moon, or Mercury. Consequently, only two of the planets are assigned to the series in Schlegel’s list, whereas three are assigned in Perny’s list.

Both of the sequences mentioned above differ, however, from the standard arrangement of the animals provided by Mayers <note>William Frederick Mayers (1831–1878), author of the influential *Chinese Reader's Manual* (1874)</note> in the *Chinese Reader's Manual*, Part II, Number 302:

| Order | Animal | Order | Animal |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. | Rat | 7. | Horse |
| 2. | Ox | 8. | Goat |
| 3. | Tiger | 9. | Monkey |
| 4. | Hare | 10. | Cock |
| 5. | Dragon | 11. | Dog |
| 6. | Serpent | 12. | Pig |

It compares reconstructions by Perny and Schlegel against the standard order established in the 19th century by Mayers.</summary>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how the twelve animals of the Chinese zodiac relate to the twenty-eight lunar mansions, comparing the ordering systems of scholars such as Gustave Schlegel, Abbé Perny, and William Frederick Mayers.</meta>

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

In this version, the Rat stands first, whereas the Rat is the fifth in Schlegel’s list and the sixth in Perny’s list. Additionally, the sequence of the other animals in Mayer’s series <note>Likely William Frederick Mayers (1831–1878), a British diplomat and sinologist known for "The Chinese Reader's Manual."</note> proceeds in the inverse order compared to the other series mentioned. However, with the exception of these differences, all these systems agree substantially on the fact that the twelve animals of the duodenary cycle <note>the twelve-year cycle of the Chinese zodiac</note> have been methodically selected from the twenty-eight animal names of the Chinese <term>xiu</term> <note>original: "sieu"; the twenty-eight lunar mansions or constellations used in traditional Chinese astronomy.</note>. They follow each other at regular, similar intervals of two and three, except for the first two in Perny’s list; it is in this pair alone that the sole appearance of Jupiter occurs. Furthermore, if all the animals were written consecutively in a circle in the order they follow in the series of twenty-eight—starting from the Rat as the starting point—they would be found to occupy exactly the same positions as they do in the cycle commonly used by the Chinese.

The Chinese have an outer circle added to this drawing in which the twenty-eight <term>xiu</term> <note>original: "sieu"; the lunar mansions or constellations that mark the moon’s path through the sky</note> are placed around the twelve animals so that they correspond with them.

Therefore, the origin of the twelve-part cycle <note>original: "duodenary cycle"</note> of animals may be traced to a systematic connection with the twenty-eight *xiu*. Consequently, its Chinese historical origin can only

<meta>This page continues the argument that the twelve-animal zodiac in China appeared at the same time as the twenty-eight lunar mansions and explores the relationship between the animal cycle and the twelve "earthly branches" (chi).</meta>

## THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

...thus be synchronous <note>original: "synchronical"</note> with the appearance in China of the 28 <term>xiu</term> <note>original: "sieu"; the lunar mansions or constellations that mark the moon’s path through the sky</note>. It will be seen further on that several of the 12 animals also occur among the animal symbols of the 28 Indian <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>original: "Nakhsattras"; the lunar mansions used in Hindu astrology</note>.

There is also another point which is worth considering: namely <note>original: "viz"</note>, that the 12-animal cycle is positively identified with the 12 <term>chi</term> cycle <note>the "Earthly Branches," a traditional Chinese system for measuring time</note>. Consequently, not only that cycle, but even the cycle of 60 <note>the sexagenary cycle, a sixty-year mathematical loop</note> which is derived from it, will both be found to have a contemporary origin with the introduction of the 28 *xiu*.

Mayers <note>William Frederick Mayers, author of the influential "Chinese Reader’s Manual" (1874)</note>, on page 357, places the two series of names together and shows that each of the 12 *chi* belongs to one of the 12 animals, so that practically they are the same. Schlegel, in the work previously cited <note>original: "op: cit:"; referring to Gustave Schlegel’s "Uranographie Chinoise" (1875)</note> on page 559, quotes from a Chinese author in a work titled "Materials for the astronomy of the country of Buddha." <margin>Note: This refers to the traditional Chinese account of the calendar's legendary origins.</margin> “The Emperor Huangdi <note>original: "Hwang-ti"</note>, in 2697 B.C., established the 12 signs Zhou, Zi <note>original: "Tcheou, Tsze"</note>, etc., to designate the twelve months, and he made them correspond to the 12 names of animals.” The animal cycle, therefore, according to the Chinese themselves, is identical with the cycle of 12 *chi*, or the 12 months of the year.

The modern cyclical names of the 12 *chi*, as distinguished from the animal names, are:

| 3. Yin | 9. Shen <note>original: "Chin"</note> |
| 4. Mao | 10. You <note>original: "Yeou"</note> |
| 5. Chen <note>original: "Chin"</note> | 11. Xu <note>original: "Su"</note> |
| 6. Si <note>original: "Se"</note> | 12. Hai |

<margin>20</margin>
It should also be observed that while these names do not appear to be derived from the astronomical names of 
<margin>26</margin>
the 28 <term>xiu</term> <note>original: "sieu"; the 28 "lunar mansions" or constellations that mark the moon's monthly path</note>, it has not been proven whether those cyclical names were known to the Chinese earlier than the animal names of the *xiu*. Furthermore, it is not established that they were known earlier than the names of the 10 <term>kan</term> <note>the "Heavenly Stems," used in conjunction with the "Earthly Branches" to form the 60-year cycle</note>, which also have no proven connection with the 28 *xiu*. But regardless of whether this is the case, it has already been shown that the use of the cycle of the 12 <term>chi</term> <note>the "Earthly Branches"</note> as a twelve-year cycle is not proven to be an ancient practice in China.

There is also another remarkable coincidence between the Hindu <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>original: "Nakshattras"; the lunar mansions in Hindu astrology</note> and the Chinese *xiu*, as well as the animals used as symbols for the latter, from which the twelve-animal cycle is derived. Specifically, the majority of the Hindu *Nakshatras* also have animal symbols. Among them are several that correspond to those in the Chinese series of 28 and the series of 12. Guerin, in the work cited previously <note>original: "op: cit:"; likely referring to J. M. F. Guérin’s "Astronomie Indienne" (1847)</note> on pages 42–43, provides a list of these Hindu symbols. Number 1 has a horse's head, or a man with a horse's head. He calls this *Nakshatra* *Ashvini* <note>original: "oshini"</note> and derives the name from *ashva* <note>original: "osho"</note>, meaning horse (which is *asp* in the Zend language <note>an ancient Iranian language</note>).

> "Sometimes these constellations are painted in the form of men with the head of a bird, a serpent, an ox, or a dog; and at other times in the form of animals of all sorts."

Paravey, in the work cited <note>original: "op : cit :"; likely referring to Charles de Paravey’s research on the Babylonian or Chaldean origins of civilizations</note> on page 31, points out that: similar symbols of men with animal heads are formally traced in the zodiacs of Esneh and Denderah <note>famous astronomical ceilings found in ancient Egyptian temple complexes</note>. He also says that similar figures of human bodies with animal heads are to be found in a Chinese encyclopedia, quoted by Rémusat <note>Jean-Pierre Abel-Rémusat, a famous 19th-century French sinologist</note>.

It will be seen from the above that 10 of the animals mentioned in the Chinese 12-animal cycle, and 13 of those mentioned in the 28 animals of the Chinese <term>xiu</term> <note>original: "sieu"; the 28 "lunar mansions" or constellations used in Chinese astronomy</note>, are all to be found in the animal symbols of the 28 Indian <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>original: "Nakshattras"</note>. Since the 12-animal Chinese signs are derived from the 28 *xiu*, and since these latter are identified with the *Nakshatras*, it is evident that the Chinese 12-animal signs are also derived from the 28 animal and other symbols of the Hindu *Nakshatras*. This completely disposes of the assertion,

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

...that they were invented by the Kirghis <note>a Turkic ethnic group of Central Asia</note>. This was most likely a claim made by the Chinese to deflect attention from the zodiac's real origin. By tracing them to the Kirghis, who have no reliable history, the subject might be shrouded in the mystery of tradition and thus acquire the appearance of antiquity.

Dr. Schlegel, in *Uranographie Chinoise* <note>original: "Uran : Chin"; a major work on Chinese astronomy by Gustave Schlegel published in 1875</note>, page 559, while treating the cycle of twelve animals, states: "The origin of this zodiac is absolutely unknown." He adds that Klaproth (*Journal Asiatique*, volume 15, page 312) mentions that the oldest known monument representing this animal zodiac is a mirror described in the collection of antiquities called *Po-kou-thou* <note>the *Xuanhe Bogutu*, an illustrated catalog of ancient bronze vessels</note> by Hoang-hio-fang. This mirror was made in the fifth year of Kao-tsu, the founder of the Tang dynasty, in <term>622 AD</term>.

This was about the same time that Hindu astronomy and astrology were formally introduced into China.

Schlegel further states in the same place <note>original: "loc: cit:"; abbreviation of the Latin "loco citato"</note>: "The Chinese book called *The Beginning of Things* (written by Liou-hang <note>likely Liu Xiang, a Han dynasty scholar</note>) ascribes the invention of this animal cycle to Hwang-ti <note>the Yellow Emperor, a legendary ancestor and cultural hero</note>." However, a Japanese author of the book called *Materials for the Astronomy of the Country of Buddha* states that there is no proof of such an origin, and that they are no older than the reign of Emperor Ming of the Han dynasty, in <term>58 AD</term>.

Dr. Schlegel (on page 561 of the previously cited work <note>original: "op: cit:"; abbreviation of the Latin "opere citato"</note>) argues, however, in support of the antiquity of this animal cycle, that in the memoirs of Kouan-lou, mention is made of "the pro—"

"...symbols <note>original: "gnostics"</note> of the dragon and the serpent," and their corresponding signs of *Chen* and *Si* <note>original: "Tchin" and "Sse"; two of the Twelve Earthly Branches used in Chinese timekeeping</note>; and he quickly concludes from this that the animal zodiac was known in the Han and Jin <note>original: "Tsin"</note> dynasties, or the 3rd century of our era. He also asserts that they were even known during the time of the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty. He simply states: "We have indirect historical proof of the existence and use of this cycle as far back as the sixth century before our era. The book called *Researches into Origins* <note>original: "Researches of origins"; likely the *Ge Zhi Jing Yuan*, an 18th-century encyclopedia of origins</note> says that the twelve animals have already existed for a long time, but no one knows where they came from. There is no mention made of them in the classics."

These guesses by Dr. Schlegel do not agree with his own statements, nor with the fact that neither the names nor the cycle of the twelve animals had any scientific or systematic existence in ancient times, outside of the twenty-eight stellar mansions, or *xiu* <note>original: "sieu"; lunar mansions in Chinese astronomy</note>, which are represented by twenty-eight animals.

<margin>Dr. Schlegel, pages 562–563</margin>
Dr. Schlegel, on pages 562–563, admits: "that many Chinese scholars <note>original: "savants"</note> have tried to account for the origin of this zodiac, but without success." He cites an author of the Yuan <note>original: "Yuen"</note> dynasty (quoted by Xu Fa <note>original: "Siu-fa"</note>, the author of the *Tian Yuan Li Li* <note>a treatise on mathematical astronomy</note>) who says that when Zhu Xi <note>original: "Choo-hi"; a famous 12th-century Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> asked when they began and in what books their origin could be found, he received the answer that they corresponded to the twenty-eight planetary homes <note>original: "domiciles"</note>, or *xiu*, but that only the dragon and the ox were to be found there; and as for the others...

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

It is probable that the transfer of the lunar names to those of the zodiacal divisions had been invented long before the Chinese ever thought of such matters. It will therefore be necessary to inquire more closely into the origin of the system of the twenty-eight asterisms <note>groups of stars that form a pattern but are not officially recognized as one of the 88 modern constellations</note> and the corresponding division of the ecliptic <note>the circular path the sun appears to follow across the sky over the course of a year</note>.

First, regarding whether the Chinese themselves originated the system of the twenty-eight *sieu* <term>original: *sieu* (modern *xiù*); the twenty-eight mansions or stations of the moon in Chinese astronomy</term>, it is useful to relate what writers on Chinese astronomy say on this point. Dr. Schlegel, in the work cited <note>original: *op: cit:* (*opere citato*); referring to Gustave Schlegel’s *Uranographie Chinoise* (1875)</note>, page 583, writing on the astrological zodiac of the twenty-eight animals, states first that it is to be found in the work of Siu-fa <note>Xǔ Fā, a Chinese scholar of the Qing Dynasty</note>. Siu-fa attributes great antiquity to it and even claims that it is the origin of the twelve animal stars. Schlegel differs from Siu-fa regarding the actual ancient era of its origin and offers two points in support of his view: 1st, no allusion is made to this in the classics; 2nd, the group of twenty-eight animals proves evidently that it could have only been composed after the division of the principal asterisms of the ecliptic into twenty-eight planetary domiciles. He reasons that things must have existed before they were named, and that this division only dates from the Zhou <note>original: "Chow"</note> dynasty, or eleven centuries before our era <note>approximately 1100 BCE</note>. It may here be remarked that as both the asterisms used in dividing the ecliptic and the animal names given to them are imaginary, they might easily have been invented together.

Dr. Schlegel does not give any proof for his statement that the naming of the twenty-eight divisions was not made...

Dr. Burgess, in his translation and notes of the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>an ancient Indian treatise on astronomy</note>, page 201, states that Mr. Biot maintains that in 2357 B.C., the Chinese had only 24 of the 28 *sieu* <term>lunar mansions or stations</term>, or stars; and that the other four (the 8th, 14th, 21st, and 28th) were added in the time of Zhou-gong <note>original: Chow-kung; a member of the royal family of the early Zhou dynasty who played a major role in consolidating the kingdom</note> around 1100 B.C., which completed the system of twenty-eight. Mr. Biot, in *Studies on Indian and Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: *Études Sur l'astronomie Indienne et Chinoise*</note>, Paris, 1862, page 247, and in the subsequent article, *Summary of the History of Chinese Astronomy* <note>original: *Précis de l'histoire de l'astronomie Chinoise*</note>, replies to these criticisms from Dr. Burgess. "...points of the heavens—Luo-hou, Jidou, Ji, and Bo—were, according to the *Hing-yun-lou*, from a western country called Yu-szei. This Chinese author states that the capital of Yu-szei was Kang-ku <note>likely Kangju, an ancient kingdom in Central Asia</note>. Souciet <note>Etienne Souciet (1685–1744), a Jesuit scholar of Chinese science</note> believes this place is located between Kashgar and Samarkand, but about three or four degrees further north than either. He also mentions that it was from <gloss>'this place'</gloss> that the Chinese obtained the grapevine in 100 B.C." Souciet adds that this place does not appear in ancient Chinese geographies, but in those of the Ming dynasty, it is called the king-<margin>Just</margin>dom of the Yous, or the country of the Uzbeks <note>original: Yues-begs</note>.

It will have been noticed that among the four points of the heavens which the Chinese learned from western teachers during the Tang dynasty, there is the *Ji* <note>original: Ki</note>. This is described by Souciet in his cited work <term>in the work cited <note>original Latin: op : cit :</note> Volume II, page 122</term> as being a cycle of 28 years. He adds "that it was used for intercalations" <term>the insertion of extra days or months into a calendar to keep it aligned with the solar year</term>. Souciet states that this word *Ji* and the names of the other three points of the heavens are taken from the language of a western country. The Chinese astronomer Hing-yuen-lou asserts that it forms part of the rule or the law of the *Polomen*; a footnote on this page in Souciet's book explains that "<term>Polomen</term> means Brahmins <note>the priestly class of Hindu society</note>."

It appears, therefore, that as far as the Chinese were involved in the origin of the 28-year cycle, they derived their knowledge of it from the Hindus around 622 A.D. Since this cycle is identical to the 28 Chinese *xiu* <term>lunar mansions or stations</term> <note>original: sieu</note>, it follows that the 28 *xiu* in China date from the same period—unless Souciet's statement is held to

Souciet further states that Li Chunfeng <note>original: Li-tchun-fong</note>, who was the mathematician for Emperor Taizong <note>original: Tai tsong</note>, the second emperor of the Tang dynasty in 629 AD, learned the four points of the heavens already mentioned (including the *Ki* <note>original: Ki</note> or 28-year cycle) from western strangers. Kutan was a stranger from a western kingdom called *Tien tchow* <note>original: Tien tchow; Tianzhu, the ancient Chinese name for India</note>, which the Chinese say is India, but which Souciet suggests may be Syria.

This further corroborates the Hindu <note>original: Hindoo</note> origin of the 28-year cycle among the Chinese. At present, however, there is no evidence beyond the testimony of the Chinese themselves that the Hindus used a cycle of 28 years, although like the Chinese, they had a system of 28 asterisms <term>asterisms <note>groups of stars that form a pattern smaller than a constellation</note></term> for dividing the heavens.

<margin>There might possibly have been a cycle of 28 years among the Bactrians or Chaldeans, as it is certainly an ancient, real astronomical period.</margin>

It may, however, be considered established that the 28-year cycle came to the knowledge of the Chinese through the *Kieou-tche*, and that the 28 animal names of the 28 asterisms are identified with it.

Father <note>original: Pere</note> Souciet further states in the work cited on page 126:—that one of the uses for the 28 characters—

> “...came from the same source. A Chinese scholar assured me that the custom of giving the name of one of the 28 constellations to each day was introduced into China through the *Jiuzhi* <note>original: Kieou tche; refers to the "Jiuzhi li," a Chinese translation of an Indian astronomical treatise</note> (the title of the book in which the 28-year cycle is mentioned).”

Souciet adds:—

> “I have not found this stated in any book, and I do not know whether the scholar was right. He did not know where he had learned it.”

The statement of the scholar, doubtless, represented a current tradition on the subject among the learned Chinese at his time. Even supposing that it was only his own original view of the case, it would still appear—from the aversion that Chinese literary men have toward innovations—that this scholar did not think it contradicted the received view on this point. It also suggests there was no general, certain belief among Chinese scholars at the time he spoke to Father Souciet that the system of the 28 names of the asterisms <term>asterisms <note>groups of stars that form a pattern smaller than a constellation</note></term> was earlier than the time of the Song <note>original: Sung</note> dynasty. It may also be remarked that Souciet certainly had no proof that the scholar was wrong, or he would have stated that he was.

Mayers, in the *Chinese Reader’s Manual* <note>original: C.R.M.</note> page 359, states:—

> “There can be little doubt that the practice of marking ‘the days of the sun’ has crept into Chinese chronology from a western quarter.”
