<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Chinese astronomical cycles, specifically the 28-year solar cycle and its potential links to Indian astronomy and the movement of the planet Saturn.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES. #

"Ki (or Khi, as he writes it) is an Indian name," and he calls it "*an invisible planet which represents our solar cycle of 28 Julian years;*" and he cites a Chinese author, who states:—

> "The Khi is produced by the intercalary <term>intercalary <note>a month or day inserted into a calendar to make it follow the solar year; a leap month</note></term> moon. In 28 years, there are 10 intercalary moons, and during this time, the Khi has made one revolution around the heavens."

This is the first recorded instance of the Chinese acknowledging a 28-year cycle.

Possibly this may refer to the planet Saturn; for Schlegel <note>Gustave Schlegel (1840–1903), a Dutch sinologist and author of "Uranographie Chinoise"</note> (pages 628, 629) states:—

> "According to the Chinese, this planet advances one degree in 28 days, and one sign of the zodiac in 28 months, so that it achieves its revolution in the heavens in 28 years."

As Schlegel observes, there is an inaccuracy in this Chinese observation, as Saturn actually performs this revolution in 28 years and 166 days. Nevertheless, it clearly shows that the Chinese possessed a cycle of 28 years, which they mistakenly applied to Saturn. These appear to be the only two instances in which they used it scientifically. 

However, in practice, they named their days in four series of seven days each—with each day under the influence of a planet—thereby adopting the method of using a seven-day week. They did this without apparently knowing that this naming system <note>original: nomenclature</note> of the days is regulated by a cycle of 28 years, which causes the sequence of days to recur in the same order at the end of that period.

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origin of the 28-year cycle and the seven-day week in China, comparing their practices with Babylonian and European systems, and questioning the antiquity of their solar year calculations.</meta>

<page-num>225</page-num>

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES. #

<margin>/</margin> The seven-day week, in which each day is under the influence of a planet, was used in Babylon at least as early as 1700 B.C. However, it must be noted that this was not a chronological epoch <note>original: epoch; here meaning a fixed point in time used as a reference for a calendar system</note>. It was merely a system used to regulate the 1st, 8th, 15th, 21st, and 28th days of each month in this planetary order. They did not specifically or similarly track the remaining days of the month between the 28th day and the 1st day of the next month. In contrast, the Chinese continue their series of days throughout the entire year without interruption, just as European nations do.

<margin>x</margin> There is no evidence that the Chinese developed this system through scientific inquiry, and it is not even certain that they used a year of 365 and a quarter days—which is the basis of the 28-year cycle—until recent times. Souciet <note>Jean-Baptiste Souciet (1685–1744), a Jesuit scholar who published works on Chinese astronomy</note>, in Volume III, page 6, says of the astronomy of the Han dynasty:

<margin>x</margin>> “The year was divided into 365 days and a quarter of a day,”

<margin>r</margin> which would lead one to conclude that this specific division of the year was only known in China starting from that era. However, there are others who maintain that the Chinese knew of this solar year much earlier, relying on the famous passage in the *Shujing* <note>original: Shoo king; one of the Five Classics of ancient Chinese literature, also known as the Classic of History</note>, where the Emperor Yao said:

<margin>x</margin>> “An entire year consists of three hundred and sixty-six days.”

Whatever the case may be regarding this point, it does not prove that the Chinese possessed a contemporary scientific knowledge of other calculations pertaining to a solar year at the supposed time of Emperor Yao. Mr. John Reeves <note>John Reeves (1774–1856), an English naturalist and tea inspector in China</note> remarks in his appendix to Morrison’s dictionary <note>Robert Morrison (1782–1834), who compiled the first major Chinese-English dictionary</note>, Volume I, part II, page 1063: “To our surprise, we find that the Chinese know...”

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the origin of the 28-year cycle in China, specifically questioning whether the Chinese possessed the scientific astronomical knowledge required to develop such a system or if they merely adopted its practical elements from elsewhere.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES. ###

> "...little or nothing about astronomical science. The Chinese have been described as having arts, but no science; and the more we are enabled by an increasing knowledge of their language to examine their literary works with our own eyes, the better we shall appreciate the accuracy of this description of them."

This would explain why the Chinese had a practical knowledge <note>original: "material knowledge"; referring to knowledge of the physical existence of the cycle rather than the theoretical science behind it</note> of the existence of the 28-year cycle without having the science to understand its application for a practical purpose, as other nations have done.

<margin>x</margin>
In any case, a scientific understanding of the systems of 28 years, or the 28 asterisms <note>asterisms: small patterns or groups of stars, often used as landmarks in the night sky</note>, could not be very ancient in China. Souciet <note>Jean-Baptiste Souciet (1685–1744), a Jesuit scholar of Chinese history</note>, in Volume III, page 2, states: "The astronomical system organized by the Emperor Kangxi <note>original: Kang-hi; the Qing emperor who reigned 1661–1722 and promoted Western science</note> and published a few years earlier, asserts that at the time of Qin Shi Huang <note>original: Tsin-chi hwang-ti; the first emperor of a unified China (reigned 221–210 BCE), known for ordering the 'burning of books'</note>, the Chinese had lost the method taught by the ancients for calculating the positions of the seven planets." Of course, Emperor Kangxi takes it for granted that prior to Qin Shi Huang, the Chinese had once possessed the method referred to.

<margin>x</margin>
This passage from Souciet is, however, remarkable because the system of the 28 asterisms is founded—according to Schlegel in the work cited <note>original: "Schlegel, op: cit:"; referring to Gustave Schlegel (1840–1903), a Dutch sinologist</note> and other supporters of the antiquity of the 28 asterisms among the Chinese—on the application of the seven planets to each of the four seasons of the year. If the calculations of these seven planets were not known, then the system of 28 asterisms in connection with them, as in Schlegel’s hypothesis, could not have been known either. At page 9, he continues: “They had no knowledge of the mathematical equations for the Sun, the Moon, and the planets.”

They did, however, know about the 28 constellations; for Souciet remarks here: “Besides the position of the sun in relation to the 24 *Jieqi* <note>original: Tsie-ki; the twenty-four solar terms of the Chinese agricultural calendar</note> or to the 28 constellations, Liu Xin and Luo Xiahong <note>original: Lo-hia-hong</note> referred it to the twelve divisions of the equator called *Ci* <note>original: Tse; the twelve stations of the Chinese zodiac</note>.”

There is thus no evidence in the Han astronomical books mentioned above regarding the local origin or antiquity of the system of 28 asterisms <note>groups of stars, specifically the "Lunar Mansions" used to track the moon's path</note> among the Chinese. He notes that they did not even understand what they wrote concerning the cycle of 19 years, and that they proposed the *Chang yuen* <note>The "Superior Epoch," a massive span of time used as a starting point for astronomical calculations</note> instead of a scientific epoch for the movements of the heavenly bodies. On page 18, he adds: "it is not at all certain that the *Chang yuen* was known before the burning of the books <note>The 213 BCE decree by the first emperor, Qin Shi Huang, to destroy historical and philosophical texts</note>."

<margin>It may be observed that there is no numerical relationship between the cycle of 28 years and the *Pou* <note>a period of 76 years</note> of 76 years, or the *Ki* of 1,520 years (equal to 20 *Pou*), or the *Yuen* of 4,560 years (equal to 60 *Pou* or 76 cycles of 60 years each), which the Chinese learned from the Indians <note>original: Hindoos</note>. Souciet states:
> "The numbers of heaven (1, 3, 5, 7, 9, totaling 25), the numbers of earth (2, 4, 6, 8, 10, totaling 30), and the expansion of the eight trigrams <term>Kwa</term> <note>The eight basic symbols used in Chinese divination and cosmology</note>, provided the numbers for the eight great solar terms <term>Tsie-ki</term> <note>original: Tsie-ki; these are divisions of the solar year</note>, the 24 solar terms, the 28 constellations, the 19-year cycle, and other things. As they were not required to provide any reasons for the facts they presented, there was no kind of combination they did not create through addition, subtraction, division, and multiplication. All of this was done to discover certain eras relative to the 60-year cycle. Furthermore, most Han dynasty astronomers believed that calculating the movements of the stars was not based on unchangeable rules."

Therefore, there could not have been any scientific system among the Chinese at that time regarding the movement of the planets through the 28 mansions—which later astronomers claim gave rise to the system of the lunar mansions <term>sieu</term> <note>original: sieu; the 28 divisions of the sky in Chinese astronomy</note>.

Despite all this, it may be admitted that the Chinese probably possessed an incomplete traditional knowledge of 28 constellations, by which certain movements of celestial bodies were tracked long before they became acquainted with them through the Hindus <note>original: Hindoos</note>. It is known that the 28 asterisms <term>asterisms</term> <note>groups of stars that form a pattern</note> were anciently part of the Indian astronomical system, or at least the astrological system. Although besides the 28 asterisms or mansions, there was another system of 27 asterisms connected with it. However, it is difficult to understand how the

The text continues at page 49: "He further made an immense sphere, with the earth in the middle and the 28 constellations appearing in the sphere. His marking of the sun's position at the winter solstice in the constellation *Di* <margin>X</margin> <note>original: Teou; one of the 28 lunar mansions, often called the 'Ladle'</note> shows that they were well known to him and that they had some reference to the sun."

Even the monk Yi Xing <note>original: Y-hang, a famous 8th-century Buddhist astronomer</note>, during the reign of the Tang Emperor Xuanzong <note>original: Hieun-tsong</note> in 721 AD, derived the 28 constellations and the 24 solar terms <term>Jieqi</term> <note>original: Tsie-ki; the 24 divisions of the solar year</note> from the mysterious number 50, or the *Fayan* <note>original: Fa-yen; likely referring to the 'Standard of Law' or numerological systems derived from the I-Ching</note>, or 49. This suggests that even at that relatively recent date, there was no scientific basis among the Chinese for the 28 mansions, nor for the 28-year cycle connected with them. Regarding the date at which the Chinese began to scientifically understand the 28 mansions <term>xiu</term> <note>original: sieu</note> or the 28-year cycle, it would be difficult to place the beginning of their systematic acquaintance with it any further back than approximately the start of the Christian era.

Based on what has been stated, it would appear that the Chinese derived their primary knowledge of the 28 mansions—and consequently the other cycles that depend on or are derived from them—through the Indians <note>original: Hindoos</note>. However, it is worth noting that the Indian and Chinese systems regarding these 28 mansions are not completely identical. Moreover, there is a probability that both the Indians and the <margin>X</margin> Chinese derived their knowledge of the entire subject from some other common source in more ancient times.

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

<margin>X</margin>
The primary Hindu <note>original: Hindoo</note> system of the lunar mansions <term>nakshatras</term> <note>sectors of the sky along the moon's path</note> consisted of only 27; whereas the Chinese, from the time they are known to have theorized on the mansions, have always had 28. This arrangement was purely astronomical. Ebenezer Burgess, in his translation of the *Surya Siddhanta* <term>Surya Siddhanta</term> <note>a foundational Sanskrit text on Indian astronomy</note>, page 91, writes:

<margin>X</margin>
> "The ecliptic is divided into 27 lunar mansions, or asterisms <note>identifiable groups of stars</note>, of equal amount; hence the portion of the ecliptic occupied by each asterism is 13° 20' or 800'."

This division of the ecliptic into 27 equal parts is reasonable, as it gives an easily defined space for each asterism; whereas if the ecliptic is divided into 28 parts, each of them would occupy 12° 51 3/7', which is a most unscientific division.

Henry Thomas Colebrooke, in his *Essays* (Volume II, pages 362–363), says that the use of the 28 *Yogas* <term>Yogas</term> <note>astrological calculations based on the positions of the sun and moon</note> is chiefly astrological among the Hindus, and has the same use among the Chinese. He adds that the *Surya Siddhanta* presents no trace of these 28 *Yogas*.

Burgess, in the work cited above (pages 177 and 179), writes:
> "the text nowhere expressly states which one of the twenty-eight asterisms it recognizes is left without a portion in its division of the ecliptic into only 27 portions."

So it appears that, in reality, Hindu astronomy admitted both the 27 and 28-mansion systems.

Mr. Jean-Baptiste Biot, in the *Journal des Savants* <note>Journal of Scientists</note> for 1840, endeavored to prove the identity of the Hindu and Chinese 28-part division of the ecliptic. However, there is a major stumbling block in the way of such a demonstration: the Chinese division is equatorial <note>based on the celestial equator</note>, and that of the

<summary>This page compares the Hindu system of 27 lunar mansions with the Chinese system of 28, noting that while the 27-part division is more mathematically practical for the ecliptic, both cultures utilized 28 mansions for astrological purposes. It highlights a key scientific difference: the Chinese system is based on the equator rather than the ecliptic.</summary>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

[The author argues that the] Hindu <note>original: Hindoo</note> system is zodiacal; and moreover, that the Chinese mansions consist of single stars only, while the Hindu system is founded on constellations, or groups of several stars.

Burgess, in the work cited above <note>original: op. cit.</note> on page 200, provides a table of correspondences between the Chinese, Hindu, and Arabian systems of the 28 asterisms <term>asterisms</term> <note>identifiable groups of stars smaller than a constellation</note>. He shows that, with the exception of the *first five* asterisms in the Hindu list, these three systems do not agree on the portions or divisions of the ecliptic <term>ecliptic</term> <note>the path the sun appears to follow across the sky</note> assigned to the asterisms by the three systems.

He also notes that for another 12 asterisms, the Hindu and Chinese systems only agree that they exist within the ecliptic, but not on the specific portions they occupy. Consequently, only 17 out of the 28 asterisms are found to exist in both systems.

In addition, he remarks that—excluding the *first five* asterisms mentioned above—there are only 13 other asterisms out of the 28 found in both the Arabian and Chinese systems. This means that, in total, only 18 out of the 28 asterisms are shared by these two systems.

Burgess adds on page 201:
> "After this exhibition of the concordances <note>original: concordances; agreements or harmonies</note>"—he might also have said discrepancies—"existing among the three systems—Hindu, Chinese, and Arabian—it is, we believe <note>original: apprehend</note>, impossible for anyone to doubt that they all share a common origin and are merely different forms of one and the same system."

<margin>X</margin>

In the more recent portions of the Vedic texts, such as in the 19th book of the *Atharva Veda* and in parts of the *Yajur Veda*, full lists of the asterisms <term>asterisms</term> <note>distinct groups or patterns of stars</note> are found; however, the deities who rule over these various asterisms are all drawn from the *Vedic Pantheon* <term>Vedic Pantheon</term> <note>the assembly of gods found in the ancient sacred Hindu texts known as the Vedas</note>. The popular deities of later times are not found among them. Both the Hindu and Arabian systems have their own specific points of agreement with the Chinese *sieu* <term>sieu</term> <note>the Chinese term for lunar mansions</note> which the other system does not share. Neither of them could have been derived through the other from a Chinese original. Since the Hindu and Arabian systems are composed of constellations <term>constellations</term> <note>groups of stars</note> and the Chinese system is composed of single stars, neither of the first two could have come independently of the other from a Chinese source."

states:—

> "The question of originality can only lie between the Chinese and the fourth system, from which the <margin>x</margin> other two have together descended. It looks more as if the series of the Chinese *sieu*, or single stars, were the original, than as if the asterisms <note>groups of stars forming a pattern</note> of the other systems had been independently selected from the groups of stars situated along the zodiac with the intention of forming a zodiacal system. The single determinations of the *sieu* would have become the nuclei of the constellations of the other systems."

This might possibly be true in theory, but to sustain the hypothesis on which it depends, it would have to be proved that the Chinese had this single star system even as late as the asterism system is known to have existed in India. Of this, there is no reliable evidence. Moreover, the constellations are certainly more striking to a stargazer than the stars of the third magnitude which figure among the Chinese *sieu*; they also present greater facility and extension for watching the movements of the planets through the heavens. It should always be remembered, when examining this question, that the Chinese single stars *divided the equator*, and the Hindu and other asterisms *divided the ecliptic* <note>the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note>, which absolutely forbids the Chinese

Furthermore, the division of the ecliptic <term>ecliptic</term><note>the apparent path of the Sun across the sky over the course of a year</note> into 28 portions is considered unscientific based on what is currently known. At the very least, there is no logical explanation for why such a strange division would be used. In contrast, a 27-fold division is so symmetrical that it is generally preferred.

R. A. Proctor <note>Richard Anthony Proctor (1837–1888), a popular English astronomer</note> in the *Contemporary Review* <note>original: C. R.</note> of June 1879, states:
> "In itself <note>original: *Per se*</note>, there is no easily understood way to divide the heavenly space into 28 portions. In each of these portions, there is no striking star or constellation that is equally distant from its neighbors; nor does a 28-part division naturally suggest itself simply by the appearance of the heavens and the stars."

And yet Dr. Burgess states on page 206:
> "To adopt a series of prominent constellations along the zodiac, and to use their proximity to track the movements of the planets, is a logical step <note>original: no unmotivated proceeding</note>."

Therefore, if the ancient Hindu <note>original: Hindoo</note> system had been scientific, it must have been so in a way that modern research has not yet discovered.

On this subject, Dr. Burgess very appropriately remarks on page 180:
> "While it might have seemed that the system was founded on a division of the ecliptic into 28 equal portions—with a star or constellation selected to mark and 'rule' each portion—it now appears that the series of 28 asterisms <term>asterisms</term><note>groups of stars that form a recognized pattern but are smaller than a full constellation</note> may be something independent of, and older than <note>original: anterior</note>, any division of the ecliptic into equal parts. It seems the two systems may have been only artificially brought into..."

> "The moon measures time in an obvious and striking way, and to ordinary observers, it appears to do so with perfect uniformity. By watching the moon's progress along its path, observers would notice and determine the length of these cycles to be between 27 and 28 days. While observing the moon's movement among the stars during a single lunation <term>lunation</term><note>the period of time between one new moon and the next, approximately 29.5 days</note>, an observer—unless being very careful—would see nothing to suggest that it is traveling around at a rate faster than one complete circuit every 28 days.

> <margin>x</margin>"Its path always lies within a certain zone, which would undoubtedly have been given a special name. This was, in reality, the middle zone of the current zodiac. The lunar zodiac follows the sun's path across the heavens. Recognizing the moon's zodiac would have happened long before determining the sun's path among the stars.
> 
> <margin>x</margin>"The Babylonians initially divided the zodiac into 28 parts. That is, the parts of the zodiac that the moon travels through day by day were replaced by..."

The claim of the Chinese to have originated, or even known in antiquity, the 27 mansions <term>mansions</term><note>lunar mansions are segments of the sky along the ecliptic through which the moon passes during its monthly cycle</note> cannot be substantiated, given all the considerations mentioned above. Furthermore, all of their chronology founded on this system and its development is equally unreliable.

<margin>x</margin>
It appears, however, that the 28-mansion system must have been the original division of the ecliptic <term>ecliptic</term><note>the sun's apparent path across the sky over the course of a year</note>. The fact that the Chinese used it only shows that they derived their knowledge of it from the same source as the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoo"</note>, the Persians, the Copts <note>an ethno-religious group from Egypt</note>, and the Arabs. This is no proof that they inherited it directly from the first inventors by a regular national succession. Regardless of when they became acquainted with it in their history, they did so only in an unscientific and incomplete way. It was quite inadequate for building the chronological system they have adopted, and for which they have tried to claim a doubtful antiquity.

There is one circumstance connected with the Chinese arrangement of the 28 constellations that is especially interesting. This is mentioned by Dr. Chalmers <note>John Chalmers (1825–1899), a Scottish missionary and scholar of Chinese astronomy</note> in his essay quoted above, and he notes that the Chinese have left it without explanation.

It is that the order of the 28 constellations proceeds from west to east, instead of arranging them from east...

The next thing to be considered is the series of 12 animals and their equivalents. As we have seen, this series is closely connected to the 28 *sieu* <term>lunar mansions</term><note>original: "sieu"; the 28 constellations of the Chinese zodiac</note> and is even derived from them.

First of all, it may be observed that there is a coincidence between the practices of the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note> and the Chinese regarding this. The Hindus derived the names of the 12 months of their solar year from the names of their 28 *Nakshattras* <term>lunar houses</term><note>original: "Nakshattras"; the Hindu system of lunar mansions used to track the moon's path</note>. They selected them from these in a way similar to how the Chinese have chosen their 12 animal months.

> Burgess, in the work previously cited <note>original: "op: cit:"; likely Ebenezer Burgess's 1860 translation of the Surya Siddhanta</note>, page 30, states:—
> “The names of the solar months are derived from the names of the asterisms in which the moon was full at the time they were first designated. The same names are transferred to the lunar months;” and on page 268, Chapter <note>original: "Cap :"</note> 14, paragraph 15 of the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>an ancient Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy</note>:—
> “The months are to be identified by the names of the asterisms (*Nakshattras*) according to 
> <margin>x</margin> 
> the alignment <term>conjunction</term> at the end of a lunar period.” Paragraph 16 states:—“To the months Karttika <note>one of the months in the Hindu calendar</note>, etc., the asterisms of the same name belong, paired *two by two* in their alignment; but three months—namely the last, the second-to-last, and the fifth—have three asterisms each.”

Before further comparing the Hindu <note>original: "Hindoo"</note> method for assigning *Nakshattras* <term>lunar mansions</term> to months with what the Chinese do, it is important to notice that *Karttika* (the first Hindu month) signifies Jupiter. This is much like the first Chinese *chi* <term>terrestrial branch</term> and the first Chinese month, which also have Jupiter as their symbol or ruling planet. It may be that Sima Qian's <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'ien," a Han dynasty historian</note> name for the first of the *chi*, "Chiti-ko," is a corruption <note>a linguistic variant or mispronunciation</note> of *Karttika*, or is connected to it etymologically.

<margin>x</margin>
The comparison to be noticed is that the assignment of so many of the *Nakshattras* to the Hindu months is the same as the relationship of the *sieu* <term>lunar mansions</term> to the 12 animal names given by the Chinese. The ox, which is the fifth animal in the series according to Perny <note>Paul Perny (1818–1907), a French missionary and sinologist</note>, corresponds to the fifth month in the Hindu series. It has three of the *sieu* assigned to it—the 7th, 8th, and 9th—just as the fifth Hindu month has three of the *Nakshattras*.

The sheep, which is the second-to-last of the Chinese animal series, has three *sieu* assigned to it (the 21st, 22nd, and 23rd). The horse, which is the last in Perny's series, also has three of the *sieu* belonging to it (the 25th, 26th, and 27th). This is exactly the case with the last and the second-to-last of the Hindu months.

In this comparison, it will be noticed that the distribution of the *Nakshattras* among the Hindu months, and that of the *sieu* among the Chinese animal months, is based on there being only 27 symbols in each series instead of 28. In the Hindu series...

In the Hindu system, it is the 22nd <term>Nakshattra</term> <note>lunar mansion</note>, and in the Chinese series, it is the 24th of the <term>sieu</term> <note>lunar mansion</note> that is excluded from the calculation. Guérin, a 19th-century French author of works on Indian astronomy</note>, in the work cited <note>original: "op : cit :" (opere citato)</note> on page 31, says:

It also proves that the practice of naming the Hindu months and the Chinese animal months from the *Nakshattras* and *sieu* in this manner must have been a recent development.

<margin>x</margin>
A similarity such as the one just pointed out cannot be explained away as a mere coincidence. The methodical way in which the Chinese selected the 12 animal month names from the names of the *sieu* is exactly similar to the Hindu method of naming the months from their *Nakshattras*. There is, therefore, every reason to conclude that the 12-animal cycle is certainly not of an earlier date in China than the date of the 28 *sieu* from which they were taken. Furthermore, since the Chinese admit their ignorance regarding the original introduction of the 12-animal cycle, they thereby admit that the date of their first acquaintance with the 28 *sieu* (or 28-year cycle) is equally uncertain.

We can hardly suppose that they were not originally applied independently to the lunar months; and certainly no more suitable derivation could be found for the name of a lunar period than from the asterism in which the moon attained her full beauty and perfection during its course.”

It will be observed that in the preceding lists, there are only 27 Nakshatras <note>lunar mansions in Hindu astrology</note> and 27 *sieu* <note>Chinese lunar mansions</note> mentioned. The Hindus omitted Abhijit, the 22nd of the 28 Nakshatras, when they reduced the number by one; similarly, the Chinese system is calculated with only 27 <margin>X</margin> *sieu*, or rather, the 14th *sieu* is omitted here to create a parallel between it and the Hindu system.

The main point presented and emphasized here to illustrate the comparison is that the first Hindu month, Karttika, represents Jupiter. Similarly, the first Chinese *che* <note>terrestrial branch</note> and the first name of the 12-animal cycle also represent the <margin>X</margin> serpent and Jupiter. The serpent is assigned the first place in the Chinese series, just as Karttika is assigned the first place by the Hindus; as Burgess states in the previously cited work <note>original: "op: cit:"; Latin: "opere citato"</note>:

> "We may also regard the rank assigned to Karttika as due to the ancient position of Karttika as the first among the lunar mansions."

Warren, in the previously cited work <note>original: "op: cit:"</note> on page 213, after mentioning that Karttika and others are the names of the twelve years of Jupiter's cycle, says: "It may be remarked that in the previous arrangement, Karttika <note>original: "Cartic"</note> is placed first in the cycle of 12." This would almost identify Karttika with *Shi-ti-ko* <note>Sheti'ge, the first year of the Jupiter cycle in ancient Chinese records</note>, <margin>X</margin> which is the first name in Sima Qian's <note>original: "Sze-ma-ts'iens"; Sima Qian was a foundational Chinese historian of the Han dynasty</note> cycle. This indicates that if this is the case, the *Shi-ti-ko* cycle was a twelve-year cycle in ancient times.

It may also be remarked that the first name of the 28 *sieu* in the ordinary naming of the series, <margin>X</margin> "Kio" <note>the "Horn" mansion</note>, also corresponds with Jupiter. This confirms the position of the serpent, which is identical

...with Jupiter, as first in the series as exhibited by Perny.

It will also be observed that the 27th and 28th *xiu* <note>original: "sieu"; Chinese lunar mansions</note> in Schlegel's arrangement are placed here as the 1st and 2nd in the comparative list of 27. This is just as the 27th and 28th Hindu Nakshatras <note>lunar mansions in Hindu astrology</note>, Ashvini <note>original: "Acvini"</note> and Bharani, in Burgess's list are sometimes placed as the 1st and 2nd of the Hindu series, as can be seen in Burgess's previously cited work <note>original: "op: cit:"; Latin: "opere citato"</note> on page 211. It is also evident from this identification of the Hindu months with the 12 cyclical animals that the 12-part cycle was originally a cycle of months rather than a cycle of years.

The comparisons made between the derivation of the Hindu names of the months and the names of the 12-animal cycle (from the Nakshatras and the *xiu* <note>original: "sieu"</note>, respectively) support the previously mentioned analogy between them. These comparisons must not, however, be pushed too far as a scientific argument. Although the Chinese derived their knowledge of the 28 asterisms <note>groups of stars</note> and their significance from the Hindus, they adopted them in a clumsy, unscientific manner; consequently, the Chinese system does not correspond entirely with the Hindu system. Guérin, in his previously cited work <note>original: "op: cit:"; Latin: "opere citato"</note> on page 164, points out a major difference between the order of the 28 mansions in China and India regarding the stars included in them. Specifically, the first Chinese *xiu*, "Kio" <note>Jiǎo, or the "Horn" mansion</note>, is identified with the star Spica in Virgo <note>original: "Spica Virginis"; Latin: "the ear of grain of the Virgin"</note>. In contrast, the first Nakshatra of the Hindus, Ashvini <note>original: "Acvini"</note>, is identified with the constellation Aries <note>the Ram</note>.

<summary>This page argues that the Chinese 12-animal cycle originated as a cycle of months based on Hindu astronomical models, while noting significant differences in how each culture identifies the starting points of their lunar mansions.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Chinese cycles, 12-animal cycle, Nakshatras, xiu, lunar mansions, Hindu-Chinese relations, Spica Virginis, Aries, Burgess, Guérin</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a technical analysis of the relationship between Hindu and Chinese astronomical systems, specifically debating the origins and dating of the lunar mansions (xiu/Nakshatras) based on the positions of stars like Spica and the constellation Aries.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

The Chinese thus seem to have started their list from the star Spica, without following the Hindus in this regard, and seemingly without knowing why they did so.

<margin>✓</margin>
Now, as Guérin <note>Monsieur Guérin, a 19th-century French scholar of ancient astronomy</note> remarks in the place cited <note>original: "loc: cit:"; Latin: "loco citato"</note>: “Moyo, the author of the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>an ancient and highly influential Indian treatise on astronomy</note>, must have observed Ashvini <note>original: "Acvini"; the first Hindu lunar mansion</note> or *Aries* on the night of the vernal equinox in 345 AD, 180° distant from that equinoctial point, and he then placed the other *Nakshattras* <note>Hindu lunar mansions</note> in order, by starting from that point.” Burgess, in the work cited <note>original: "op: cit:"; Latin: "opere citato"</note> on page 14, mentions that it coincided in position with the vernal equinox around 570 AD.

<margin>X ✓</margin>
Therefore, since the Chinese *xiu* <note>original: "sieu"; lunar mansion</note> Jiao <note>original: "Kio"</note> is the star Spica in the constellation Virgo, and is also 180° after the *xiu* Lou <note>original: "Leou"</note> (which is the mansion corresponding to Ashvini or Aries), the Chinese books that designated Jiao as their first lunar mansion must have been written after Moyo’s time. It would follow that the entire Chinese system of 28 *xiu*, upon which all their chronology seems to depend, also dates to this same recent period.

<margin>✓</margin>
It would be interesting to discover why the Chinese placed Jiao, or Spica, as the first on their list of *xiu*, as it does not align with the Chinese theory of the year beginning at the winter solstice. The name Jiao, by which the Chinese call their first *xiu*, means “a horn”; interestingly, the Arabian, Persian, and Indian series also begin with a “horn”—specifically that of Aries, the ram, which marks the first Hindu *Nakshattra*.

It may be suggested as an answer to this inquiry that since the Indian name for Aries is *Krio*, which is taken from the Greek <note>original: "Krio"; from the Greek "Krios," meaning ram</note>; and as the Chinese cannot pronounce—

<insert>
Why should they take from the Greek if it is derived from Chaldean or Persian?

...they dropped the letter R and called the word *Jiao* <note>original: "Kio"</note>. If this is the case, the ancient status of the 28 Chinese lunar mansions <note>original: "sieu"</note> and their logical consequences may be nothing more than a modern invention.

Fortia d’Urban, in the work cited <note>original: "op: cit:"; Latin: "opere citato"</note>, volume 2, page 69, remarks on this subject:
> “With the exception of the Chinese, all [other cultures] have taken the same stars for the starting point of the division of the 28 constellations; namely, the head of Aries. The Chinese, on the contrary, have fixed the starting point in the part of the heavens diametrically opposed to this, toward the feet of Virgo and near the star Spica.” <margin>✓</margin>

In explanation of this, Fortia d’Urban suggests that this method of arranging the 28 lunar mansions was probably caused by the Chinese having taken the *new moon* as the starting point, while the Hindus and others took the *full moon*; or perhaps it was because the Chinese began their arrangement at a different, opposite solstice from the one used by the Hindus.

If this were accepted, it would certainly reveal the modern origin of the Chinese acquaintance with the 28 lunar mansions. In adopting the method suggested above, they could not have been guided by their ancient tradition regarding the start of the month, according to Fortia d’Urban himself, as he mentions in volume 2, page 75:
<margin>X</margin>
> “The Chinese also originally regulated their calendars by the full moons;”

And it is evident that the Hindus did the same, based on what Burgess mentions in the work cited on page 269:
> “According to Sir William Jones in *Asiatic Researches* <note>original: "As : Res :"; a 19th-century journal of oriental studies</note>, volume 2, page 296, it is asserted by the Hindus that when their lunar year...”

“...was arranged by former astronomers, *the moon was at the full on the very day* when it entered the Nakshatra <note>the lunar mansions of Hindu astrology</note> from which that month is named”; though Burgess <note>Ebenezer Burgess (1805–1870), an American missionary and translator of Indian astronomical texts</note> adds: “whether this assertion is strictly true is very doubtful.” It is clear, therefore, that in ancient times the Chinese did not differ from the Hindus on this point.

Regarding the beginning of the lunar mansions <note>original: "sieu"</note> in relation to the solstices, it is much more likely to be the case, and it is probable that this occurred in ancient times, as will be shown later. However, this does not explain why they are arranged to start at the equinoxes, unless there is some hidden scientific reason for doing so.

As Guerin further remarks in the work cited <note>original Latin: "op: cit:"; short for "opere citato"</note> on pages 171 and 172—after providing a table of the animals associated with the 28 Coptic <note>Egyptian</note> mansions, along with the corresponding names of the Arabian and Persian mansions—the name of the 14th Coptic mansion, *Khoritos*, corresponds to the first of the Arabian and Persian mansions and the first Chinese lunar mansion <note>original: "sieu"</note>. This name means the station of the highest elevation of the star Spica in Virgo <note>original Latin: "Spica Virginis"</note>, which occurs at the spring <note>original: "vernal"</note> equinox at midnight. The Arabian and Persian names *Simakool* and *Nazal* share this same meaning. From this, he concludes:

> “It is evident that the Copts, the Arabs, the Persians, and the Chinese, who use the star Spica in Virgo as the equinoctial point in their lunar mansions <note>original: "Nakhsrattras"</note>, show by doing so that they have borrowed these lunar divisions from the Hindus, since the...”

### THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

“...observations of Moyo <note>likely referring to the astronomer Ma Xu or a contemporary of the Jin Dynasty</note>, and not before his time, that is, 346 AD.”

The derivation of the months and the twelve names of the animal cycle from the <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>the lunar mansions of Hindu astrology</note> and <term>sieu</term> <note>the lunar mansions of Chinese astronomy, now usually written as "xiu"</note> is confirmed by the following remarks of Fortia d’Urban <note>Marquis de Fortia d’Urban (1756–1843), a French historian and mathematician</note> in the work cited <note>original Latin: "op: cit:"; short for "opere citato"</note>—Volume 2, page 75:

<margin>x</margin>
> “The Brahmins <note>the priestly caste of India</note> say that when their calendar was regulated, the moon was full. The Chinese also originally regulated their calendar by the full moons. They borrowed the names of their months from a people who regulated them in a similar manner, whether it was the Indians or another nation. This is evident because they have preserved the names of the months, which are only slight alterations of those used by the Indians. Since these names correspond to the same season and the same month, they must have been taken from the same constellations from which the Hindus derived these names. There has been a communication between them, therefore—regardless of where it started—or a common origin for the calendars of the two peoples: the Indians and the Chinese. This is proved by the fact that the three months of winter in the Chinese calendar are *Pehoua*, *Mokue*, and *Pholkuna*. The three winter months of the Indian calendar are *Poucha*, *Mogh*, and *Phalgoun*. Now, these Indian names are taken from the eighth <term>Nakshatra</term>, *Pouchia*; from the tenth, *Makam*; and from the twelfth, *Phalgouni*.”

<margin>x</margin>
<margin>x</margin>
> “Thus, January is called *Tai* in India and *Tybi* in Egypt; February is called *Mokue* in China, and...”

The Chinese month names mentioned above are undoubtedly modern and were introduced more recently than the animal-based names for the months. However, they are ancient in India. In addition to the analogies noted by Fortia d’Urban <note>Jean-Isaac-Antoine-Pascalis de Fortia d'Urban (1773–1843), a French historian who studied the origins of calendars</note>, other similarities exist, such as:

Probably the rest of the months may also be identified. If the date of their introduction into China could be discovered, it would further confirm that Chinese chronological systems originated in India.

There is another very striking similarity between the 28 Hindu asterisms <note>The Nakshatras: lunar mansions in Hindu astrology</note> and their 12 months, and the Chinese 28 *sieu* <note>Xiu: the 28 lunar mansions of Chinese astronomy</note> and their 12 animals.

It should be remembered that both the Chinese and the Hindus anciently began the year at the winter solstice, just as the Chaldeans did. The 24 *Tsie-ki* <note>Jieqi: the 24 solar terms of the Chinese calendar which divide the year into segments based on the sun's position</note> commenced at the winter solstice. (See Gaubil <note>Antoine Gaubil (1689–1759), a Jesuit missionary and leading astronomer in 18th-century China</note>, *Treatise on Chinese Astronomy* <note>original French: “Traite de l’astronomie Chinoise”</note>, page 93.)

-># CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

Schlegel, in the work cited <note>original: *op. cit.*, referring to Gustave Schlegel’s *Uranographie Chinoise* (1875)</note>, page 21, quotes the author of the *Tien-yuen-li-li* (Siu-fa, <term>A.D.</term> 1622), who states: "The ancients certainly began their calendar with the winter solstice."

The Hindu *Nakshatra* <note>A lunar mansion or station of the moon in Indian astronomy</note> called *Pushya*, and the month derived from it, denotes the winter solstice; similarly, the Chinese *sieu* <note>The *xiù*, or "Lunar Mansions," of Chinese astronomy</note> called *Heu* <note>The "Emptiness" mansion, *Xū*</note> also aligns with the winter solstice. Therefore, by starting both series of 28 asterisms with the signs corresponding to the winter solstice and continuing them both in this order, it will be seen that the 12 Chinese animal signs—which correspond to certain of the *sieu*—occur in the exact same order as they are usually mentioned in the 12-year cycle: namely, the Rat as No. 1, and the rest in succession <note>original: *en suite*</note>.

It will be noticed that in the arrangement of the Chinese *sieu* <note>The 28 mansions or "stations" of the moon</note>, the order of placement has been reversed from the way they usually appear. This has been done to align them with the position of the Hindu *Nakshatras*, which move from east to west. Because the Chinese *sieu* are typically placed from west to east, their sequential order had to be transposed to allow for an accurate comparison between the two series.

This similarity between the Hindu and Chinese systems is even more remarkable because it agrees with the astronomical assignment of the twelve animals. The Rat is always positioned at the winter solstice; consequently, the animal cycle begins at the winter solstice, just as the solar year and the twenty-eight lunar mansions do.

It is evident, therefore, that this twelve-year cycle—and the specific sequence in which it occurs—is derived from the Hindus.

The author argues that this evidence proves the Chinese 12-year cycle originated from Hindu astronomical traditions.</summary>

...order, and it is symbolic of the months, counting from the winter solstice.

The twelve animals could not have been selected by chance from the twenty-eight animals that correspond to the twenty-eight *sieu* <term>lunar mansions in Chinese astronomy</term> and arranged in this specific order. It is only by joining and comparing the two twenty-eight-fold systems of the Hindus <note>original: Hindoos</note> and the Chinese, starting from the same solstitial point, that the cyclical order of the twelve animals can be produced.

Another comparison needs to be drawn between the Hindu *Nakshatras* <term>lunar mansions in Indian astronomy</term> and the Chinese *sieu* to show the occurrence of animal symbols in connection with the former. Although this is not exactly similar to the animal representations of the Chinese *sieu*, it is interesting because it demonstrates that the same system prevailed in both. It also shows that the Chinese derived their concept of animals, with reference to the constellations, from the same source from which they obtained their knowledge of the twenty-eight *sieu*—namely, the Hindus.

The following table will show this analogy clearly.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the 28 lunar mansions (Sieu) relate to the 12 signs of the solar zodiac. -># THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.<-

It can be considered nearly certain that the 12 solar zodiac signs used in modern astronomy were derived from the 12 animals selected from the 28 animal symbols of the 28 asterisms <term>groups of stars or constellations</term>. The fact that the names of the 12 months were selected from the names of the 28 asterisms supports this claim.

<margin>X</margin>
The very meaning of the word "zodiac" <note>From the Greek *zodiakos*, meaning "circle of animals."</note> shows that it was originally composed of animals. Signs such as Libra (the Scales), Sagittarius (the Archer), and Aquarius (the Water-Bearer) are not strictly animal signs and must have been introduced later than the primary symbols. Regarding this, a statement by Paravey in *Essay on the Origin, etc.* <note>original: "Essai sur l’origine, &c"</note>, page 36, may shed some light on the question:

> "The primitive alphabet of the Arabs was reorganized 
> and increased by the 28 constellations to 28 letters; 
> 14 of which were radicals <term>root letters</term>, and 14 were servile <term>letters used to form grammatical endings</term>, and we 
> still see among the Arabs 12 letters called 'solar.'"

As there is an evident connection between these 12 "solar letters" among the 28 letters of the Arabic alphabet and the 12 animals among the 28 asterisms, it would appear that these 12 animals were truly the original solar zodiac.

<margin>X</margin>
Paravey also quotes, in the work cited <note>original: "oc : cit :" (opere citato)</note>, Anquetil’s *Zend Avesta*, Volume 1, page 213, in a passage cited by Hager, that the Sanskrit language originally had 28 letters, though it was later increased to 60. This is particularly interesting as it shows how the 60-period cycle was developed from the system of 28, as has already been made clear above. The Chinese seem not to have known the—

<summary>The author argues that the 12-sign zodiac originated from 12 animals selected from the 28 lunar asterisms, citing linguistic parallels in the Arabic and Sanskrit alphabets. This evidence is used to explain how astronomical cycles of 28 were expanded into cycles of 60.</summary>

<keywords>zodiac, asterisms, Paravey, Arabic alphabet, Sanskrit, solar letters, Chinese cycles, Zend Avesta, 60-year cycle</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the origins of the Chinese zodiac. The author is comparing the Chinese system of twelve animals and twenty-eight lunar mansions with the ancient Babylonian (Accadian) and Hindu astronomical systems to determine which civilization is older.</meta>

# CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

...actual signs of the zodiac until very recently. Keeping in view that the zodiac signs are identical to the cycle of twelve animals, the Chinese could hardly have known even about these animals in very ancient times.

The Babylonians, or rather their predecessors the Accadians <term>An ancient Mesopotamian civilization that preceded the Babylonians</term>, named their months after the twelve zodiac signs, just as the Hindus named their twelve months after twelve of the *Nakshattras* <term>Lunar mansions or star clusters in Hindu astrology</term>. See Archibald Sayce, *Babylonian Literature*, page 55. This same author says, "We owe the signs of the zodiac to the Accadians." Therefore, this invention must be ancient. Because the Chinese were unacquainted with these signs at the same time as the Accadians, they cannot be considered to share the same antiquity as that people.

The twelve Accadian names of the months were:

| | |
|---|---|
| Iyar | Marcheshvan |
| Sivan | Kislev |
| Tammuz | Tebet |
| Av | Shevat |
| Elul | Adar |
| Tishrei | Veadar <note>The intercalary or "leap" month in the calendar.</note> |

<margin>See *Records of the Past*, Vol. 7, page 169.</margin>

It would be interesting to discover the meaning of the Accadian month names listed above and whether they use the same symbols as the twelve animals in the Chinese cycle, especially since the Chinese adapted the animal cycle to the naming of the twelve months.

Professor Weber, in his *Indian Studies* <note>original: "Indische Studien"</note>, states that the 28-fold division of the ecliptic <term>The apparent path of the sun across the sky</term> was known and...

...used by the Babylonians; so it is not at all unlikely, but even probable, that they derived the names of their twelve months from their twenty-eight-fold division of the heavens, just as the Hindus did.

It is possible that the primitive names of the twenty-eight divisions of the ecliptic <term>The apparent path of the sun across the sky</term> were not those of animals. Guerin, in the work cited previously <note>original: "op: cit:"</note> at the end, gives drawings of several early Persian, Assyrian, and Babylonian ancient monuments having twenty-eight figures on them in connection with the twelve zodiacal solar signs. These are all human figures, armed like soldiers and supposed to represent the "Hosts of heaven" <note>original: "4 Kings, 23, 5." In modern Bibles, this is 2 Kings 23:5, which mentions those who burned incense to the planets and the host of heaven.</note>. They are evidently also figures of the twenty-eight-fold division of the solar ecliptic.

In Egypt, the twenty-eight constellations—according to Paravey (in the work cited previously, page 31)—are formally traced in the zodiacs of Esna and Dendera with animal heads on human figures. This is exactly as Guerin (page 51) says they appeared in India, and according to Paravey (who quotes Rémusat), in China as well. Guerin (page 224) says: 

"The twenty-eight *Nakshatras* <term>Lunar mansions or star clusters in Hindu astrology; here spelled "Nokhyottsos"</term> are represented by twenty-eight figures in the Isiac Tablet <term>A famous bronze tablet with Egyptian-style imagery, also known as the Bembine Tablet</term>. There, the signs include several animals, such as the ox, lion, scorpion, pig, dog, hare, cock, stag, tortoise, frog, the jackal, the ass, hippopotamus, and ram." 

Therefore, those nations that identify these stars with animal names would have only become acquainted with them later; they then adapted their own ideas of the stars as representatives of animals to the twenty-eight asterisms <term>Small groups of stars that form a recognized pattern</term>.

It is quite certain that the Babylonians divided the sky into four equal parts (see Sayce, page 54), similarly...

...to that which Schlegel says was the basis of the 28-fold division, or four parts of seven each. It also notes that each month of the Babylonians was divided into four parts <margin>X</margin> of seven days each, by which the 7th, 14th, 21st, and 28th days were kept as days of rest. Each day in the series of seven was named after the sun, moon, and five planets, just as each part of the four divisions of the heavens is named by the Chinese after the same <margin>X</margin> planets, and even each day of the twenty-eight days in a similar order. The Babylonians, therefore, had some established theory that twenty-eight periods were the limit of the connection <note>original: "connexion"</note> of the moon with the heavens, as Mr. Proctor <note>Richard A. Proctor (1837–1888) was a famous English astronomer who popularized the study of ancient astronomy.</note> has well observed.

This division of the 28 stations <term>Also known as "lunar mansions," these are 28 sections of the sky along the moon's path</term> of the heavens into four portions, supposed to correspond with the four seasons of the year, is, however, not absolute, and too much stress need not be laid upon it. De Vertus, in the work cited previously <note>original: "op: cit:"</note>, page 10, writes on this subject as follows:

> “The four seasons, in the sense in which we understand the word, have never existed. These definitions, merely applicable to some portions of the earth, cease to be so over the greater part of its surface. The inhabitant of the polar region knows nothing about four seasons, for he is enveloped in a night of six months. The inhabitant of central Africa, burning or drenched on the equator, has no knowledge of them either. The torrid zone <term>The tropical region of the Earth between the Tropic of Cancer and the Tropic of Capricorn</term> has but two seasons, the one dry and the other rainy. The four seasons are the four great upheavals of the earth and of the seas...”

# THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

"...at the four full moons of March, June, September, and December."

Lewis, in the work cited previously <note>original: "op: cit:" (opere citato), referring to Sir George Cornewall Lewis's *An Historical Survey of the Astronomy of the Ancients* (1862)</note> on page 11, writes on this subject:
<margin>X</margin>
> "The division of the four seasons, though of considerable antiquity, is not decisively indicated in nature. The contrast <note>original: "antithesis"</note> between summer and winter is obvious; the revival of nature in spring, after the dormancy <note>original: "torpor"</note> of winter, is also a marked era. But autumn is a less definite season. Hence, a division of the year into three seasons—spring, summer, and winter—is attributed to the ancient Egyptians. The year of the ancient Germans consisted only of these three seasons, according to Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a Roman historian</note>; and in English, the same three seasons are denoted by Anglo-Saxon words, whereas the word 'autumn' is borrowed from the Latin."

It is quite sufficient for the present purpose to have shown that the measurement of the moon's motion through the heavens by 28 periods—which, as we have seen, are the basis of all Chinese chronology—was unknown to them at the ancient time when they were certainly in use by other more ancient nations, such as the Babylonians <note>original: "Babylonioms" (OCR error)</note> and Indians. It would even appear that this 28-fold division of the heavens was known to the Egyptians (see Bailly, in the work cited previously <note>original: "op: cit:" referring to Jean-Sylvain Bailly's *History of Ancient Astronomy*</note>, page 101 <note>original: "CI"</note>), but they seem to have adhered more to the conclusions that other nations had drawn from them in the form of a 12-fold division of the heavens, rather than having retained the original system of astronomy which has still lingered among the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note>, Persians, Arabs, and Chinese.

<summary>The author argues that the 28-period lunar system used in Chinese chronology likely originated from more ancient civilizations like the Babylonians and Indians, noting that even the four-season model is a later cultural development.</summary>

### CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.

James Bonwick, in his book *Egyptian Belief and Modern Thought* (London, 1878), writes on page 331:
<margin>X</margin>
> "The lunar mansions <note>sections of the sky the moon passes through each night</note>, as they are called in astrology, are 28 in number in both Chaldea <note>ancient Babylonia</note> and Egypt. Osiris <note>the Egyptian god of the afterlife</note> is reported to have lived 28 years in the world." 

And on page 233, he adds:

> "Apis <note>original: "Aais" — refers to the sacred bull of Memphis</note>, the bull god, was solemnly put to death when he reached the age of 28 (if he lived that long), because Osiris died after 28 years."

The Coptic people <note>the native Christian population of Egypt</note> certainly maintain this 28-part division of the heavens. Victor Guérin provides a list of them on page 171 of the previously cited work <note>original: "op: cit:" (opere citato)</note>, which he took from the writings of Athanasius Kircher <note>a 17th-century Jesuit scholar</note>. It is remarkable that the 28 Coptic asterisms <note>groups of stars</note> have exactly the same spatial relationship to the Persian and Arabian mansions as the Hindu *Nakshatras* <note>lunar mansions in Indian astronomy</note> have to the Chinese *sieu* <note>or *xiù*, the 28 mansions of the Chinese zodiac</note>. 

Specifically, the 1st Coptic mansion corresponds to the 16th mansion of the Arabs and Persians, and the 14th Coptic mansion is related to the 1st mansion of the Arabs and Persians. This follows the same pattern as the relationship between the Hindu and Chinese systems. The Egyptians seem to have been earlier and more scientific students of this method of dividing the heavens and calculating time than the Chinese. This is because the Chinese adaptation of the 28 asterisms involves a more modern astronomical shift than what is seen in the ancient Hindu and Coptic systems. Furthermore, the Chinese method of arranging these mansions appears to have no scientific basis.

However, a distinction should be drawn between the first knowledge of the 28 *sieu* acquired by the Chinese and their later understanding of the same system...

<summary>This page compares the 28-part "lunar mansion" astronomical systems across ancient Egypt, Chaldea, India, and China, arguing that the Egyptian and Hindu systems are more ancient and scientifically grounded than the Chinese version.</summary>

-># THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.<-

...which has been shown to have come to them from the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note>. Ebenezer Burgess <note>An American missionary and translator of the Surya Siddhanta, an ancient Indian astronomical text</note> demonstrates that this initial knowledge of the 28 *sieu* <note>or *xiù*, the 28 "mansions" or constellations of the Chinese zodiac</note> was not derived from the Hindus, but <margin>x</margin>from another source, as the Hindu and Chinese systems do not align. The knowledge of the 28 asterisms <note>groups of stars</note> moving from west to east must have been part of that original knowledge, of which only a vague and unconscious portion remains in the traditions of the Chinese. It is quite possible, and even probable, that the Chinese received this primary knowledge through the astronomers of Balkh <note>an ancient city and center of learning in modern-day Afghanistan</note>, who had learned it from the Babylonians as part of a comprehensive astronomical system. Whatever its source, the knowledge of the *sieu* and their significance has always remained misunderstood by the Chinese, though they have used it in a creative way to construct an equally imaginary historical timeline.

From the examination and analysis of the Chinese cycles provided above—upon which the entire structure of their chronology is based—we must conclude that the cycles of 60, 28, 12, and 10, as they use them, had no historical existence in China during the early periods to which the Chinese have assigned them. Moreover, the 60-year cycle can be traced back to the Hindu cycle of Jupiter, which is known to have been introduced into China at a much more recent date than the time it is claimed to have originated there. Finally, the cycle of 10 is historically known to have been certainly used by...

<summary>This page argues that China's ancient astronomical cycles and "lunar mansions" were not indigenous inventions but were likely imported from Babylonian and Hindu sources via Central Asian trade routes. The author concludes that the traditional Chinese historical timeline is based on cycles that did not actually exist in the early periods to which they are attributed.</summary>

--- Page 280 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Chinese astronomical cycles—specifically the 12-year and 28-year cycles—were not indigenous inventions but were borrowed from Hindu sources much later than Chinese tradition claims.</meta>

->## CHINESE CHRONOLOGY AND CYCLES.<-

...other nations before reliable Chinese history provides any record of its use in China. Furthermore, any claim made by the Chinese for priority <note>original: "priority," meaning the state of being first in time or importance</note> in this knowledge is critically unsustainable. The cycle of 12 was not used for reckoning years until long after the date assigned by the Chinese for its introduction and use for that purpose. It is actually subsequent to and derived from the cycle of 28, or the 28 *sieu* <note>or *xiù*, the 28 "mansions" or constellations of the Chinese zodiac</note>.

At most, the Chinese themselves confess that they know nothing for certain about the beginning of their knowledge regarding either the 12 or 28-year cycles as represented by animal symbols. Consequently, they can offer no reliable data for the time of their first appearance in China. There is every reason to believe, from the evidence presented above, that both came to China from the Hindus <note>original: "Hindoos"</note> in quite recent times. They were not even understood scientifically by the Chinese until even later periods. 

Therefore, any argument drawn from the superficial acquaintance the Chinese possessed of those cycles cannot establish that they were even capable of devising them—much less that they were anciently intelligent enough to scientifically employ the chronological cycles and tables which they parade before the uneducated as the genuine chronological record of an enlightened antiquity.

The chronology of the Chinese can, therefore, only be considered an elaborate fictitious contrivance <note>original: "contrivance," meaning a clever or artificial plan or invention</note> designed to put into a narrative and historical order the traditions that have filtered through the outskirts of ancient civilized...

<summary>The author concludes that Chinese chronological cycles were borrowed from Hindu astronomy in recent history and that the "ancient" Chinese historical record is a modern fictional construct.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that Chinese history and chronology were retroactively constructed by incorporating the traditions of various migrant groups who settled in the region from Central Asia.</meta>

### THE ORIGIN AND APPLICATION OF CHINESE CYCLES.

Central Asia, toward Chinastan <note>original: "Chinastan," an archaic Persian-derived name for China</note>; where at different periods, outcasts, adventurous military chiefs, and even colonies have found a dwelling place. They brought with them the traditions of their former homes, which their descendants gradually imagined belonged to ancestors who had lived in China throughout the entire duration of those traditions. Thus, they appropriated these traditions for their own glorification and that of their adopted country.

The natural consequence of this would be the necessity of a chronology to give consistency and a plausible appearance <note>original: "speciousness," meaning the quality of appearing to be true or right</note> to the historical narrative. It is at least a credit to Chinese ingenuity that they could devise a system of dates and epochs by which they composed a history and made it accepted as the records of their race, even during periods when they had no national existence.

# THE HISTORY OF THE PROGRESS AND EFFECTS OF THE

Christian religion in the world. Just as it has produced many changes and improvements in human affairs, the tradition of its history was preserved with unusual care. Among the first Christians, who lived at the time of these changes, there were those who were led to record and preserve faithfully what they had seen and heard.

The purpose of this work is to give consistency and unity to the historical narrative, and it is offered with the hope that it will find favor in the eyes of the public.

# APPENDIX.

---

Regarding the importance, or even the inclusion, of the Rat in the Chinese twelve-year animal cycle—and considering the physical insignificance of this little creature—one might assume there is a specific reason for giving it a place in such a highly symbolic series of animals. The following excerpts from authors who have studied this subject are included here for reference and to encourage further investigation into why the Chinese gave the Rat such unusual prominence.

Klaproth, in his *Memoirs Relating to Asia* <note>original: "Memoires relatifs à l’Asie"</note>, Volume 2, page 296, provides an excerpt from Remusat’s *History of the City of Khotan* <note>original: "Histoire de la ville de Khotan"</note>. He presents this as a remarkable connection between the traditions preserved among the peoples of Central Asia and those of the Egyptian priests recorded by Herodotus <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BC) was a Greek historian; he famously recorded a story where mice defeated an invading army by eating their leather equipment.</note>. This connection sheds light on why the Rat is associated with the sacred animals that form the zodiac.

> A large army of Huns (the Xiongnu <note>original: "Hioung Nou"</note>) or Turks invaded the Kingdom of Khotan <note>An ancient Buddhist kingdom located on the silk road in modern-day western China.</note>. The King of that country did not have enough soldiers to resist the enemy. Therefore, he prepared a sacrifice to the rats of the desert and prayed for them to become his allies. That same night, he saw a...

<summary>This page introduces an inquiry into why the Rat is included in the Chinese zodiac, citing a legend from the Kingdom of Khotan where a king prayed to desert rats for military aid.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese zodiac, Rat, Khotan, Xiongnu, Klaproth, Remusat, mythology, animal symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page concludes the legend of the King of Khotan and his rat allies before drawing a parallel with a similar Egyptian tradition regarding King Sethos and his victory over the Assyrians.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

---

> "...large Rat, who said to him, 'You have sought our aid <note>original: "succour"</note>; prepare your troops for battle tomorrow morning, and you will be victorious.' The next morning, the King suddenly attacked the Xiongnu <note>original: "Hioung nou"</note>. Caught by surprise, the Xiongnu tried to mount their horses and put on their armor. However, it turned out that the horses' harnesses, the soldiers' clothes, the bowstrings, and the straps of their armor—in fact, everything made of fabric or thread—had been completely gnawed and shredded by the rats. Consequently, the entire army was taken prisoner. To show his gratitude for such an important service, the King of Khotan built a temple and offered sacrifices. Ever since then, people have continuously made offerings there."

The Egyptian tradition tells a similar story: Sethos, a priest of Vulcan <note>The Roman name for the god Hephaestus; in an Egyptian context, this usually refers to the god Ptah.</note>, succeeded Anysis. Because Sethos had neglected to maintain his army, the soldiers refused to march when Sennacherib, King of the Assyrians and Arabs, invaded Egypt.

Alarmed, Sethos went to the temple of Vulcan to pray for help. He fell asleep and saw the deity in a dream; the god promised him assistance and told him not to be afraid. Sethos then marched to Pelusium <note>An important city on the eastern edge of the Nile Delta.</note> with all the men he could gather. When he arrived, a multitude of field rats swarmed over the enemy's camp. During the night, they gnawed through the bowstrings, the quivers, and the leather straps of the shields, forcing the enemy army to flee. In memory of this event, a stone statue of Sethos was placed in the temple of Vulcan, depicting him *with a rat*

<summary>This page recounts two legends—one from Khotan and one from Egypt—where rats saved a kingdom by destroying the enemy's equipment, explaining the animal's high status in these cultures.</summary>
<keywords>King of Khotan, Xiongnu, Sethos, Vulcan, Ptah, Sennacherib, Pelusium, sacred rats, folklore parallels, Egyptian history</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the rat in ancient history and mythology, concluding the account of King Sethos of Egypt and transitioning to 19th-century observations of rat behavior and symbolic meanings in Tartary, China, and Europe.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

...in his hand, with this inscription: "While beholding me, learn to revere the gods."

With reference to the rat in Tartary, Klaproth, in the work cited <note>original: "op : cit :"</note> on page 299, writes: "During my stay at Irkutsk in 1806, a report was received from the commandant of Okhotsk, which stated that an innumerable troop of rats, having crossed the sea, had not only eaten up all the stores in the government <term>magazines</term> <note>Historical term for storehouses for gunpowder or food supplies.</note>, but that they had even eaten the buildings themselves."

The rat in the Chinese zodiac <note>original: "cycle"</note> is sometimes called the mouse, and this little animal held a place in Turanian mythology. C.F. Keary <note>The OCR reads "Keany," but this refers to the historian Charles Francis Keary.</note>, in *The Dawn of History* (pages 172–173), writes: "The Slavonic dwellers of Hamelin—in the legend of the Pied Piper of Hamelin—held a notion which likens the soul to a mouse. They spoke mythically of the deaths of children as 'the departure of the mice,' perhaps because the mouse hibernates like the sleeping earth. For a similar reason, the beetle was made a symbol of the soul, or of immortality, among the Egyptians."

Was the rat, among the primitive Chinese or Tartars, the symbol of the future state of their ancestors' souls?

James Bonwick, in *Egyptian Belief and Modern Thought* (London, 1878), in the chapter on "Animal Worship," writes: "In the Egyptian scriptures, the worship or solemn appeal <note>original: "adjuration"</note> to the rat took place at Heracleopolis. The <term>ichneumon</term> <note>A type of mongoose-like animal known for eating crocodile eggs.</note> and the rat were feared more than they were honored, as they represented destruction."

<summary>This appendix provides cross-cultural evidence for the rat's significance, from a destructive plague in 19th-century Siberia to its role as a symbol of the soul or destruction in European, Chinese, and Egyptian mythologies.</summary>
<keywords>Klaproth, Okhotsk, Chinese zodiac, Turanian mythology, Pied Piper of Hamelin, C.F. Keary, soul symbolism, James Bonwick, Heracleopolis, ichneumon</keywords>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first part of the study, transitioning from the symbolic mouse in European and Egyptian mythology to the "Ice Rat" (Mammoth) of Chinese lore and its possible connections to Indian astronomical systems.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

Gustave Schlegel, in his *Chinese Uranography* <note>original: "Uranographie Chinoise," a seminal 1875 work on Chinese astronomy.</note> page 768, writes the following about the Rat in the cycle of twelve animals:

"The Emperor Kangxi <note>original: "Kanghi" (reigned 1661–1722)</note>, two centuries ago, gave the following description of the mammoth in his *Physical Observations* (See *Memoirs of the Chinese* <note>original: "Memoires des Chinois"</note>, iv, 481): 'The cold is extreme and almost continuous in the place where the animal called Ping-chou (the Ice Rat) is found. Its shape resembles that of a rat, but it is as large as an elephant. It inhabits dark caverns and constantly avoids the light. An ivory is harvested from it which is as white as that of an elephant. The ancient book, *Chin-y-king* <note>Likely the "Shanhaijing" or Classic of Mountains and Seas.</note>, speaks of this animal in these terms: "There is in the extreme north, amid the snow and ice that covers that country, a rat which weighs as much as one thousand pounds." The *Tse chow-le* calls it the Ping-chou, or Ice Rat, and mentions another species which is no larger than a buffalo.'" <note>This refers to the historical belief in China and Siberia that mammoth remains found in the permafrost belonged to giant, subterranean rodents that died upon contact with light.</note>

A question arises here as to whether the Rat of the Chinese zodiac <note>original: "cycle"</note> was originally the elephant, which represents the 20th and 21st Indian mansions of the lunar cycle of 28.
